% Standard format for the individual parameter guide.
\newif\ifparameterdetail
\newcommand{\parameterentry}[5]{%
%
  \chapter{#1}%
  \section{What the test does}
  #2
  \section{What are normal ranges}
  #3 These are example adult ranges only. The laboratory's own range should be used first. The correct range may change with age, sex, pregnancy, menstrual-cycle timing, altitude, medicines, and the test method.
  \section{What are abnormal results}
  \begin{description}[leftmargin=1.6cm,style=nextline]
    \item[\textnormal{(I) Low values}] #4
    \item[\textnormal{(II) High values}] #5
  \end{description}
  \section{Why the test is important}
  \parameterdetailfalse
\IfSubStr{#1}{Basophils}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
    The basophil percentage is a descriptive result within the white-cell differential, not a stand-alone disease test. Because it is a percentage, it can rise when another white-cell type falls. The absolute basophil count and total white-cell count are therefore important for judging whether the number of basophils is truly increased. Persistent or marked basophilia may support investigation for hypothyroidism or a bone-marrow disorder; the percentage alone does not diagnose the cause. These points are summarized in the \href{https://www.merckmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/basophilic-disorders}{Merck Manual} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/white-blood-count-wbc/}{MedlinePlus WBC guidance}.
    \section{History and FDA status}
    Paul Ehrlich first described human basophils in 1879 while studying stained blood films. He used basic dyes that made the cells' dark granules visible under a microscope; his work also established the idea of counting different types of white blood cells. The basophil percentage is therefore a biological measurement, not a single branded product. The FDA does not approve ``basophil percentage'' as one standalone test and there is no single FDA approval date for it. Instead, the FDA clears or approves specific blood-cell analysers and test systems for particular uses. For example, the FDA cleared the Sysmex XW-100 analyser for a complete blood count with a white-cell differential in 2015 and later cleared a CLIA-waived version in 2017. That clearance applies to that analyser and its stated use, not to every laboratory method or to a diagnosis of allergy or leukemia. See the historical review by Kay (2016), \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}, and the FDA notice on the XW-100 (2017), \href{https://www.fda.gov/news-events/press-announcements/fda-clears-common-blood-cell-count-test-offers-faster-results-patients-and-providers}{FDA clears common blood cell count test}.
    \section{Modern view}
    Modern analysers usually estimate basophils as part of an automated five-part white-cell differential. The percentage is a relative value: it can rise even when the true basophil count has not increased if neutrophils or lymphocytes fall. For this reason, the absolute basophil count, the total white-cell count, analyser flags, and—in selected cases—a microscope review are more informative than the percentage alone. Basophil percentage is useful for describing the blood-cell pattern, but it is not a stand-alone screening test for allergy, thyroid disease, or leukemia.
    \section{What the doctor may do next}
    If the percentage is high, the usual first step is to review the total white-cell count, the absolute basophil count, the other differential counts, and any analyser flag. A repeat CBC or blood-smear examination may be needed when the result is unexpected or persistent. Further evaluation depends on the pattern and on the suspected underlying disorder; the Merck Manual states that treatment is directed at the disorder causing the basophil change.
    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
    Basophil percentage is usually found as part of a complete blood count and often causes no symptoms. When symptoms are present, they usually come from the condition associated with the basophil change. Reported possibilities include:
    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
      \item Itching or other symptoms of an allergic reaction.
      \item Symptoms caused by an underactive thyroid, if hypothyroidism is the cause.
      \item No symptoms at all—the change may be found by chance during a complete blood count done for another reason.
    \end{enumerate}
    These symptoms are not specific to basophilia. The Merck Manual notes that symptoms usually depend on the underlying disorder and that basophilia is often found incidentally. See \href{https://www.merckmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/basophilic-disorders}{Basophilic Disorders}.
  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Eosinophils}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
      The eosinophil percentage is useful for describing the white-cell pattern, but the absolute eosinophil count is the key value for defining and grading eosinophilia. A high percentage can be misleading when another white-cell type is reduced. The result is most useful when it is persistent and agrees with the symptoms, exposure history, medicines, and other blood results. This interpretation is supported by the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/eosinophilic-disorders/eosinophilia}{MSD Manual}.
      \section{History}
      Eosinophils were identified as a distinct stained blood-cell type during Paul Ehrlich's work on blood-cell staining and differential counting in 1879. The historical account is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``eosinophil percentage.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and test systems for stated uses, not the cell type as a standalone product; its general IVD explanation is available at \href{https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/products-and-medical-procedures/in-vitro-diagnostics}{FDA In Vitro Diagnostics}.
      \section{Modern view}
      Modern analysers commonly report eosinophils as part of a five-part white-cell differential. The percentage is a relative value. The absolute eosinophil count is more useful for deciding whether eosinophilia is present and how severe it is. A persistent high count has more clinical importance than a single small change, and the percentage should be read with the total white-cell count and the laboratory's flags. These principles are described in the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/eosinophilic-disorders/eosinophilia}{MSD Manual}.
      \section{What the doctor may do next}
      Follow-up may include a repeat CBC with differential, review of medicines and supplements, questions about travel and possible parasite exposure, and targeted tests based on symptoms. The MSD Manual lists stool testing for parasites and additional tests for organ involvement or a suspected cause; it also notes that the work-up should be directed by the clinical history rather than ordered as one identical panel for everyone.
      \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
      A high eosinophil count may be found without symptoms. When symptoms are present, the clinical history may include:
      \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
        \item wheezing, cough, or other asthma symptoms;
        \item sneezing or nasal symptoms from allergic disease;
        \item an itchy rash or other skin symptoms;
        \item fever, muscle aches, or digestive symptoms after possible parasite exposure; or
        \item fever, weight loss, swollen lymph nodes, or organ-specific symptoms that require evaluation for a more serious cause.
      \end{enumerate}
      These symptoms are not specific to eosinophilia. The MSD Manual emphasizes that mild eosinophilia may have no symptoms and may be discovered during a CBC done for another reason.
    
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Lymphocytes}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
        The lymphocyte percentage is a relative value and should not be used instead of the absolute lymphocyte count. A percentage can rise because another white-cell type has fallen. The result becomes more meaningful when the absolute count, total white-cell count, duration of the change, and clinical history are considered together. The MSD Manual notes that lymphocyte disorders may be temporary or persistent and that blood lymphocytes do not represent every lymphocyte in the body's tissues.
        \section{History}
        The modern white-cell differential developed from blood-film staining and cell classification work, including Paul Ehrlich's methods published in 1879--1880. His staining methods helped distinguish different white-cell types and established the basis of the differential count used today. This history is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``lymphocyte percentage.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and test systems for stated uses.
        \section{Modern view}
        Modern analysers usually report lymphocytes as part of a five-part white-cell differential, but the percentage is not the same as the number of lymphocytes. The absolute lymphocyte count is more useful when deciding whether lymphocytopenia or lymphocytosis is present. A persistent abnormality, an analyser flag, or an abnormal blood smear carries more weight than one small percentage change. These principles are described by \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/leukopenias/lymphocytopenia}{MSD Manual Lymphocytopenia} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential}.
        \section{What the doctor may do next}
        Follow-up may include a repeat CBC with differential and a blood-smear review. If the absolute count remains low and infections or immune problems are present, the MSD Manual describes evaluation of lymphocyte subgroups and immunoglobulin levels. If the absolute count remains high, the clinician may assess the blood smear and the pattern over time before deciding whether specialist testing is needed.
        \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
        A lymphocyte percentage is often measured as part of a CBC and may be abnormal without causing symptoms. Symptoms that may lead to investigation include:
        \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
          \item repeated or unusual infections;
          \item fever or other symptoms of an acute infection;
          \item enlarged lymph nodes or spleen;
          \item mouth ulcers or skin changes; or
          \item symptoms associated with the illness causing the abnormal count.
        \end{enumerate}
        These symptoms are not specific to a lymphocyte disorder. The MSD Manual notes that mild lymphocytopenia may be found by chance and that symptoms often come from the underlying condition.
      
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Monocytes}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
          The monocyte percentage is a relative value and does not replace the absolute monocyte count. It is most useful when read with the total white-cell count, the other differential counts, the duration of the change, and the person's symptoms. Monocyte disorders are often discovered during a CBC done for another reason, and the monocyte change usually does not cause symptoms by itself. This interpretation is supported by the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/monocyte-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
          \section{History}
          Monocytes became identifiable as a separate white-cell group through the blood-film staining and differential-counting methods developed in the nineteenth century. Paul Ehrlich's staining methods in 1879--1880 helped establish the modern classification of white blood cells. This history is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``monocyte percentage.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and test systems for stated uses.
          \section{Modern view}
          Modern analysers commonly report monocytes as part of a five-part white-cell differential. The percentage can change because another white-cell type changes, so the absolute monocyte count is needed to assess true monocytosis or monocytopenia. A persistent abnormality is more important than a single small change. A blood smear may help when the CBC is abnormal or the analyser reports unusual cells; \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-smear/}{MedlinePlus Blood Smear} describes the smear as a way to examine cell number, size, and shape.
          \section{What the doctor may do next}
          Follow-up may include a repeat CBC with differential, review of recent infection and medicines, and a blood-smear examination. Further tests depend on the pattern and may be directed toward infection, inflammation, immune disease, or a blood or bone-marrow disorder. The MSD Manual states that treatment depends on the cause.
          \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
          Monocyte changes usually do not cause symptoms. Symptoms that may lead to the CBC or to further evaluation include:
          \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
            \item fever or other symptoms of infection;
            \item cough or other lung symptoms;
            \item skin symptoms; or
            \item repeated or unusual infections.
          \end{enumerate}
          These symptoms are not specific to monocyte disorders; the underlying illness, not the percentage itself, usually causes symptoms.
        
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Segmented neutrophils}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
            The segmented-neutrophil percentage is a relative value and does not replace the absolute neutrophil count. A percentage may fall because another white-cell type has increased, even when the absolute neutrophil count is adequate. The absolute count is the value used to define and grade neutropenia, and it is more closely related to infection risk than the percentage alone. These points are described by \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/leukopenias/neutropenia}{MSD Manual Neutropenia}.
            \section{History}
            Mature neutrophils became distinguishable as a blood-cell group through nineteenth-century blood-film staining and differential-counting methods. Paul Ehrlich's work published in 1879--1880 helped establish the staining and classification methods used in modern differentials. The historical account is provided by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``segmented neutrophil percentage.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and test systems for stated uses.
            \section{Modern view}
            Modern analysers usually report mature neutrophils as part of a five-part white-cell differential. The percentage describes the balance among white-cell types; it does not directly state how many neutrophils are present. The absolute neutrophil count, repeated results, analyser flags, and—when needed—a blood-smear review are more useful for assessing neutropenia or neutrophilia than the percentage alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-smear/}{MedlinePlus Blood Smear}.
            \section{What the doctor may do next}
            Follow-up may include a repeat CBC with differential and a blood-smear review. If the absolute neutrophil count is low, the MSD Manual describes evaluation for infection and review of medicines, chemotherapy, radiation, nutritional deficiency, autoimmune disease, and bone-marrow problems. Fever with severe neutropenia needs urgent medical assessment.
            \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
            The neutrophil percentage itself usually causes no symptoms. Symptoms that may lead to a CBC or further assessment include:
            \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
              \item fever or chills;
              \item mouth ulcers or sore throat;
              \item cough or difficulty breathing;
              \item abdominal pain or diarrhoea;
              \item burning during urination; or
              \item a new skin infection, redness, swelling, or drainage.
            \end{enumerate}
            These symptoms may indicate infection and are especially important when the absolute neutrophil count is low. The MSD Manual notes that fever may be the only sign of serious infection in neutropenia.
          
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Mentzer Index}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
              The Mentzer Index is useful only as a low-cost screening clue in a person with microcytosis. It may help decide whether iron deficiency or thalassemia trait deserves further testing, but it cannot safely distinguish the two on its own. The published evidence shows that its accuracy varies between populations and that other indices may perform better in some groups. See the original study by Mentzer and later comparative studies in \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4123424/}{PubMed} and \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/17476506/}{PubMed}.
              \section{History}
              W. C. Mentzer described this index in a 1973 Lancet paper titled ``Differentiation of iron deficiency from thalassaemia trait.'' It was designed as a calculation from routine red-cell measurements, rather than as a new laboratory instrument. Because it is a calculation, there is no separate FDA approval date for the Mentzer Index. The FDA regulates specific analysers and laboratory test systems, not every calculation made from their results.
              \section{Modern view}
              The index is calculated as MCV divided by RBC count. A value below about 13 has traditionally favoured thalassemia trait and a value above about 13 has traditionally favoured iron deficiency, but the cutoff is not universal. Coexisting iron deficiency and thalassemia can make the result misleading. Current practice uses the index only as a prompt for confirmatory tests, not as a diagnosis or a reason to start iron treatment without evidence of deficiency.
              \section{What the doctor may do next}
              Follow-up usually focuses on confirming the cause of the small red cells:
              \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                \item repeat or review the CBC, including hemoglobin, MCV, RBC count, and RDW;
                \item perform iron studies, especially ferritin, when iron deficiency is possible;
                \item perform hemoglobin analysis or electrophoresis when thalassemia is possible; and
                \item consider genetic testing in selected cases, especially when alpha-thalassemia is suspected or the diagnosis remains unclear.
              \end{enumerate}
              MedlinePlus describes iron tests and hemoglobin electrophoresis as part of evaluating iron deficiency and thalassemia.
              \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
              The Mentzer Index itself causes no symptoms. The CBC may be ordered when a person has symptoms of anemia, such as:
              \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                \item tiredness;
                \item weakness;
                \item shortness of breath;
                \item pale skin; or
                \item reduced exercise tolerance.
              \end{enumerate}
              Some people with thalassemia trait have no symptoms or only mild anemia. Symptoms are not specific to iron deficiency or thalassemia and require confirmation with appropriate testing. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000587.htm}{MedlinePlus Thalassemia} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus Iron Tests}.
            
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{White blood cell count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                The total WBC count is a broad signal about the immune and blood-forming systems. It can show that the number of white cells is low or high, but it cannot show which type is responsible or identify the disease. A differential count and, when indicated, a blood smear add that information. MedlinePlus states that a WBC count cannot confirm a diagnosis by itself.
                \section{History}
                White blood cells were first observed after the development of microscopy. The modern differential count grew from nineteenth-century blood-film staining and cell-classification methods, including Paul Ehrlich's work in 1879--1880. Automated cell counters later made routine counting faster and more repeatable. This history of blood-cell classification is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``WBC count.'' The FDA regulates specific analysers and test systems for stated uses.
                \section{Modern view}
                Modern CBC analysers count white cells electronically or by light-based methods and may provide an automated differential. Results can be affected by the sample, the analyser method, medicines, smoking, stress, pregnancy, and recent illness. A flagged result or an unexpected result may need confirmation with a repeat CBC or manual blood-smear review. The FDA's 2017 clearance of the Sysmex XW-100 applied to that named CBC analyser and its intended use, not to every WBC method.
                \section{What the doctor may do next}
                Follow-up depends on whether the WBC count is low or high and which cell type is affected:
                \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                  \item review the differential and absolute neutrophil count;
                  \item repeat the CBC when the result is unexpected or may be temporary;
                  \item review medicines, recent infection, smoking, stress, and other relevant history;
                  \item examine a blood smear when the result is persistent or cells look unusual; or
                  \item consider infection testing, immune testing, or bone-marrow evaluation when the clinical pattern requires it.
                \end{enumerate}
                MedlinePlus lists the CBC, blood differential, blood smear, and sometimes a bone-marrow test as possible tests used to investigate an abnormal WBC result.
                \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                A high or low WBC count may cause no symptoms. Symptoms that may lead to a CBC include:
                \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                  \item unusual tiredness or weakness;
                  \item fever or other symptoms of infection;
                  \item unexplained bruising; or
                  \item other symptoms that suggest a blood, immune, or inflammatory problem.
                \end{enumerate}
                These symptoms are not specific to a WBC disorder. A low count can increase infection risk, while symptoms of a high count usually come from the infection, inflammation, medicine effect, or other condition causing it.
              
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Red blood cell count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                  The RBC count is useful for detecting a low or high number of red blood cells, but it does not by itself show how well the blood carries oxygen. Hemoglobin and hematocrit, along with red-cell size and reticulocyte results, are needed to understand the pattern. MedlinePlus states that an abnormal RBC count may be an early sign of illness but is interpreted with other tests.
                  \section{History}
                  Red blood cells were among the first cells seen with early microscopes. Counting became more useful as blood-film methods and calibrated counting instruments developed. Modern CBC analysers measure RBC number and size as part of a group of blood-cell tests. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``RBC count''; FDA regulation applies to specific analysers and test systems for stated uses.
                  \section{Modern view}
                  Modern analysers count red cells and also report related measurements such as hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, MCH, and RDW. The RBC count is affected by plasma water, altitude, pregnancy, smoking, medicines, and disease. A high or low RBC count is therefore interpreted as part of the complete blood count, not as a diagnosis by itself.
                  \section{What the doctor may do next}
                  Follow-up for an abnormal RBC count may include:
                  \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                    \item hemoglobin, hematocrit, and red-cell indices;
                    \item reticulocyte count to assess new red-cell production;
                    \item iron, vitamin B12, and folate tests when anemia is possible;
                    \item blood-smear examination; or
                    \item testing for bleeding, kidney disease, low oxygen, or a bone-marrow disorder when the pattern suggests one of these causes.
                  \end{enumerate}
                  MedlinePlus lists reticulocyte, iron, and vitamin B tests as possible follow-up tests for an abnormal RBC count.
                  \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                  Symptoms that may lead to an RBC count include:
                  \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                    \item tiredness or weakness;
                    \item shortness of breath;
                    \item headache;
                    \item dizziness;
                    \item pale skin;
                    \item abnormal heartbeat; or
                    \item vision problems.
                  \end{enumerate}
                  The first five symptoms are commonly associated with a low RBC count, while headache, dizziness, and vision problems may occur with a high count. The symptoms are not specific to an RBC disorder.
                
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Hemoglobin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Hemoglobin is the iron-containing protein inside red blood cells. It carries oxygen from the lungs to the body's tissues. A hemoglobin measurement is usually part of a complete blood count and helps show whether the blood has enough oxygen-carrying capacity; it does not by itself identify the cause of an abnormal result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/hemoglobin-test/}{MedlinePlus Hemoglobin Test}.
                    \section{History}
                    Hemoglobin was isolated and studied chemically in the nineteenth century; Felix Hoppe-Seyler's work in the 1860s helped establish its identity and its relation to oxygen transport. The historical account is summarized in the NCBI-indexed review of oximetry and hemoglobin research, \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3537215/}{History of blood gas analysis: Oximetry}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``hemoglobin concentration.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and assay systems for stated uses, so an analyser's clearance is not an approval of one universal hemoglobin reference range or of a diagnosis.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Hemoglobin concentration is now commonly measured automatically as part of a CBC, alongside the RBC count, hematocrit, MCV, and other red-cell indices. A low result is commonly used to identify anemia and to estimate its severity, but the pattern of the other CBC results and the clinical context are needed to investigate the cause. A high result can reflect increased red-cell production or reduced plasma water; altitude, smoking, dehydration, medicines, heart or lung disease, and sleep apnea can affect interpretation. These limitations and influences are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/hemoglobin-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/anemia}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the size and persistence of the change and the rest of the CBC. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing hematocrit, RBC count, MCV, MCH, RDW, and the reticulocyte count;
                      \item checking ferritin and other iron studies when iron deficiency or blood loss is possible;
                      \item checking vitamin B12 and folate when a large-cell anemia is possible;
                      \item examining a blood smear or testing for hemoglobin variants when the CBC or family history suggests an inherited disorder; or
                      \item assessing bleeding, kidney disease, inflammation, low oxygen, dehydration, or a bone-marrow disorder when the overall pattern points to one of these causes.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus lists the CBC, reticulocyte, iron, vitamin, and hemoglobin-related tests as possible parts of evaluating anemia; the appropriate follow-up is determined by the individual result and history.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a hemoglobin test include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item tiredness or weakness;
                      \item dizziness or headache;
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item pale skin;
                      \item cold hands and feet;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item reduced ability to exercise; or
                      \item a family history of thalassemia, sickle-cell disease, or another inherited blood disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Some people with anemia have no symptoms, and these symptoms can have causes other than an abnormal hemoglobin result. A high result may also be found during evaluation of symptoms or conditions associated with low oxygen, but the hemoglobin test alone cannot establish that cause. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/hemoglobin-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/anemia}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Mean corpuscular volume}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    MCV means mean corpuscular volume. It measures the average size of red blood cells, in femtolitres (fL). It is one of the red-cell indices in a CBC and helps classify anemia as microcytic (small cells), normocytic (usual-sized cells), or macrocytic (large cells). MCV alone cannot diagnose a disease. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/mcv-mean-corpuscular-volume/}{MedlinePlus MCV Test} and the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/approach-to-the-patient-with-anemia/evaluation-of-anemia}{MSD Manual evaluation of anemia}.
                    \section{History}
                    The idea of using average red-cell volume as a red-cell index developed during nineteenth- and twentieth-century hematology. Maxwell Wintrobe helped establish the practical use of red-cell indices for classifying anemia, and later automated cell counters made measurement and calculation routine. A peer-reviewed history of the CBC describes the development of MCV and the other red-cell indices, \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13040180/}{The evolution of the complete blood count}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``MCV.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and laboratory systems for stated uses.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Most modern CBC analysers determine red-cell size electronically and report MCV with hemoglobin, hematocrit, RBC count, MCH, MCHC, and RDW. An MCV below about 80~fL is commonly called microcytosis and an MCV above about 100~fL macrocytosis in adults, but the laboratory's interval and the person's age matter. A mean value can hide two red-cell populations; RDW, a blood smear, and the rest of the CBC may reveal that pattern. Reticulocytes are larger than mature red cells and can raise MCV. These points are described by \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/approach-to-the-patient-with-anemia/evaluation-of-anemia}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on whether MCV is low, high, or changing over time. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing hemoglobin, hematocrit, RBC count, RDW, and reticulocytes;
                      \item checking ferritin and other iron studies when small cells suggest iron deficiency;
                      \item checking hemoglobin analysis when thalassemia or another hemoglobin disorder is possible;
                      \item checking vitamin B12 and folate, and reviewing alcohol use, liver tests, thyroid tests, and medicines when cells are large; or
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear and considering further evaluation when the abnormality persists or other blood-cell lines are affected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus and the MSD Manual describe these accompanying tests as ways to narrow the cause; MCV itself does not determine which follow-up is needed.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC containing MCV include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item weakness or fatigue;
                      \item headache;
                      \item dizziness;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item pale skin; or
                      \item symptoms of a long-term illness that can be associated with anemia.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MCV abnormalities do not usually cause symptoms themselves. The symptoms above can have many causes, and MCV must be interpreted with the CBC, medical history, and other tests. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/mcv-mean-corpuscular-volume/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Mean corpuscular hemoglobin (MCH)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    MCH means mean corpuscular hemoglobin. It estimates the average amount of hemoglobin in one red blood cell and is reported in picograms per cell. It is one of the red-cell indices in a CBC; it helps describe an anemia pattern but is not a stand-alone diagnosis. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/red-blood-cell-rbc-indices/}{MedlinePlus RBC Indices} and the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/approach-to-the-patient-with-anemia/evaluation-of-anemia}{MSD Manual evaluation of anemia}.
                    \section{History}
                    MCH was developed as one of the red-cell indices used to describe the size and hemoglobin content of red blood cells. Maxwell Myer Wintrobe introduced the practical set of MCV, MCH, and MCHC in 1929. This history is described in the peer-reviewed review \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1995040/}{Maxwell Myer Wintrobe: New History and a New Appreciation}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``MCH.'' The FDA regulates the particular CBC analyser or laboratory system used to produce the result.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    In an automated CBC, MCH is commonly calculated from hemoglobin and the RBC count rather than interpreted alone. A low MCH often accompanies small, pale red cells, especially in iron deficiency, while a high MCH often accompanies large red cells; the MCV, MCHC, RDW, hemoglobin, and smear provide more useful clinical context. The MSD Manual notes that MCH has little clinical significance by itself, so a small isolated change should not be treated as a diagnosis.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up is directed by the hemoglobin and the other red-cell indices. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing MCV, MCHC, RDW, hemoglobin, hematocrit, and RBC count;
                      \item checking ferritin and other iron studies when iron deficiency is possible;
                      \item checking vitamin B12 and folate when large red cells or macrocytosis are present;
                      \item reviewing a reticulocyte count and peripheral blood smear; or
                      \item testing for bleeding, kidney disease, inflammation, thyroid or liver disease, or a hemoglobin disorder when the overall pattern suggests one.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus and the MSD Manual describe red-cell indices, reticulocytes, smear examination, iron, vitamin, and other targeted tests as parts of anemia evaluation; MCH alone does not select the follow-up test.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC containing MCH include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item weakness or fatigue;
                      \item headache;
                      \item dizziness;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item pale skin; or
                      \item cold hands and feet.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MCH itself does not cause symptoms. These symptoms may occur with anemia but are not specific to an abnormal MCH; interpretation requires the complete CBC and the clinical history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/red-blood-cell-rbc-indices/}{MedlinePlus RBC Indices}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration (MCHC)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    MCHC means mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration. It describes how concentrated hemoglobin is inside the red-cell volume and is reported in grams per decilitre (g/dL). It is one of the red-cell indices in a CBC and is used with hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, and the blood smear to describe anemia or an unusual red-cell pattern. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/red-blood-cell-rbc-indices/}{MedlinePlus RBC Indices}.
                    \section{History}
                    MCHC was developed with the other red-cell indices used to describe red-cell size and hemoglobin. Maxwell Myer Wintrobe introduced the practical group of MCV, MCH, and MCHC in 1929. This history is described in \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC1995040/}{Maxwell Myer Wintrobe: New History and a New Appreciation}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``MCHC.'' The FDA regulates the specific analyser or laboratory system used to produce it.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    MCHC is commonly calculated from hemoglobin and hematocrit in an automated CBC. A low MCHC may occur with hypochromic anemia, commonly iron deficiency, while a high MCHC may occur with some red-cell disorders such as hereditary spherocytosis. A very high or unexpected value can also be caused by a sample or analyser problem, including hemolysis, lipemia, icterus, or red-cell agglutination. A peer-reviewed laboratory study describes MCHC as a useful warning signal for analytical interference, so the result should be checked against the specimen, haemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, and smear before it is interpreted as disease.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the MCHC value and the rest of the CBC. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, MCH, RDW, RBC count, and the analyser comments;
                      \item repeating the CBC or testing a fresh sample when the result is unexpected or the specimen is visibly or instrumentally flagged as unsuitable;
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear for spherocytes, agglutination, or other red-cell changes;
                      \item checking reticulocytes, bilirubin, lactate dehydrogenase, and haptoglobin when hemolysis is possible; or
                      \item checking ferritin and other iron studies when a low MCHC occurs with small or pale red cells.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The need for each follow-up test is determined by the complete pattern; an isolated MCHC result does not diagnose hereditary spherocytosis or hemolysis. See the peer-reviewed study on \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6817011/}{interference of in-vitro hemolysis with the CBC}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC containing MCHC include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item tiredness or weakness;
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item dizziness or headache;
                      \item pale skin;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item yellowing of the skin or eyes;
                      \item dark urine; or
                      \item a family history of an inherited red-cell disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MCHC itself does not cause symptoms. These symptoms may occur with anemia or red-cell destruction but have many other possible causes, so they cannot establish what an MCHC result means. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/red-blood-cell-rbc-indices/}{MedlinePlus RBC Indices} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/hemoglobin-test/}{MedlinePlus Hemoglobin Test}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Red-cell distribution width (RDW)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    RDW measures how much the size of a person's red blood cells varies. It is usually reported as part of a CBC and is used with hemoglobin and the other red-cell indices when evaluating anemia. A high RDW means that the red cells differ in size more than expected; RDW does not identify the cause by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/rdw-red-cell-distribution-width/}{MedlinePlus RDW Test}.
                    \section{History}
                    RDW developed from the measurement of red-cell size distributions and the red-cell indices used to classify anemia. Nineteenth- and twentieth-century blood counting and histogram methods made it possible to describe variation in cell size; the NCBI account of red-cell indices identifies RDW as the measure of anisocytosis and records that Wintrobe introduced the main red-cell indices in 1929. See \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK260/}{NCBI Clinical Methods: Red Cell Indices}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``RDW.'' The FDA regulates specific CBC analysers and laboratory systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    RDW is calculated from the distribution of measured red-cell volumes and may be reported as RDW-CV (percent) or RDW-SD (fL), depending on the analyser. A high RDW can support investigation of iron deficiency, vitamin B12 or folate deficiency, mixed red-cell populations, recent blood loss, transfusion, or other conditions, but it is not specific. A normal average cell size can coexist with a high RDW when two populations of different-sized cells are present; this is why MCV, hemoglobin, reticulocytes, and a blood smear matter. These principles are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/rdw-red-cell-distribution-width/}{MedlinePlus} and the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/approach-to-the-patient-with-anemia/evaluation-of-anemia}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the RDW pattern and the rest of the CBC. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, MCH, MCHC, RBC count, and reticulocytes;
                      \item checking ferritin and other iron studies when iron deficiency or blood loss is possible;
                      \item checking vitamin B12 and folate when large or mixed red-cell populations are possible;
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear; or
                      \item evaluating bleeding, transfusion history, kidney or liver disease, inflammation, medicines, or bone-marrow disease when the overall findings point in that direction.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus and the MSD Manual describe the CBC, smear, reticulocyte count, iron, vitamin, and targeted tests as possible parts of anemia evaluation. An RDW result alone does not select treatment.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC containing RDW include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item weakness or fatigue;
                      \item headache;
                      \item dizziness;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item pale skin; or
                      \item cold hands and feet.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    RDW itself does not cause symptoms. These symptoms may occur with anemia but are not specific to an RDW abnormality. A low RDW generally means that red cells are similar in size and is usually not a disease finding; the laboratory's interval and the complete CBC still matter. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/rdw-red-cell-distribution-width/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Platelet count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    A platelet count measures the number of platelets in a blood sample. Platelets are blood components made in the bone marrow that help form a plug to stop bleeding. The count is usually part of a CBC and helps investigate bleeding, clotting, and diseases that affect the bone marrow or blood cells. It does not test whether the platelets work normally. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/platelet-tests/}{MedlinePlus Platelet Tests} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/platelet-disorders}{NHLBI Platelet Disorders}.
                    \section{History}
                    Platelet counting developed from microscopic examination and manual counting of blood cells, followed by automated electronic and optical cell counters. The peer-reviewed history of the CBC describes the progression from manual blood-cell counting to automated cell enumeration, while an early published evaluation documents an automated platelet-counting system. See \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13040180/}{The evolution of the complete blood count} and \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC477265/}{Evaluation of an automatic platelet counting system}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``platelet count.'' FDA regulation applies to specific analysers and test systems for stated uses.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Modern CBC analysers usually count platelets by electrical impedance, optical signals, or related methods. A low count is thrombocytopenia and can increase bleeding risk; a high count is thrombocytosis and may increase the risk of clotting, although the risk depends on the cause and the degree of elevation. A falsely low count can occur when platelets clump in the tube, so an unexpected result may need a smear review or a repeat sample. A platelet count does not measure platelet function; specialised platelet-function tests answer that different question. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/platelet-tests/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/platelet-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI diagnosis of platelet disorders}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the count, symptoms, previous results, and the other CBC values. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC when the result is unexpected or temporary change is possible;
                      \item reviewing a peripheral blood smear for platelet clumping and abnormal blood cells;
                      \item reviewing medicines, recent infection, inflammation, bleeding, pregnancy, and liver or spleen disease;
                      \item checking iron, vitamin B12, or other tests when a reactive increase is possible; or
                      \item considering bone-marrow or genetic testing when a persistent unexplained abnormality suggests a marrow disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    If bleeding or clotting is suspected, platelet-function or other coagulation tests may be needed because the platelet count alone cannot show how well platelets work. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/thrombocythemia-thrombocytosis}{NHLBI thrombocytosis}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a platelet count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item nosebleeds;
                      \item bleeding from the gums;
                      \item bleeding that lasts a long time after a small injury;
                      \item pinpoint red spots or purple patches on the skin;
                      \item heavy or prolonged menstrual bleeding;
                      \item blood in the urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item headache, dizziness, weakness, numbness, or vision or speech changes;
                      \item chest pain or difficulty breathing; or
                      \item pain, swelling, or warmth in a leg.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Many people with a mild platelet abnormality have no symptoms. Chest pain, difficulty breathing, or sudden neurological symptoms can indicate a serious clot and need urgent medical attention. The symptom lists are based on \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/platelet-disorders/symptoms}{NHLBI platelet-disorder guidance}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Mean platelet volume (MPV)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    MPV means mean platelet volume. It measures the average size of platelets, the blood components that help form clots. MPV is usually reported with the platelet count in a CBC. It may provide a clue about platelet production and turnover, but MPV alone cannot diagnose a platelet or bone-marrow disorder. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/mpv-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus MPV Test}.
                    \section{History}
                    MPV became practical as automated blood counters began measuring platelet size as well as platelet number. An early study by Bessman, Gilmer, and Gardner reported that using mean platelet volume could improve detection of platelet disorders; the citation is indexed by the U.S. National Library of Medicine at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/4074887/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``MPV.'' FDA regulation applies to the particular analyser or laboratory system used.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Modern analysers derive MPV from the distribution of platelet volumes. Newly produced platelets are generally larger than older platelets, so a high MPV can occur when the marrow is releasing many new platelets, while a low MPV can occur when platelet production is reduced. The meaning depends strongly on the platelet count, sample age, anticoagulant, analyser, and laboratory method; MPV values from different laboratories are not automatically interchangeable. MedlinePlus states that MPV must be interpreted with platelet counts and other results.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on MPV together with the platelet count and the clinical picture. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing the CBC and the trend in platelet count and MPV;
                      \item repeating the CBC or examining a blood smear when platelet clumping or a sample problem is possible;
                      \item reviewing medicines, recent infection, inflammation, alcohol use, liver or spleen disease, and bleeding or clotting history;
                      \item checking reticulated or immature platelet measures where available; or
                      \item considering bone-marrow, immune, genetic, or platelet-function testing when the persistent pattern suggests a specific disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MPV does not replace platelet-function tests or a specialist evaluation. The follow-up is selected from the platelet count, smear, symptoms, history, and other blood results. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/platelet-tests/}{MedlinePlus Platelet Tests}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC containing MPV include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item bleeding that takes a long time to stop;
                      \item nosebleeds;
                      \item bleeding from the gums;
                      \item easy bruising;
                      \item pinpoint red or purplish spots on the skin;
                      \item heavy or prolonged menstrual bleeding;
                      \item weakness or dizziness;
                      \item persistent headache or dizziness;
                      \item pain or burning in the hands or feet; or
                      \item chest pain or difficulty breathing.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MPV itself does not cause symptoms. These symptoms may reflect bleeding, clotting, anemia, or another illness and are not specific to a high or low MPV. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/mpv-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus MPV Test} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/platelet-disorders/symptoms}{NHLBI platelet-disorder symptoms}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Neutrophil count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The neutrophil count measures the number of neutrophils in blood. Neutrophils are white blood cells that provide an early defense against many bacterial and fungal infections. The clinically important measure is usually the absolute neutrophil count (ANC), not the neutrophil percentage alone. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/leukopenias/neutropenia}{MSD Manual Neutropenia} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential}.
                    \section{History}
                    Neutrophil counting grew from nineteenth-century microscopy, blood-film staining, and manual differential counts. Automated cell counters later made total and differential white-cell counts faster and more reproducible. The history of these developments is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes} and by the peer-reviewed review \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC13040180/}{The evolution of the complete blood count}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``neutrophil count.'' FDA regulation applies to particular analysers and test systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    The ANC is generally obtained from the total WBC count and the neutrophil fraction, sometimes including bands. The MSD Manual describes an adult lower limit of about 1,500 cells per microlitre, with mild neutropenia at 1,000--1,500, moderate neutropenia at 500--1,000, and severe neutropenia below 500; the risk of infection rises as the count falls. Counts can vary over short periods with activity, anxiety, infection, and medicines, so a single borderline result may not describe a person's usual count. A high count can occur with infection, inflammation, stress, smoking, steroids, or a marrow disorder, but the number alone does not identify the cause.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the ANC, fever, duration, medicines, and the other CBC results. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC with differential to confirm the result and assess its trend;
                      \item reviewing medicines, chemotherapy, radiation, recent infections, nutrition, and autoimmune history;
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear when cells are abnormal or the result persists;
                      \item testing for an infection or an immune, nutritional, or inflammatory cause when indicated; or
                      \item considering bone-marrow or genetic testing for persistent, severe, unexplained, or congenital neutropenia.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Fever during severe neutropenia is an urgent clinical situation because serious infection may have few local signs. The MSD Manual and MedlinePlus describe the CBC, differential, smear, and cause-directed evaluation used in this setting.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a neutrophil count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fever or chills;
                      \item sore throat or mouth ulcers;
                      \item cough or difficulty breathing;
                      \item abdominal pain or diarrhoea;
                      \item burning or pain during urination;
                      \item skin redness, swelling, pain, or drainage;
                      \item repeated or unusual infections; or
                      \item symptoms of an inflammatory or blood disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A low neutrophil count may cause no symptoms, and fever may be the only sign of a serious infection. These symptoms are not specific to neutropenia, but fever with a very low ANC requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/leukopenias/neutropenia}{MSD Manual Neutropenia}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Lymphocyte count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The lymphocyte count measures the number of lymphocytes in blood. Lymphocytes include B cells, T cells, and natural-killer cells, which help protect against infection and other threats. The absolute lymphocyte count is more useful than the percentage alone for deciding whether there are too few or too many lymphocytes. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/lymphopenia/diagnosis}{NHLBI Lymphopenia Diagnosis} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/leukopenias/lymphocytopenia}{MSD Manual Lymphocytopenia}.
                    \section{History}
                    Lymphocytes became recognisable as a white-cell group through microscopy, blood-film staining, and differential counting. The development of differential counts is described in the American Society for Microbiology history of leukocyte classification, \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``lymphocyte count.'' FDA regulation applies to the specific analyser or test system used.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    For adults, a commonly cited absolute lymphocyte range is about 1,000--4,800 cells per microlitre, although age and laboratory method matter. The MSD Manual defines adult lymphocytopenia as fewer than 1,000 cells per microlitre; the blood percentage may look normal even when the absolute count is low. A high count may be temporary during infection, while a persistent high count can require evaluation for a lymphoid blood disorder. The blood count represents only a small part of the body's total lymphocyte pool, so it does not describe every lymphocyte in tissues or every immune function.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on whether the low or high absolute count is persistent and on the clinical context. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating a CBC with differential after recovery from an acute illness;
                      \item reviewing medicines, nutrition, recent infections, HIV risk, autoimmune disease, and cancer treatment;
                      \item checking lymphocyte subsets such as CD4, CD8, B cells, and natural-killer cells by flow cytometry;
                      \item checking immunoglobulins or targeted infection tests; or
                      \item examining a blood smear and, in selected persistent cases, lymph nodes or bone marrow.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NHLBI lists CBC with differential, flow cytometry, immunoglobulin tests, infection testing, and sometimes bone-marrow or lymph-node evaluation when a low lymphocyte count needs investigation.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a lymphocyte count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item frequent colds, pneumonia, or other repeated infections;
                      \item unusual infections caused by bacteria, viruses, fungi, or parasites;
                      \item infections that do not heal quickly;
                      \item fever;
                      \item enlarged lymph nodes or spleen;
                      \item sore throat, cough, or other infection symptoms; or
                      \item unexplained weight loss, night sweats, or persistent fatigue.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A low lymphocyte count may cause no symptoms, and these symptoms can result from many illnesses. A high count may also be found during testing for an infection or a blood disorder. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/lymphopenia/diagnosis}{NHLBI} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/lymphocytic-leukocytosis}{MSD Manual Lymphocytic Leukocytosis}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Monocyte count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The monocyte count measures the number of monocytes in blood. Monocytes help respond to infection and clear dead or damaged cells; many later become macrophages after moving into tissues. The count is normally part of a CBC with differential. A monocyte result is interpreted with the total WBC count, the other white-cell types, symptoms, and the trend over time. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/monocyte-disorders}{MSD Manual Monocyte Disorders}.
                    \section{History}
                    Monocytes became recognisable as a separate white-cell group through microscopy, blood-film staining, and differential counting developed during nineteenth-century hematology. Automated analysers later made routine cell counting and differential reporting faster. The history of white-cell classification is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``monocyte count.'' FDA regulation applies to the particular analyser or laboratory system.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A high monocyte count, called monocytosis, may occur with chronic infection, autoimmune or inflammatory disease, blood disorders, or some cancers. A low count, called monocytopenia, may occur with bloodstream infection, chemotherapy, corticosteroids, immunoglobulin treatment, or a bone-marrow disorder. The percentage and absolute count can disagree, so the absolute count and total WBC count are needed. A low or high monocyte count usually does not cause symptoms by itself; symptoms usually come from the condition causing it.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the absolute count, whether it persists, and the other CBC results. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC with differential after a temporary infection or stress response has resolved;
                      \item reviewing medicines, recent and chronic infections, autoimmune disease, inflammatory disease, and family history;
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear for abnormal or immature cells;
                      \item ordering targeted infection, inflammation, or immune tests; or
                      \item considering bone-marrow or genetic testing when a persistent unexplained abnormality or other cytopenias suggest a marrow disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The MSD Manual and MedlinePlus describe CBC review, smear examination, and cause-directed testing as the basis for evaluating monocyte abnormalities. The monocyte count alone does not determine treatment.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a monocyte count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fever or chills;
                      \item cough or difficulty breathing;
                      \item skin redness, swelling, pain, or drainage;
                      \item repeated or unusual infections;
                      \item swollen lymph nodes or an enlarged spleen;
                      \item persistent fatigue or weakness; or
                      \item symptoms of an autoimmune or inflammatory illness.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Monocyte abnormalities often cause no symptoms, and the listed symptoms are not specific to monocyte disease. They may prompt a CBC because of the underlying infection, inflammatory illness, or blood disorder. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/monocyte-disorders}{MSD Manual Monocyte Disorders}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Eosinophil count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The eosinophil count measures the number of eosinophils in blood. Eosinophils participate in allergic inflammation and in responses to some parasites. The absolute eosinophil count is more informative than the percentage alone when deciding whether eosinophilia is present. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/eosinophilic-disorders/eosinophilia}{MSD Manual Eosinophilia} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential}.
                    \section{History}
                    Eosinophils became identifiable as a white-cell group through nineteenth-century microscopy and staining methods. Paul Ehrlich's work helped establish the classification of granulocytes and the differential count; the history is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``eosinophil count.'' FDA regulation applies to specific analysers and test systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Eosinophilia is generally defined as an absolute eosinophil count above 500 cells per microlitre. The MSD Manual classifies 500--1,500 as mild, 1,500--5,000 as moderate, and more than 5,000 as severe. Common causes include allergy, medicine reactions, parasitic infection, autoimmune or inflammatory disease, and some cancers or marrow disorders. A high percentage can occur because another white-cell type is low, so the total WBC count and absolute count are essential.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the level, persistence, symptoms, travel or exposure history, medicines, and other CBC findings. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC with differential to confirm persistence;
                      \item reviewing medicines and recent or chronic allergic symptoms;
                      \item testing stool or blood for parasites when exposure makes this appropriate;
                      \item checking organ-specific, autoimmune, inflammatory, or infection tests; or
                      \item examining a blood smear and considering bone-marrow or molecular testing when the count is marked, persistent, or otherwise unexplained.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The MSD Manual describes the history, physical examination, repeat blood counts, and cause-directed testing used in eosinophilia. The eosinophil count alone cannot distinguish allergy from infection or a marrow disorder.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to an eosinophil count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item wheezing, cough, or difficulty breathing;
                      \item itchy skin, hives, or swelling;
                      \item abdominal pain, diarrhoea, or vomiting;
                      \item fever;
                      \item unexplained weight loss or night sweats;
                      \item rash;
                      \item muscle or joint pain; or
                      \item symptoms after travel, food exposure, or starting a medicine.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Eosinophilia may also be found without symptoms. The listed symptoms are not specific to eosinophilia and may reflect the allergic, infectious, inflammatory, or other condition causing the count to change. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/eosinophilic-disorders/eosinophilia}{MSD Manual Eosinophilia}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Basophil count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The basophil count measures the number of basophils in blood. Basophils are a small group of white blood cells that can release histamine and other mediators during immune and allergic reactions. The count is usually reported as part of a CBC with differential. See \href{https://www.merckmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/basophilic-disorders}{Merck Manual Basophilic Disorders} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential}.
                    \section{History}
                    Basophils became recognisable through microscopy and blood-film staining during the development of differential white-cell counting. Paul Ehrlich's nineteenth-century staining work helped establish the classification of granulocytes; this history is described by the American Society for Microbiology in \href{https://journals.asm.org/doi/10.1128/microbiolspec.mchd-0032-2016}{Paul Ehrlich and the Early History of Granulocytes}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``basophil count.'' FDA regulation applies to specific analysers and test systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Merck describes basophils as less than 3 percent of circulating white cells, or about 0--300 cells per microlitre, but the laboratory's own reference interval must be used. A low count can occur with thyrotoxicosis, acute hypersensitivity reactions, or infections. A high count can occur with hypothyroidism and, when marked, with myeloproliferative neoplasms such as polycythemia vera or myelofibrosis. The absolute count is more informative than the percentage alone, and a single result does not diagnose allergy or a marrow disease.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the absolute count, whether the result persists, and the rest of the CBC. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC with differential when the result is unexpected;
                      \item reviewing allergic symptoms, infection, thyroid disease, medicines, and the previous blood-count trend;
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear when the count is persistent, marked, or accompanied by analyser flags;
                      \item checking thyroid, inflammatory, or infection-related tests when the clinical pattern suggests them; or
                      \item considering bone-marrow or molecular testing when marked persistent basophilia raises concern for a marrow disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The Merck Manual describes treatment as directed at the disorder causing the basophil change. The basophil count alone does not determine treatment.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a basophil count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item itching;
                      \item hives or other rash;
                      \item swelling of the lips, face, or throat;
                      \item wheezing or difficulty breathing;
                      \item fever or other infection symptoms;
                      \item unexplained tiredness or weakness;
                      \item weight change, heat or cold intolerance, or palpitations; or
                      \item symptoms associated with an enlarged spleen or another blood disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Basophil changes are often discovered by chance and the symptoms usually come from the allergic, thyroid, infectious, inflammatory, or marrow disorder causing the change. Sudden throat swelling or severe breathing difficulty is an emergency. See \href{https://www.merckmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/basophilic-disorders}{Merck Manual Basophilic Disorders}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Reticulocyte count}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    A reticulocyte count measures immature red blood cells recently released from the bone marrow. It shows whether the marrow is making new red cells at an appropriate rate. The result is useful in separating anemia caused by reduced production from anemia caused by blood loss or increased red-cell destruction, and it can help monitor treatment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/reticulocyte-count/}{MedlinePlus Reticulocyte Count} and the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/approach-to-the-patient-with-anemia/evaluation-of-anemia}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{History}
                    Reticulocytes were identified through microscopy and staining as immature red cells containing residual RNA. Manual counting was later supplemented by fluorescent and flow-cytometric methods; an early automated analyser study is indexed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2294704/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``reticulocyte count.'' FDA regulation applies to specific reagents, analysers, and test systems for stated uses.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    The result may be reported as a percentage of red cells, an absolute count, or a corrected reticulocyte production measure. The percentage can look high simply because the total RBC count is low, so the absolute count and the hemoglobin level are important. A high count generally indicates increased marrow response, such as after bleeding or during hemolysis; a low count suggests inadequate production from iron or vitamin deficiency, kidney disease, marrow failure, infection, or liver disease. Pregnancy, altitude, medicines, transfusion, and the timing of treatment can affect the result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/reticulocyte-count/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the reticulocyte result and the type and severity of anemia. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing the CBC, hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, RBC count, and absolute reticulocyte count;
                      \item checking ferritin and other iron studies, vitamin B12, and folate;
                      \item examining a peripheral blood smear;
                      \item checking bilirubin, lactate dehydrogenase, haptoglobin, and a direct antiglobulin test when hemolysis is possible;
                      \item assessing bleeding, kidney and liver function, or infection; or
                      \item considering bone-marrow testing when production is persistently low and the cause is not found.
                    \end{enumerate}
