                    The MSD Manual lists the reticulocyte count, smear, iron, vitamin, haemolysis, chemistry, and sometimes marrow tests as parts of anemia evaluation. The result alone does not identify the cause.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a reticulocyte count include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item weakness or fatigue;
                      \item headache;
                      \item dizziness;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item pale skin;
                      \item dark urine or yellowing of the skin or eyes; or
                      \item bleeding or symptoms after a recent blood loss.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A reticulocyte abnormality may be found without symptoms. The listed symptoms can result from anemia, bleeding, hemolysis, or another illness and are not specific to the reticulocyte count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/reticulocyte-count/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Reticulocyte hemoglobin (Ret-He)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Reticulocyte hemoglobin, often reported as Ret-He or RET-He, estimates the hemoglobin in newly produced red blood cells. Because reticulocytes have only recently left the bone marrow, the result gives information about iron available for red-cell production at the time of testing. It is usually obtained from an automated CBC analyser and is not the same as hemoglobin in mature red cells. See the peer-reviewed studies indexed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28299633/}{PubMed} and \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16999719/}{PubMed}.
                    \section{History}
                    Reticulocyte hemoglobin became available as automated analysers began measuring the hemoglobin content of reticulocytes. The parameter is relatively recent compared with hemoglobin, MCV, and MCH; early studies evaluated Ret-He and the comparable CHr measurement as markers of iron-restricted red-cell production. See \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/16999719/}{Brugnara and colleagues} and \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/28299633/}{Toki and colleagues}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``Ret-He.'' FDA regulation applies to the specific analyser and reagent system used.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Ret-He is a near-term marker of how much hemoglobin reticulocytes are making, so it can change sooner than hemoglobin or MCV when iron supply to the marrow changes. A low result supports iron-restricted erythropoiesis, but it is not specific for simple dietary iron deficiency; thalassemia, inflammation, kidney disease, and other causes of anemia may complicate interpretation. Cutoffs are analyser- and population-dependent. Published studies have reported different useful thresholds, so a universal cutoff should not be used. See \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33952394/}{Auerbach, Staffa, and Brugnara} and \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/32043622/}{diagnostic performance of Ret-He}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on Ret-He, hemoglobin, MCV, RDW, inflammation, kidney function, and treatment history. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing the CBC and reticulocyte count;
                      \item checking ferritin, serum iron, TIBC or transferrin, and transferrin saturation;
                      \item checking inflammation, kidney function, vitamin B12, and folate when the pattern is not simple iron deficiency;
                      \item reviewing blood loss, diet, absorption problems, chronic disease, and iron treatment; or
                      \item checking hemoglobin analysis when thalassemia or another hemoglobin disorder is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Ret-He can support diagnosis and monitoring, but it does not replace ferritin and the rest of the clinical evaluation. A high result generally indicates better hemoglobinisation of new red cells or recovery after iron treatment; it does not by itself prove iron overload.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC containing Ret-He include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item tiredness or weakness;
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item headache or dizziness;
                      \item pale skin;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item cold hands and feet;
                      \item reduced ability to exercise; or
                      \item symptoms or history suggesting blood loss, poor iron intake, or poor absorption.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Ret-He itself does not cause symptoms. Iron deficiency can exist before anemia and may have few symptoms; the listed symptoms can also have other causes. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/reticulocyte-count/}{MedlinePlus Reticulocyte Count} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus Iron Tests}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    ESR measures how far red blood cells settle in a vertical tube during a specified time, usually one hour. Inflammation can make red cells form heavier groups that settle faster. ESR is a nonspecific signal: it can support the evaluation or monitoring of inflammation but cannot identify the disease causing it. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/erythrocyte-sedimentation-rate-esr/}{MedlinePlus ESR Test}.
                    \section{History}
                    Edmund Faustyn Biernacki published clinical observations and a measuring method in 1897. Robert Fåhræus studied the reaction in 1918, and Alf Vilhelm Westergren described the method that became the standard in 1921. This history is documented in the peer-reviewed review \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5380769/}{Sesquicentennial of the birth of Edmund Faustyn Biernacki} and the NCBI review \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK557485/}{Erythrocyte Sedimentation Rate}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``ESR.'' The FDA regulates particular analysers and test systems; the Westergren method is a laboratory standardisation issue rather than a disease approval.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A high ESR can occur with infection, autoimmune or inflammatory disease, kidney disease, anemia, and some cancers. A low ESR can occur with polycythemia, sickle-cell disease, high white-cell counts, heart failure, low fibrinogen, or low plasma protein. Age, sex, pregnancy, menstrual cycle, obesity, medicines, and red-cell conditions also affect the result. A normal ESR does not exclude inflammation, and a high ESR does not prove it; CRP is often measured with ESR because it provides complementary information. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/erythrocyte-sedimentation-rate-esr/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK275/}{NCBI Clinical Methods}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the degree of change, symptoms, and the clinical question. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating ESR when the result is unexpected or monitoring over time is needed;
                      \item checking CRP and a CBC, including hemoglobin and red-cell indices;
                      \item checking kidney function, liver tests, infection tests, or autoimmune tests when symptoms suggest them;
                      \item using imaging or other targeted tests to look for the source of inflammation; or
                      \item evaluating anemia, abnormal proteins, or other blood disorders when these may have changed the ESR.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus states that ESR cannot diagnose a specific disease and that additional tests are often needed. Treatment is directed at the underlying condition, not at the ESR number itself.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to an ESR include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item unexplained fever;
                      \item headache;
                      \item unexplained weight loss;
                      \item joint stiffness or pain;
                      \item neck or shoulder pain;
                      \item loss of appetite; or
                      \item anemia or symptoms of anemia.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ESR itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms can arise from infection, autoimmune disease, anemia, cancer, or many other conditions and are not specific to a high or low ESR. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/erythrocyte-sedimentation-rate-esr/}{MedlinePlus ESR Test}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Peripheral blood smear}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    A peripheral blood smear spreads a drop of blood on a glass slide, stains it, and examines the cells under a microscope. It can show the size, shape, colour, maturity, and approximate number of red cells, white cells, and platelets, and it may reveal blood parasites. It is usually interpreted with the CBC rather than used alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-smear/}{MedlinePlus Blood Smear} and \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK263/}{NCBI Clinical Methods}.
                    \section{History}
                    Microscopic blood-film examination predates automated blood counting. James Homer Wright published a rapid differential stain for blood films and malarial parasites in 1902; the original paper is indexed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19971449/}{PubMed}. Wright-type staining and related stains made blood-cell morphology easier to examine, while automated analysers later added rapid numerical counts and flags. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological examination ``peripheral blood smear.'' FDA regulation applies to specific stains, analysers, and laboratory systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    The smear remains useful when a CBC is abnormal, an analyser flags unusual cells, or a parasite or red-cell shape disorder is suspected. It can support recognition of blasts, sickle-shaped cells, spherocytes, fragmented red cells, target cells, abnormal platelets, or parasites, but a smear finding is not automatically a diagnosis. Results can be affected by how the film was made, stained, and read, so the morphology is interpreted with the CBC, symptoms, history, and confirmatory tests. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-smear/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK263/}{NCBI Clinical Methods}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the cells that look abnormal. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC and reviewing the analyser flags;
                      \item checking reticulocytes, iron, vitamin B12, and folate for an anemia pattern;
                      \item checking bilirubin, lactate dehydrogenase, haptoglobin, or a direct antiglobulin test when red-cell destruction is possible;
                      \item using a malaria or other parasite test when exposure and morphology support it;
                      \item performing flow cytometry, molecular testing, or bone-marrow examination when abnormal white cells persist or blasts are suspected; or
                      \item arranging specialist review when the smear suggests a serious blood disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus notes that an abnormal smear may require additional tests; the finding must be confirmed and interpreted in context.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a peripheral blood smear include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue or weakness;
                      \item yellowing of the skin or eyes;
                      \item nosebleeds or other unusual bleeding;
                      \item easy bruising;
                      \item fever;
                      \item bone pain;
                      \item swollen lymph nodes;
                      \item fever, chills, body aches, nausea, or fatigue after travel to an area where malaria is present; or
                      \item an unexplained abnormal CBC.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A smear itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms are not specific to a smear finding; they are reasons to investigate possible blood, marrow, infection, or parasite disease. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-smear/}{MedlinePlus Blood Smear}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Immature granulocyte percentage}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The immature granulocyte percentage (IG\%) estimates the proportion of white cells that are immature granulocytes, such as promyelocytes, myelocytes, and metamyelocytes. These cells are released from the bone marrow before full maturation. IG\% can provide an additional signal of marrow response, but it cannot diagnose infection or sepsis by itself. See the peer-reviewed studies on automated measurement at \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3289863/}{PMC} and \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/14608908/}{PubMed}.
                    \section{History}
                    Immature granulocytes were traditionally recognised and counted by examining stained blood films. Automated analysers later made it possible to estimate IG\% and the absolute immature-granulocyte count from the same blood sample as the CBC. Early studies evaluated these automated measurements as possible markers of bacterial infection and sepsis. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``IG\%.'' FDA regulation applies to the particular analyser and reagent system used.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A low IG\% is common and generally does not prove that infection is absent. A raised IG\% can occur when the marrow responds to infection, inflammation, severe stress, bleeding, or recovery from neutropenia. Research has reported different cutoffs and diagnostic performance in different populations; therefore a value such as 3\% is not a universal diagnostic threshold. Newborns have different reference patterns from adults and older children. IG\% is best treated as an auxiliary CBC result interpreted with symptoms, examination, WBC count, ANC, CRP, procalcitonin, cultures, and the clinical setting. See \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/30806482/}{PubMed research on IG\% and sepsis}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the IG\%, the total WBC count, ANC, age, symptoms, and concern for infection. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the CBC with differential when the result is unexpected;
                      \item reviewing the blood film or analyser flags for immature or abnormal cells;
                      \item checking CRP, procalcitonin, blood cultures, urine testing, or imaging when infection is suspected;
                      \item reviewing recent surgery, injury, medicines, chemotherapy, and recovery from neutropenia; or
                      \item considering marrow evaluation when immature cells persist without an explained reactive cause.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The studies cited above show that IG\% can add information but has limited sensitivity and variable cutoffs. A low or high IG\% should not delay urgent assessment when symptoms suggest serious infection.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a CBC reporting IG\% include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fever or chills;
                      \item fast breathing or difficulty breathing;
                      \item fast heartbeat;
                      \item confusion or unusual sleepiness;
                      \item severe weakness;
                      \item new pain, redness, swelling, or drainage suggesting infection;
                      \item painful urination; or
                      \item symptoms after surgery, injury, chemotherapy, or severe inflammation.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    IG\% itself causes no symptoms. The listed symptoms are not specific to an immature-granulocyte abnormality, but fever, confusion, breathing difficulty, or marked deterioration can indicate a medical emergency and require prompt assessment. See the infection-focused studies at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23398965/}{PubMed} and \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24798344/}{PubMed}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Prothrombin time (PT)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Prothrombin time measures how many seconds plasma takes to form a clot after specific reagents are added. It examines part of the clotting system and is commonly reported with the INR, which helps compare results from different laboratories. PT/INR is used to monitor warfarin, investigate unusual bleeding or clotting, assess vitamin K status, and provide information about the liver's ability to make several clotting factors. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus PT/INR Test} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI Bleeding-Disorder Diagnosis}.
                    \section{History}
                    Armand J. Quick and colleagues introduced the prothrombin-time technique in 1935. Differences between thromboplastin reagents led to the later development of the INR system to improve comparability when monitoring vitamin-K-antagonist treatment. This history is documented in the NLM-indexed review \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6987933/}{Armand J. Quick: pioneer and prophet of coagulation research} and the review \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3222258/}{The progress of prothrombin time measurement}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``PT.'' FDA regulation applies to particular reagents, analysers, and point-of-care systems for stated uses.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A prolonged PT or INR means that clot formation took longer than expected. Causes can include warfarin, vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, a clotting-factor deficiency, or consumptive coagulopathy. A low PT/INR in a person taking warfarin may mean inadequate anticoagulation and greater clot risk; in a person not taking warfarin, a shortened PT is usually less specific. PT does not assess every clotting factor, platelet function, or every anticoagulant effect, so it is interpreted with aPTT, platelet count, fibrinogen, liver tests, medicines, and clinical history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hepatic-and-biliary-disorders/testing-for-hepatic-and-biliary-disorders/laboratory-tests-of-the-liver-and-gallbladder}{MSD Manual liver testing}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on whether the person takes warfarin, whether bleeding or clotting is present, and whether PT or INR is abnormal. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating PT/INR and checking the sample, dose history, medicines, supplements, alcohol, and diet;
                      \item measuring aPTT, platelet count, fibrinogen, and D-dimer to assess other parts of hemostasis;
                      \item checking liver tests and vitamin K deficiency when synthesis or absorption is a concern;
                      \item performing clotting-factor assays or a mixing study when a factor deficiency or inhibitor is possible; or
                      \item assessing for thrombosis or bleeding with imaging or other targeted tests when symptoms require it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Do not stop or change warfarin or another anticoagulant solely because of a laboratory result; medication changes require the prescribing clinician's instructions. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to PT/INR testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item unexplained heavy bleeding;
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item unusually heavy nosebleeds;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item leg pain or tenderness;
                      \item leg swelling, redness, or warmth;
                      \item difficulty breathing or cough;
                      \item chest pain; or
                      \item rapid heartbeat or rapid breathing.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    PT/INR itself causes no symptoms. The listed symptoms can indicate bleeding or a blood clot but have many possible causes. Severe bleeding, chest pain, or sudden breathing difficulty needs urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus PT/INR Test}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{International normalized ratio (INR)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    INR is a standardized way of reporting the prothrombin-time result. It is calculated from the patient's PT, a laboratory reference PT, and the sensitivity of the thromboplastin reagent. INR is mainly used to monitor vitamin-K-antagonist treatment such as warfarin; it can also help interpret unexplained bleeding, clotting, or liver dysfunction. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus PT/INR Test}.
                    \section{History}
                    The World Health Organization introduced the INR system in the early 1980s to reduce the effect of differences among thromboplastin reagents when monitoring oral anticoagulant therapy. The historical development and standardisation are described in the NLM-indexed review \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/9260421/}{Using the international normalized ratio to standardize prothrombin time} and the WHO-related account at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/6879511/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological calculation ``INR.'' FDA regulation applies to the specific PT/INR analyser, reagent, or home-testing device.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    In a person taking warfarin, a low INR means the blood is clotting more readily than intended and may indicate inadequate anticoagulation; a high INR means clotting is slower and bleeding risk is higher. The intended range depends on the reason for treatment and is not the same for every patient. In people not taking warfarin, an abnormal INR may reflect vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, a bleeding disorder, or consumptive coagulopathy. INR does not measure platelet function and is not a complete test for every anticoagulant. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hepatic-and-biliary-disorders/testing-for-hepatic-and-biliary-disorders/laboratory-tests-of-the-liver-and-gallbladder}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the INR, the reason for testing, medicines, and symptoms. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the PT/INR and reviewing warfarin dose, missed doses, new medicines, supplements, alcohol, illness, and diet;
                      \item checking aPTT, platelet count, fibrinogen, and D-dimer when a broader clotting problem is possible;
                      \item checking liver tests and vitamin K status when the person is not taking warfarin;
                      \item performing a mixing study or clotting-factor assays when a factor deficiency or inhibitor is suspected; or
                      \item assessing for bleeding or thrombosis with urgent examination and imaging when symptoms require it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    INR targets and anticoagulant doses must be individualized. Do not change or stop warfarin without instructions from the prescribing clinician. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/druginfo/meds/a682277.html}{MedlinePlus Warfarin Information}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to INR testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item unexplained heavy bleeding;
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item nosebleeds or bleeding from the gums;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item blood in urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item black or tarry stool;
                      \item leg pain, swelling, redness, or warmth;
                      \item difficulty breathing or cough;
                      \item chest pain; or
                      \item rapid heartbeat or breathing.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    INR itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms may reflect bleeding or a clot and have other possible causes. Severe bleeding, chest pain, sudden breathing difficulty, or sudden neurological symptoms requires urgent medical attention. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prothrombin-time-test-and-inr-ptinr/}{MedlinePlus PT/INR Test}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT) measures, in seconds, how long a plasma sample takes to form a clot after laboratory reagents are added. It is sensitive to several clotting factors in the intrinsic and common pathways. It is often ordered with PT/INR when a bleeding or clotting problem is being evaluated, and it can be used to monitor unfractionated heparin in appropriate settings. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/partial-thromboplastin-time-ptt-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    The partial thromboplastin time was described by Langdell, Wagner, and Brinkhous in 1953. Later laboratory methods added an activator, producing the activated partial thromboplastin time. This history is summarized in the NLM-indexed review \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2180278/}{Historical account of tests of hemostasis}, and the original 1953 report is indexed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13045017/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``aPTT''; FDA authorization applies to specific analysers, reagents, and heparin-monitoring systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A prolonged aPTT means that clot formation took longer under the test conditions; it does not by itself identify the cause or predict bleeding. Causes include heparin, deficiency of factors such as VIII, IX, or XI, an inhibitor such as lupus anticoagulant, severe liver disease, or disseminated intravascular coagulation. Lupus anticoagulant can prolong the aPTT while being associated with thrombosis rather than bleeding. A short aPTT is usually nonspecific and should be interpreted with the laboratory's method, the clinical setting, and other results. The test does not measure platelet function and is not a complete screen for every anticoagulant. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/partial-thromboplastin-time-ptt-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the result, medicines, symptoms, and reason for testing. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the aPTT and checking the blood sample, collection tube, and medication history;
                      \item reviewing PT/INR, platelet count, fibrinogen, liver tests, and other CBC results;
                      \item performing a mixing study to help distinguish a missing clotting factor from an inhibitor;
                      \item measuring factor VIII, IX, XI, or other factors when a deficiency is suspected;
                      \item testing for lupus anticoagulant or related antiphospholipid antibodies when clinically appropriate; or
                      \item using an anti-Xa assay, thrombin time, or another test when heparin or a direct anticoagulant may affect interpretation.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The follow-up test must match the anticoagulant and the clinical question; treatment should not be started, stopped, or changed from an aPTT result alone. The NHLBI describes PT, PTT, platelet count, and sometimes fibrinogen or thrombin time as initial tests in suspected bleeding disorders. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health-topics/diagnosis-evaluation-and-management-of-von-willebrand-disease/von-willebrand-disease-full-report}{NHLBI guidance}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to aPTT testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item frequent or prolonged nosebleeds;
                      \item bleeding from the gums;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item prolonged bleeding after a cut, dental work, surgery, or injury;
                      \item blood in urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item painful or swollen joints, especially with a personal or family history of hemophilia;
                      \item a new blood clot or repeated pregnancy loss when an antiphospholipid disorder is being considered; or
                      \item monitoring while receiving unfractionated heparin.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The aPTT itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms have many possible causes. Severe bleeding, chest pain, sudden breathing difficulty, or sudden neurological symptoms requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Fibrinogen}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Fibrinogen is a liver-made blood protein that is converted into fibrin, the mesh that strengthens a blood clot. A fibrinogen blood test measures the amount or clot-forming activity of this protein. It may be ordered when there is unusual bleeding, suspected consumption of clotting factors, severe liver disease, or a need to assess clotting before or during major illness. See \href{https://www.medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003650.htm}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    The widely used Clauss clotting method for fibrinogen measurement was published by A. Clauss in 1957. The original article is indexed in PubMed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/13434757/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``fibrinogen''; FDA regulation applies to particular reagents, analysers, and assay systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly quoted adult reference interval is 200--400 mg/dL (2.0--4.0 g/L), but the laboratory's method and interval control interpretation. Low fibrinogen can reduce clot strength and occur with disseminated intravascular coagulation, severe liver disease, major bleeding, dilution, or an inherited deficiency. High fibrinogen can occur as an acute-phase response to inflammation, infection, injury, pregnancy, or other physiologic states; it is not a specific diagnosis. Clauss and PT-derived methods can disagree, especially in some abnormal samples, so method and clinical context matter. See \href{https://www.medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003650.htm}{MedlinePlus} and the assay-comparison study at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/12083508/}{PubMed}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the result and the clinical situation. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating fibrinogen when the sample, timing, or result is uncertain;
                      \item checking PT/INR, aPTT, platelet count, D-dimer, thrombin time, and a CBC when a broader clotting or consumption problem is possible;
                      \item checking liver tests when reduced production is suspected;
                      \item measuring fibrinogen activity and antigen when an inherited or qualitative fibrinogen disorder is considered; or
                      \item urgent assessment and treatment for major bleeding, shock, pregnancy-related complications, or severe illness when clinically indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NHLBI lists CBC and platelet count, PT, PTT, and sometimes fibrinogen or thrombin time among initial laboratory tests in suspected bleeding disorders. A fibrinogen value should not be interpreted or treated in isolation. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health-topics/diagnosis-evaluation-and-management-of-von-willebrand-disease/von-willebrand-disease-full-report}{NHLBI guidance}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to fibrinogen testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item unexplained or excessive bleeding;
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item frequent nosebleeds or bleeding from the gums;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item prolonged bleeding after an injury, dental procedure, or surgery;
                      \item blood in urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item repeated pregnancy loss or pregnancy-related bleeding;
                      \item severe infection, major trauma, burns, or suspected disseminated intravascular coagulation; or
                      \item a suspected inherited bleeding disorder or abnormal PT, aPTT, platelet count, or D-dimer.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Fibrinogen itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms have many possible causes. Severe bleeding, fainting, confusion, chest pain, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{D-dimer}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    D-dimer is a small protein fragment released when cross-linked fibrin in a blood clot is broken down. The blood test is used mainly to help assess suspected venous thromboembolism, such as deep-vein thrombosis or pulmonary embolism, in people whose clinical probability is not already high. A negative result can help rule out a clot in the appropriate clinical setting; a positive result does not prove that a clot is present. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/d-dimer-test/}{MedlinePlus}, \href{https://www.cdc.gov/blood-clots/testing-diagnosis/index.html}{CDC}, and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/venous-thromboembolism/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    D-dimer testing developed from laboratory measurement of fibrin degradation products as the biology of fibrin formation and breakdown became understood. Because the test is an assay for a biological fragment rather than a single FDA-defined procedure, there is no one FDA approval date for ``the D-dimer test.'' FDA authorization applies to the specific platform, reagent, and intended use. The CDC and NHLBI describe its modern diagnostic role; see \href{https://www.cdc.gov/blood-clots/testing-diagnosis/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/venous-thromboembolism/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A low or negative result is useful chiefly when the person has a low or intermediate pretest probability of venous thromboembolism and the assay is validated for that use. A high result is nonspecific and can occur with pregnancy, older age, recent surgery, trauma, infection, inflammation, cancer, heart disease, or reduced mobility. Results must be interpreted using the assay's units and cutoff; some clinical protocols use age-adjusted cutoffs in older adults. A D-dimer result cannot show where a clot is or replace imaging when imaging is indicated. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/d-dimer-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.cdc.gov/blood-clots/testing-diagnosis/index.html}{CDC}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on symptoms and the estimated likelihood of a clot. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item no further clot imaging when a validated negative result is obtained in a suitable low-risk setting;
                      \item compression or Doppler ultrasound when a deep-vein clot is suspected;
                      \item CT pulmonary angiography when pulmonary embolism is suspected and imaging is appropriate;
                      \item a ventilation-perfusion scan when CT pulmonary angiography is unsuitable or another approach is chosen;
                      \item repeat or additional blood tests when disseminated intravascular coagulation or another illness is being evaluated; or
                      \item urgent treatment and assessment when symptoms strongly suggest a dangerous clot.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A high D-dimer should not be treated as proof of thrombosis, and a normal result should not override a high clinical suspicion. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/d-dimer-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/venous-thromboembolism/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to D-dimer testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of one leg or arm;
                      \item pain or tenderness in one leg or arm;
                      \item warmth or a change in skin color over a painful or swollen limb;
                      \item unexplained shortness of breath;
                      \item chest pain that may worsen with a deep breath or cough;
                      \item coughing, sometimes with blood;
                      \item a fast or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item dizziness or fainting; or
                      \item unexplained bleeding or widespread bruising when a consumption disorder is being considered.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The D-dimer test itself causes no symptoms. Sudden breathing difficulty, chest pain, fainting, or coughing blood requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/blood-clots/testing-diagnosis/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/007620.htm}{MedlinePlus Medical Encyclopedia}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Thrombin time}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Thrombin time (TT) measures how long a plasma sample takes to form fibrin after a measured amount of thrombin is added. Because the reagent supplies thrombin directly, the result mainly examines the conversion of fibrinogen to fibrin rather than the earlier clotting-factor steps. See \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/mesh/68013918}{NCBI MeSH} and \href{https://ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK265/}{NCBI Clinical Methods}.
                    \section{History}
                    The scientific basis of the test follows the identification of thrombin and fibrinogen: Buchanan recognized thrombin in 1838, Hammarsten purified fibrinogen in 1875, and Arthus identified the need for calcium in 1890. These milestones are described in the NLM-indexed historical review \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/2180278/}{Historical account of tests of hemostasis}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``thrombin time''; FDA authorization applies to a specific reagent and analyser system.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly quoted reference interval is about 14--19 seconds, but the laboratory method and reference interval must be used. A prolonged TT may result from heparin or a direct thrombin inhibitor, low or abnormal fibrinogen, or fibrin/fibrinogen degradation products. A short TT is usually not clinically important. TT is very sensitive to heparin and is therefore not generally the preferred test for monitoring heparin or direct thrombin inhibitors; the assay's sensitivity and the medication history matter. See \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/mesh/68013918}{NCBI MeSH} and the laboratory recommendations at \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6559624/}{PMC}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up depends on the TT, other coagulation results, and medicines. It may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the test and checking for heparin contamination or anticoagulant use;
                      \item measuring fibrinogen concentration and activity;
                      \item checking PT/INR, aPTT, platelet count, D-dimer, and liver tests;
                      \item using reptilase time to help distinguish heparin effect from a fibrinogen problem;
                      \item testing for a direct thrombin inhibitor or other anticoagulant when relevant; or
                      \item evaluating for disseminated intravascular coagulation, severe liver disease, or an inherited fibrinogen disorder when the overall pattern supports it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NHLBI includes fibrinogen or TT as optional tests alongside CBC, platelet count, PT, and PTT in an initial evaluation for some bleeding disorders. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health-topics/diagnosis-evaluation-and-management-of-von-willebrand-disease/von-willebrand-disease-full-report}{NHLBI guidance}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to TT testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item unexplained or excessive bleeding;
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item frequent nosebleeds or bleeding from the gums;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item prolonged bleeding after an injury, dental procedure, or surgery;
                      \item blood in urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item suspected disseminated intravascular coagulation or severe liver disease;
                      \item an unexpectedly prolonged PT or aPTT; or
                      \item monitoring or investigation of heparin or a direct thrombin inhibitor effect.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Thrombin time itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms have many possible causes. Severe bleeding, fainting, confusion, chest pain, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Factor VIII activity}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Factor VIII activity measures how well factor VIII works in plasma. Factor VIII is a clotting protein that helps generate thrombin and is carried and stabilized in the blood by von Willebrand factor. The assay is used mainly to investigate bleeding and to diagnose or monitor hemophilia A. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003678.htm}{MedlinePlus}, \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hemophilia/testing/index.html}{CDC}, and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    Factor VIII testing grew from the laboratory investigation of hemophilia and from clotting assays that compare a patient's plasma with factor-VIII-deficient plasma. The historical record is tied to the recognition that hemophilia A reflects low or abnormal factor VIII; CDC describes the modern screening and factor-testing approach. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``factor VIII activity''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents, analysers, and clinical-use systems. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hemophilia/testing/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/coagulation-factor-tests/}{MedlinePlus Coagulation Factor Tests}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    MedlinePlus gives a common reference value of about 50--200\% of the laboratory control, but the report's own interval and assay type must be used. Very low activity supports hemophilia A, while reduced activity can also occur in severe von Willebrand disease or acquired factor VIII inhibition. High activity is not a diagnosis of hemophilia and can be seen with biologic variation or an acute-phase response; the result must be interpreted with bleeding history, aPTT, von Willebrand testing, and treatment status. Factor VIII is unstable, so poor collection, transport, or processing can produce a falsely low result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003678.htm}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/sites/default/files/publications/08-5832.pdf}{NHLBI guidance}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating factor VIII activity when the result does not fit the clinical picture or specimen handling was poor;
                      \item checking aPTT, PT/INR, CBC, and platelet count;
                      \item measuring von Willebrand factor antigen and activity when von Willebrand disease is possible;
                      \item measuring factor IX or other clotting factors when the pattern suggests another deficiency;
                      \item performing a mixing study and an inhibitor assay when acquired hemophilia or an antibody is suspected; or
                      \item genetic testing of the F8 gene when inherited hemophilia is being evaluated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    CDC notes that factor assays and genetic testing help diagnose hemophilia and that inhibitor testing may be needed in people receiving treatment. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hemophilia/testing/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hemophilia/testing/testing-for-inhibitors-and-hemophilia.html}{CDC inhibitor testing}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to factor VIII testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item prolonged bleeding after a cut, injection, dental procedure, surgery, or injury;
                      \item frequent or prolonged nosebleeds;
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item blood in urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item painful or swollen joints after minor injury or without a clear injury;
                      \item repeated soft-tissue or muscle bleeding;
                      \item a personal or family history of hemophilia or another bleeding disorder; or
                      \item checking response to hemophilia treatment or looking for a factor VIII inhibitor.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Factor VIII activity itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms have many possible causes. Severe bleeding, a painful swollen joint, fainting, or breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hemophilia/testing/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{von Willebrand factor antigen}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Von Willebrand factor antigen (VWF:Ag) measures the amount of von Willebrand factor protein in blood. It does not measure whether the protein works normally. VWF helps platelets attach at an injured blood-vessel wall and carries factor VIII. VWF:Ag is one part of the specific testing used to evaluate von Willebrand disease (VWD). See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/von-willebrand/diagnosis/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    In the 1950s, laboratory studies separated the plasma factor associated with the bleeding disorder later called von Willebrand factor from factor VIII. Immunoassays subsequently allowed measurement of the amount of VWF antigen. This development is described in the NHLBI historical account of VWD at \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health-topics/diagnosis-evaluation-and-management-of-von-willebrand-disease/von-willebrand-disease-full-report}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``VWF antigen''; FDA authorization applies to specific antibody reagents and analyser systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly used reference interval is roughly 50--200\% or IU/dL, but the laboratory's interval, assay, blood group, age, pregnancy, stress, infection, and treatment can affect the result. A low VWF:Ag may support a quantitative VWF deficiency, but it does not by itself diagnose VWD or define its type. A normal or high result does not always exclude VWD because VWF can rise during stress, inflammation, pregnancy, or with other physiologic changes. The amount of VWF must be compared with VWF activity, factor VIII activity, bleeding history, and sometimes multimer testing. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/von-willebrand/diagnosis/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health-topics/diagnosis-evaluation-and-management-of-von-willebrand-disease/von-willebrand-disease-full-report}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating VWF:Ag and related tests because levels vary over time;
                      \item measuring VWF activity, such as ristocetin cofactor or another validated activity assay;
                      \item measuring factor VIII activity;
                      \item checking VWF multimers, collagen-binding activity, or platelet-binding tests when a subtype is suspected; or
                      \item considering genetic testing or referral to a haemostasis specialist in selected cases.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    CDC states that a combination of tests is needed and that repeat testing may be required before an accurate diagnosis. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/von-willebrand/diagnosis/index.html}{CDC}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to VWF:Ag testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item frequent or prolonged nosebleeds;
                      \item easy or unexplained bruising;
                      \item bleeding from the gums;
                      \item unusually heavy menstrual bleeding;
                      \item prolonged bleeding after dental work, surgery, childbirth, or injury;
                      \item blood in urine, stool, or vomit;
                      \item iron-deficiency anemia without an identified cause;
                      \item a personal or family history of excessive bleeding; or
                      \item an abnormal aPTT, factor VIII result, or other bleeding evaluation.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    VWF:Ag itself causes no symptoms. These symptoms have many possible causes. Severe bleeding, fainting, or breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/von-willebrand/diagnosis/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Antithrombin activity}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Antithrombin is a natural blood protein that slows clotting by blocking thrombin and other activated clotting factors. An antithrombin activity test measures how well this protein works; some laboratories also measure its amount. The test is mainly used when an unusual or repeated venous clot suggests a possible inherited or acquired thrombophilia, or when heparin appears less effective than expected. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003661.htm}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/genetics/condition/hereditary-antithrombin-deficiency/}{MedlinePlus Genetics}.
                    \section{History}
                    Antithrombin was studied as a natural inhibitor of thrombin during the development of coagulation science. The first recognized family with inherited antithrombin deficiency and recurrent venous thrombosis was reported in 1965. This history is summarized in \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/8186357/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``antithrombin activity''; FDA authorization applies to specific functional or antigen assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly used activity interval is about 80--120\%, but the laboratory's interval and assay must be used. Low activity can reflect inherited deficiency or acquired loss/consumption, including severe liver disease, nephrotic protein loss, disseminated intravascular coagulation, major illness, or recent heparin treatment. A high activity result generally does not establish a disease and is rarely the main clinical concern. Testing during an acute clot, pregnancy, severe illness, or anticoagulant treatment may not represent the person's baseline and can require repeat testing. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003661.htm}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://onlinelibrary.wiley.com/doi/full/10.1111/j.1751-553X.2011.01307.x}{the laboratory review}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating antithrombin activity after an acute illness or anticoagulant effect has resolved;
                      \item measuring antithrombin antigen to distinguish a low amount from reduced function;
                      \item reviewing liver and kidney tests, urine protein, pregnancy status, and medicines;
                      \item testing for protein C, protein S, factor V Leiden, or a prothrombin gene variant when thrombophilia testing is appropriate;
                      \item investigating an active or previous clot with ultrasound or other imaging; or
                      \item considering genetic counselling or testing when inherited deficiency is strongly suspected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A low antithrombin result alone does not prove inherited deficiency. Timing, treatment, acute illness, and repeat confirmation are important. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/venous-thromboembolism/diagnosis}{NHLBI} and \href{https://www.medlineplus.gov/ency/article/000156.htm}{MedlinePlus DVT information}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to antithrombin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item a blood clot in a deep vein without a clear provoking cause;
                      \item repeated deep-vein thrombosis or pulmonary embolism;
                      \item a blood clot at an unusually young age;
                      \item several close relatives with venous blood clots;
                      \item unexplained clotting during pregnancy or after delivery;
                      \item swelling, pain, warmth, or color change in one leg or arm;
                      \item sudden shortness of breath or chest pain;
                      \item coughing blood, fainting, or a fast heartbeat; or
                      \item unexpectedly poor response to heparin treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Antithrombin activity itself causes no symptoms. Sudden breathing difficulty, chest pain, fainting, or one-sided limb swelling requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/venous-thromboembolism/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Lupus anticoagulant screen}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    A lupus anticoagulant screen looks for antibodies that interfere with phospholipid-dependent clotting tests. Despite its name, lupus anticoagulant is not a test for lupus and does not usually mean that a person is bleeding. The antibodies are one of the three laboratory groups used when antiphospholipid syndrome (APS) is evaluated. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/antiphospholipid-syndrome}{NHLBI}, \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/bleeding-disorders/diagnosis}{NHLBI bleeding-disorder diagnosis}, and the CDC-supported review at \href{https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/39156}{CDC Stacks}.
                    \section{History}
                    Lupus anticoagulant testing developed from observations of biologic false-positive syphilis tests and of circulating anticoagulants in some people with systemic lupus erythematosus. The history of these discoveries and later antiphospholipid testing is reviewed in the CDC-supported article \href{https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/39156}{Antiphospholipid Syndrome Laboratory Testing and Diagnostic Strategies}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological concept ``lupus anticoagulant''; FDA authorization applies to particular screening, mixing, confirmatory, and analyser systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A negative screen means the assay did not detect the relevant interference under its conditions. A positive or abnormal screen is not enough to diagnose APS. The result must be interpreted with confirmatory phospholipid-dependent testing, anticardiolipin and anti-beta-2-glycoprotein I antibodies, and a compatible clinical event. Persistent antibody positivity is important, and anticoagulants can interfere with the test. Lupus anticoagulant can prolong the aPTT while being associated with thrombosis rather than bleeding. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/antiphospholipid-syndrome}{NHLBI} and \href{https://stacks.cdc.gov/view/cdc/39156}{CDC-supported review}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating testing after the appropriate interval to assess persistence;
                      \item completing dilute Russell viper venom time, aPTT-based testing, mixing, and phospholipid-confirmation steps according to the laboratory protocol;
                      \item measuring anticardiolipin and anti-beta-2-glycoprotein I antibodies;
                      \item reviewing anticoagulant treatment and possible assay interference;
                      \item investigating a suspected clot with ultrasound, CT pulmonary angiography, or other imaging; or
                      \item assessing pregnancy complications or an autoimmune condition when the clinical history supports it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NHLBI states that APS diagnosis requires both APS antibodies and a related health problem. A positive laboratory result without a compatible clinical event is not, by itself, APS. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/antiphospholipid-syndrome}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to lupus anticoagulant testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item an unexplained deep-vein thrombosis;
                      \item pulmonary embolism or another unexplained venous clot;
                      \item stroke or another arterial clot at an unexpectedly young age;
                      \item repeated pregnancy loss;
                      \item high blood pressure or other pregnancy complications associated with placental disease;
                      \item an unexplained prolonged aPTT;
                      \item livedo reticularis or a net-like skin color change;
                      \item systemic lupus erythematosus or another autoimmune disease; or
                      \item a close relative with antiphospholipid syndrome.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The screen itself causes no symptoms. Sudden breathing difficulty, chest pain, fainting, new weakness, speech difficulty, or one-sided swelling requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/antiphospholipid-syndrome}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Fasting glucose}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Fasting plasma glucose measures the glucose concentration in blood after at least 8 hours without food or caloric drinks. It measures the glucose level at one point in time and is used to screen for and diagnose diabetes or prediabetes, and to investigate unusually low glucose. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-glucose-test/}{MedlinePlus}, \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/index.html}{CDC}, and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diabetes/overview/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Modern diagnostic use of fasting glucose was standardized through successive expert recommendations. The National Diabetes Data Group published criteria in 1979; in 1997 an international expert committee convened by the American Diabetes Association lowered the fasting-plasma-glucose diagnostic cutoff from 140 to 126 mg/dL. This history is documented by NIDDK at \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/-/media/Files/Strategic-Plans/Diabetes-in-America-3rd-Edition/DIA_Ch01.pdf}{Diabetes in America}. There is no single FDA approval date for ``fasting glucose'' as a biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to specific glucose reagents, analysers, and point-of-care systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    For nonpregnant adults, CDC and NIDDK use these diagnostic categories: 99 mg/dL or below is normal, 100--125 mg/dL is the prediabetes range, and 126 mg/dL or above is in the diabetes range. A diabetes diagnosis usually requires confirmation with a repeat or different abnormal test unless the clinical situation is clear. Pregnancy uses different testing pathways and cutoffs. Low glucose may cause symptoms when the level falls too low, but the clinical meaning depends on symptoms, medicines, timing, and repeat measurement. Acute illness, stress, medicines, and specimen handling can affect glucose. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/index.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diabetes/overview/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating fasting plasma glucose when the result is near or above a diagnostic threshold;
                      \item checking HbA1c, a random plasma glucose, or a 2-hour oral glucose-tolerance test;
                      \item checking ketones, electrolytes, kidney function, or blood gases when severe hyperglycemia or ketoacidosis is possible;
                      \item reviewing meals, alcohol, diabetes medicines, other medicines, illness, and exercise when glucose is low or unexpectedly high;
                      \item using diabetes-related autoantibodies and C-peptide when the type of diabetes is uncertain; or
                      \item arranging pregnancy-specific testing when the person is pregnant.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NIDDK states that the test used for follow-up depends on symptoms, pregnancy, and whether the purpose is screening, diagnosis, or classification of diabetes. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diabetes/overview/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to fasting-glucose testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item increased thirst;
                      \item frequent urination;
                      \item increased hunger;
                      \item unexplained weight loss;
                      \item fatigue or weakness;
                      \item blurred vision;
                      \item slow-healing cuts or recurrent infections;
                      \item sweating, shaking, hunger, or palpitations when low glucose is suspected;
                      \item confusion, seizure, or loss of consciousness; or
                      \item a risk factor such as overweight, high blood pressure, previous gestational diabetes, or a family history of diabetes.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The blood test itself causes no glucose-related symptoms. Severe confusion, seizure, loss of consciousness, vomiting, or rapid/deep breathing requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-glucose-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/index.html}{CDC}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Estimated average glucose (eAG)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Estimated average glucose (eAG) is a value calculated from HbA1c. It expresses the HbA1c result in the same units commonly used by home glucose meters, such as mg/dL, to make a long-term average easier to compare with daily glucose readings. It is not a direct measurement and will not match every individual glucose reading. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK} and \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/prediabetes-a1c-test.html}{CDC}.
                    \section{History}
                    The eAG concept was developed from the international A1C-Derived Average Glucose (ADAG) study, conducted to quantify the relationship between HbA1c and average glucose. The study was published in 2008 and is indexed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/19012527/}{PubMed}; its clinical translation is described at \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2742903/}{PMC}. There is no single FDA approval date for the calculation ``eAG''; FDA regulation applies to the HbA1c assay and any reporting or monitoring system.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    There is no independent normal range for eAG because it is derived from HbA1c. For example, an HbA1c of 5.7\% corresponds to an eAG of about 117 mg/dL, 6\% to about 126 mg/dL, and 7\% to about 154 mg/dL using the commonly used ADAG relationship. eAG is a long-term average, so it does not show glucose spikes, lows, or day-to-day variability. HbA1c-derived eAG may be misleading when red-cell survival or hemoglobin measurement is altered. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item comparing eAG with fasting, random, or home glucose readings;
                      \item checking HbA1c quality and conditions that can make it unreliable;
                      \item using fasting glucose or an oral glucose-tolerance test when HbA1c and glucose disagree;
                      \item reviewing continuous-glucose-monitoring data when glucose variability is important; or
                      \item checking a CBC, reticulocyte count, iron, vitamin B12, folate, or hemoglobin variant testing when altered red-cell survival is suspected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    eAG is an interpretation aid, not a separate diagnostic test. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to eAG reporting with HbA1c include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item increased thirst;
                      \item frequent urination;
                      \item unexplained weight loss;
                      \item fatigue or blurred vision;
                      \item slow-healing wounds or recurrent infections;
                      \item known diabetes requiring monitoring;
                      \item glucose readings that do not match the HbA1c result;
                      \item anemia, recent blood loss, transfusion, or a hemoglobin variant; or
                      \item pregnancy or kidney disease, when HbA1c interpretation may require additional care.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    eAG itself causes no symptoms. Severe hyperglycemia or hypoglycemia symptoms require prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c) measures the percentage of hemoglobin with glucose attached to it. Because red blood cells usually circulate for about 2--3 months, HbA1c reflects average blood glucose over that period, with more influence from recent weeks. It is used to diagnose diabetes and prediabetes and to monitor diabetes. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/prediabetes-a1c-test.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    HbA1c was identified as a glycated hemoglobin fraction in the late twentieth century and was later standardized for clinical use. The International Expert Committee recommended HbA1c as a diagnostic test in 2009 after linking standardized HbA1c values with diabetes complications. The report is available through \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2791773/}{PMC}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``HbA1c''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays, analysers, and point-of-care systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    For nonpregnant adults, CDC and NIDDK classify HbA1c below 5.7\% as the usual normal range, 5.7--6.4\% as prediabetes, and 6.5\% or above as the diabetes range. A diabetes diagnosis usually requires confirmation unless the clinical situation is clear. HbA1c does not require fasting, but it can be inaccurate with anemia, hemoglobin variants, recent blood loss or transfusion, altered red-cell survival, pregnancy, kidney disease, or some other conditions. A treatment target is individualized; below 7\% is common for many adults with diabetes but is not safe or appropriate for everyone. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/prediabetes-a1c-test.html}{CDC} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating HbA1c or confirming diabetes with fasting glucose, random glucose, or an oral glucose-tolerance test;
                      \item reviewing home glucose or continuous-glucose-monitoring results;
                      \item checking for anemia, recent blood loss, transfusion, kidney disease, or a hemoglobin variant when the result does not fit;
                      \item checking urine albumin, kidney function, lipids, blood pressure, and eye or foot health after diabetes is confirmed; or
                      \item individualizing the treatment target and testing interval based on age, complications, medicines, and hypoglycemia risk.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NIDDK notes that HbA1c may not be reliable in specific conditions and that another glucose test may be needed. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/a1c-test}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to HbA1c testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item increased thirst;
                      \item frequent urination;
                      \item increased hunger;
                      \item unexplained weight loss;
                      \item fatigue or weakness;
                      \item blurred vision;
                      \item slow-healing wounds or recurrent infections;
                      \item overweight, high blood pressure, or a family history of diabetes; or
                      \item known diabetes requiring monitoring.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    HbA1c itself causes no symptoms. Severe vomiting, confusion, loss of consciousness, seizure, or rapid/deep breathing requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/prediabetes-a1c-test.html}{CDC}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Additional kidney profile: Urea}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Urea is the main nitrogen-containing waste product formed when the liver breaks down protein. The kidneys normally remove urea into urine. A serum urea test therefore provides information about waste handling, but it is affected by hydration, protein intake, bleeding, catabolism, and liver function and is not a stand-alone kidney-filtration test. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bun-blood-urea-nitrogen/}{MedlinePlus BUN} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Urea was isolated from human urine by Hilaire Rouelle in 1773, and Friedrich Wöhler synthesized it in 1828. These milestones are described in the peer-reviewed historical review \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26913884/}{On the discovery of urea}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``serum urea''; FDA authorization applies to specific chemistry reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Urea may be reported in mmol/L, while BUN reports only the nitrogen portion of the molecule in mg/dL; the units and laboratory interval must be checked before comparison. High urea can result from reduced kidney filtration, dehydration, gastrointestinal bleeding, high protein intake, or increased tissue breakdown. Low urea can occur with low protein intake, malnutrition, severe liver dysfunction, or overhydration. Creatinine, eGFR, electrolytes, urine albumin, and the clinical state are more informative for assessing kidney disease. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bun-blood-urea-nitrogen/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking creatinine and eGFR;
                      \item checking electrolytes, glucose, calcium, phosphorus, and bicarbonate;
                      \item testing urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio or other urine markers;
                      \item reviewing hydration, diet, gastrointestinal bleeding, medicines, and liver tests; or
                      \item using ultrasound or other imaging when obstruction or structural kidney disease is suspected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A urea result alone cannot diagnose kidney failure. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/advanced-search/quick-reference-uacr-gfr}{NIDDK kidney assessment guidance}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to serum urea testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the feet or ankles;
