                      \item reduced or unusually frequent urination;
                      \item blood or persistent foam in the urine;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, poor appetite, or metallic taste;
                      \item fatigue, itching, muscle cramps, or sleep problems;
                      \item dehydration, severe illness, or gastrointestinal bleeding;
                      \item diabetes, high blood pressure, or known kidney disease; or
                      \item monitoring a medicine or condition that may affect kidney function.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The urea result itself causes no symptoms. Markedly reduced urine, severe breathlessness, confusion, or vomiting requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Additional kidney profile: BUN/creatinine ratio}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The BUN-to-creatinine ratio divides blood urea nitrogen by serum creatinine. It can provide limited context about hydration, renal blood flow, protein breakdown, or gastrointestinal bleeding, but it is not a stand-alone kidney diagnosis. Both values must be interpreted in their correct units and with the laboratory's method. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bun-blood-urea-nitrogen/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    This ratio is a calculated clinical-chemistry index derived from two older waste-product measurements. It is not a separate substance measured by one universal assay, so there is no single FDA approval date for the ratio. FDA authorization applies to the specific BUN and creatinine test systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly quoted adult ratio is about 10:1--20:1 when both results use conventional units, but the laboratory and unit convention control interpretation. A high ratio can occur with dehydration, reduced kidney perfusion, gastrointestinal bleeding, high protein intake, or catabolism. A low ratio can occur with low protein intake, severe liver dysfunction, or reduced urea production. Creatinine trend, eGFR, urine albumin, symptoms, and hydration are usually more informative than the ratio alone.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating BUN and creatinine when the result or hydration status is uncertain;
                      \item checking eGFR, electrolytes, urinalysis, and urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio;
                      \item reviewing fluid intake, vomiting, diarrhea, bleeding, diet, exercise, and medicines;
                      \item assessing liver function when urea production may be reduced; or
                      \item urgent evaluation when there is reduced urine, confusion, severe dehydration, or suspected gastrointestinal bleeding.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reduced or unusually frequent urination;
                      \item swelling of the feet, ankles, or face;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, poor appetite, or fatigue;
                      \item thirst, dizziness, or fainting from possible fluid loss;
                      \item black stools or vomiting blood;
                      \item known kidney, heart, or liver disease; or
                      \item monitoring a medicine or condition that can affect kidney function.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Blood urea nitrogen (BUN)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Blood urea nitrogen (BUN) measures the amount of nitrogen in blood that comes from urea. Urea is produced during protein breakdown and is normally removed by the kidneys. BUN is commonly ordered with creatinine and eGFR, but changes in BUN can also reflect hydration, diet, gastrointestinal bleeding, liver function, and catabolism. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bun-blood-urea-nitrogen/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    The analyte urea was isolated by Hilaire Rouelle in 1773, and Wöhler's 1828 synthesis helped establish that an organic compound could be made from inorganic substances. The history is reviewed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/26913884/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for ``BUN'' as a biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to particular chemistry assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly used adult BUN interval is about 7--20 mg/dL, but the laboratory's interval must be used. High BUN may occur with reduced kidney filtration, dehydration, gastrointestinal bleeding, high protein intake, fever, burns, or catabolism. Low BUN may occur with low protein intake, malnutrition, severe liver disease, or overhydration. BUN is less specific than eGFR for filtration and must be interpreted with creatinine, urine findings, and the trend. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bun-blood-urea-nitrogen/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating BUN with creatinine and eGFR;
                      \item calculating the BUN-to-creatinine ratio when hydration, renal perfusion, bleeding, or protein breakdown is being considered;
                      \item checking urinalysis and urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio;
                      \item checking electrolytes, bicarbonate, glucose, and liver tests; or
                      \item assessing hydration, bleeding, obstruction, or acute kidney injury according to symptoms.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    BUN is a clue, not a diagnosis of kidney disease. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/advanced-search/quick-reference-uacr-gfr}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to BUN testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the legs or face;
                      \item reduced urination or blood in the urine;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, poor appetite, or fatigue;
                      \item confusion, itching, muscle cramps, or sleep problems;
                      \item dehydration or severe infection;
                      \item black or bloody stool or suspected gastrointestinal bleeding;
                      \item diabetes, high blood pressure, or known kidney disease; or
                      \item monitoring potentially kidney-toxic medicines.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    BUN itself causes no symptoms. Severe confusion, markedly reduced urine, vomiting, or breathlessness requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/bun-blood-urea-nitrogen/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Creatinine}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum creatinine measures a waste product produced largely by normal muscle energy metabolism. The kidneys filter creatinine, so the result is used with age and sex in an equation to estimate glomerular filtration. Creatinine is useful but is also affected by muscle mass, diet, some medicines, and assay differences. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatinine-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    The Jaffe reaction became a widely used chemical method for creatinine measurement after work by Max Jaffé; the method's history and limitations are reviewed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/3296510/}{PubMed} and \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4421578/}{PMC}. There is no single FDA approval date for ``serum creatinine'' as a biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Common adult serum creatinine intervals are approximately 0.6--1.3 mg/dL, with laboratory, sex, age, and muscle-mass differences. A high result may reflect reduced filtration, acute kidney injury, obstruction, dehydration, or a medication effect. A low result commonly reflects low muscle mass or malnutrition and does not necessarily indicate unusually good filtration. eGFR is generally more informative than creatinine alone, and a rapidly changing creatinine makes eGFR equations less reliable. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatinine-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/advanced-search/quick-reference-uacr-gfr}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating creatinine and reviewing the change over time;
                      \item calculating eGFR and checking urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio;
                      \item checking BUN, electrolytes, bicarbonate, glucose, calcium, and phosphorus;
                      \item reviewing medicines, supplements, recent exercise, diet, hydration, and muscle mass; or
                      \item using kidney ultrasound or other imaging when obstruction or structural disease is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NIDDK notes that creatinine-based eGFR is less reliable with rapidly changing creatinine or extremes of muscle mass and that cystatin C can improve estimation in selected situations. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/advanced-search/quick-reference-uacr-gfr}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to creatinine testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the feet, ankles, or around the eyes;
                      \item reduced urine output or persistent foamy urine;
                      \item blood in the urine or painful urination;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, poor appetite, or fatigue;
                      \item itching, muscle cramps, confusion, or sleep problems;
                      \item dehydration, severe illness, or a possible kidney injury;
                      \item diabetes, high blood pressure, or a family history of kidney disease; or
                      \item monitoring a medicine that may affect kidney function.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Creatinine itself causes no symptoms. Markedly reduced urine, severe breathlessness, confusion, or persistent vomiting requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) is a calculated estimate of how much blood the kidneys filter each minute, usually adjusted to a standard body-surface area. Adult equations commonly use serum creatinine, age, and sex; combined creatinine--cystatin C equations may be more informative in selected cases. eGFR is a key marker of chronic kidney disease but is not a direct measurement of filtration. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/clinical-tools-patient-management/kidney-disease/laboratory-evaluation/estimated-gfr-calculators}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    GFR estimation moved from direct clearance measurements to equations that could be reported with routine creatinine. The MDRD equation was developed in 2006, and the CKD-EPI creatinine equation in 2009; the history and comparison are reviewed at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/24485147/}{PubMed}. Current NIDDK guidance supports equations without a race coefficient and recommends combined creatinine--cystatin C estimation in selected situations. There is no single FDA approval date for ``eGFR'' as a calculation; FDA authorization applies to the underlying assays or software systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    eGFR is commonly categorized as G1 at 90 or above, G2 at 60--89, G3a at 45--59, G3b at 30--44, G4 at 15--29, and G5 below 15 mL/min/1.73 m$^2$. Chronic kidney disease requires persistence for at least 3 months or another marker of kidney damage; one low result can reflect acute kidney injury. eGFR is less reliable with rapidly changing creatinine, extremes of muscle mass or body size, and unusual diet. Urine albumin and eGFR together give a more complete CKD assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/advanced-search/quick-reference-uacr-gfr}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating creatinine and eGFR to establish persistence and trend;
                      \item measuring urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio and performing urinalysis;
                      \item checking blood pressure, glucose, electrolytes, bicarbonate, calcium, phosphorus, and albumin;
                      \item using cystatin C or a combined creatinine--cystatin C equation when eGFR is near a treatment or dosing decision; or
                      \item using measured GFR or kidney imaging in selected complex cases.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    An eGFR number alone does not identify the cause of kidney disease. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/clinical-tools-patient-management/kidney-disease/laboratory-evaluation/estimated-gfr-calculators}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to eGFR reporting include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the feet, ankles, or face;
                      \item reduced urine or persistent foam in the urine;
                      \item blood in the urine;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, poor appetite, or fatigue;
                      \item itching, muscle cramps, confusion, or sleep problems;
                      \item diabetes, high blood pressure, heart disease, or a family history of kidney disease;
                      \item an abnormal creatinine result; or
                      \item monitoring known chronic kidney disease or medicine dosing.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    eGFR itself causes no symptoms. Markedly reduced urine, severe breathlessness, confusion, or persistent vomiting requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Uric acid}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    A serum uric acid test measures urate, the end product of purine breakdown. Urate normally dissolves in blood and is removed mainly through the kidneys. Excess production or reduced removal can raise the concentration and allow crystals to form in joints or the urinary tract. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/uric-acid-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Urea and uric acid were among the early chemical substances studied in urine and blood. The clinical use of urate measurement developed alongside the recognition of gout and urate crystal disease; the modern laboratory test and its uses are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/uric-acid-test/}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``serum uric acid''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Common adult intervals are approximately 3.4--7.0 mg/dL for men and 2.4--6.0 mg/dL for women, but the laboratory interval must be used. High urate can occur with reduced kidney removal, purine-rich intake, alcohol, medicines, chemotherapy-related cell breakdown, or inherited/metabolic conditions. High urate does not prove gout: gout diagnosis generally requires clinical assessment and, when needed, joint-fluid crystal analysis. Low urate may occur with medicines, inherited transport disorders, or increased renal loss and is often less clinically important. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/uric-acid-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item examining an acutely painful swollen joint and considering synovial-fluid crystal analysis;
                      \item checking kidney function, urinalysis, and urine uric acid when stones are suspected;
                      \item reviewing diet, alcohol, medicines, kidney disease, and chemotherapy;
                      \item repeating urate when the result was obtained during an acute gout flare; or
                      \item using kidney imaging when a stone or obstruction is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Serum urate is used with symptoms and other tests; treatment decisions should not be based on the number alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/uric-acid-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to uric-acid testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item sudden severe pain in a joint;
                      \item a red, warm, or swollen joint, especially the big toe;
                      \item repeated attacks of suspected gout;
                      \item flank or back pain with blood in the urine;
                      \item painful urination or suspected kidney stones;
                      \item monitoring during chemotherapy or another treatment that rapidly destroys cells;
                      \item known kidney disease; or
                      \item monitoring urate-lowering treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Uric acid itself causes no symptoms. Severe flank pain, fever with a painful joint, inability to urinate, or severe illness during chemotherapy requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/uric-acid-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Sodium}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum sodium measures the main positively charged electrolyte outside cells. It is chiefly a marker of the relationship between body sodium and body water, and it helps nerves, muscles, and cells function. Sodium is often measured with potassium, chloride, bicarbonate, glucose, kidney tests, and osmolality. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/sodium-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus Electrolyte Panel}.
                    \section{History}
                    Sodium measurement became part of routine chemistry testing as flame photometry and later ion-selective electrodes made electrolyte measurement practical and automated. The current clinical uses and limitations are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/sodium-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}; this is a measurement technology rather than a single FDA-approved biological test. FDA authorization applies to specific sodium reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult reference interval is about 135--145 mmol/L, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low sodium may result from excess water, diuretics, SIADH, heart or liver disease, kidney disease, or sodium loss. High sodium usually indicates too little body water relative to sodium and may occur with water loss or limited access to water. The speed of change and the underlying cause influence neurologic risk; the number alone does not identify the cause. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/sodium-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating sodium and checking glucose and serum osmolality;
                      \item checking urine osmolality and urine sodium;
                      \item reviewing fluid intake, vomiting, diarrhea, sweating, diuretics, and other medicines;
                      \item checking kidney, thyroid, adrenal, heart, and liver status when indicated; or
                      \item urgent controlled correction when severe or rapidly changing abnormality causes neurologic symptoms.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Correction must be medically supervised because overly rapid change can injure the brain. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/sodium-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to sodium testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item weakness or fatigue;
                      \item headache, nausea, or vomiting;
                      \item confusion or unusual behavior;
                      \item muscle twitching or cramps;
                      \item excessive thirst or very little urination;
                      \item seizures or loss of consciousness;
                      \item prolonged vomiting, diarrhea, sweating, or poor fluid intake; or
                      \item use of medicines that affect fluid balance.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe confusion, seizure, or loss of consciousness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/sodium-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Potassium}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum potassium measures an electrolyte needed for cell function and the electrical activity of skeletal and heart muscle. The kidneys, hormones, acid--base status, medicines, and movement of potassium between cells all affect the blood result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus Electrolyte Panel}.
                    \section{History}
                    Potassium measurement became routine with the development of flame photometry and later ion-selective electrode methods for serum electrolytes. Current clinical uses are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific potassium assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult reference interval is about 3.5--5.0 mmol/L, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low potassium may result from diuretics, vomiting, diarrhea, hormonal excess, or kidney losses and can cause muscle weakness or arrhythmia. High potassium may result from kidney failure, medicines, acidosis, tissue breakdown, or a hemolyzed specimen; a false high result must be excluded before treatment when clinically safe. Potassium abnormalities can be dangerous even when symptoms are mild. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating potassium promptly and checking for hemolysis;
                      \item reviewing kidney function, bicarbonate, glucose, magnesium, and medicines;
                      \item obtaining an ECG when potassium is substantially abnormal or symptoms are present;
                      \item measuring urine potassium when kidney loss is being evaluated; or
                      \item urgent treatment and cardiac monitoring for dangerous levels or ECG changes.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Potassium-lowering or potassium-replacement treatment should be guided by repeat testing, kidney function, ECG findings, and clinical urgency. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to potassium testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item muscle weakness or unusual fatigue;
                      \item cramps, twitching, or numbness;
                      \item palpitations or an irregular heartbeat;
                      \item fainting or dizziness;
                      \item vomiting or diarrhea;
                      \item kidney disease or reduced urine;
                      \item use of diuretics, ACE inhibitors, ARBs, or potassium supplements; or
                      \item severe illness, burns, trauma, or suspected tissue breakdown.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe weakness, palpitations, fainting, or chest discomfort requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Chloride}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum chloride measures an electrically negative electrolyte that helps maintain fluid balance and works with sodium and bicarbonate in acid--base regulation. It is usually interpreted as part of an electrolyte or metabolic panel rather than alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Chloride measurement became practical for routine clinical use with automated electrolyte methods, including flame photometry and ion-selective electrodes. MedlinePlus describes its role in electrolyte testing; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific chloride reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult reference interval is about 98--106 mmol/L, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low chloride may accompany vomiting, diuretics, low body salt, or metabolic alkalosis. High chloride may accompany dehydration, saline administration, diarrhea, renal tubular disorders, or metabolic acidosis. Chloride is interpreted with sodium, bicarbonate, potassium, kidney function, and the clinical history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating chloride with a complete electrolyte panel;
                      \item calculating the anion gap and reviewing bicarbonate;
                      \item checking kidney function, glucose, and urine electrolytes;
                      \item reviewing vomiting, diarrhea, fluid therapy, and diuretic use; or
                      \item checking blood gases when a significant acid--base disorder is suspected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Chloride does not identify a diagnosis by itself; the associated sodium, bicarbonate, and anion-gap pattern is more informative. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to chloride testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item prolonged vomiting or diarrhea;
                      \item weakness, fatigue, or confusion;
                      \item excessive thirst or dehydration;
                      \item rapid or deep breathing;
                      \item muscle twitching or cramps;
                      \item kidney, heart, or liver disease;
                      \item use of diuretics or large-volume intravenous saline; or
                      \item an abnormal sodium or bicarbonate result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe confusion, rapid/deep breathing, fainting, or severe dehydration requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Bicarbonate}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum bicarbonate, often reported as total carbon dioxide, estimates the main blood buffer that helps control acid--base balance. It is interpreted with sodium, chloride, potassium, kidney function, and sometimes a blood-gas measurement. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{History}
                    Routine bicarbonate measurement developed with electrolyte and blood-chemistry methods that made serum total carbon dioxide practical to measure. Current use in electrolyte panels and acid--base assessment is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific chemistry assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult reference interval is about 22--29 mmol/L, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low bicarbonate may indicate metabolic acidosis or compensation for respiratory alkalosis; causes include kidney disease, ketoacidosis, lactic acidosis, diarrhea, and some toxins. High bicarbonate may indicate metabolic alkalosis or compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis; causes include vomiting, diuretics, and chronic lung disease. Total CO$_2$ is not identical to a blood-gas bicarbonate value and may be affected by specimen handling. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the chemistry panel and reviewing specimen handling;
                      \item calculating the anion gap;
                      \item measuring arterial or venous blood gases and lactate when urgent acid--base disease is possible;
                      \item checking glucose, ketones, kidney function, electrolytes, and toxic exposures; or
                      \item reviewing vomiting, diarrhea, medicines, and chronic lung or kidney disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The bicarbonate result must be interpreted with pH and carbon dioxide when the acid--base disorder is clinically important. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to bicarbonate testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item rapid or deep breathing;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or abdominal pain;
                      \item weakness, fatigue, or confusion;
                      \item dehydration or prolonged diarrhea;
                      \item kidney disease or diabetes;
                      \item suspected poisoning or severe infection;
                      \item chronic lung disease; or
                      \item an abnormal sodium, chloride, potassium, or anion-gap result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe breathing difficulty, confusion, persistent vomiting, or loss of consciousness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Anion gap}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The anion gap is calculated from measured electrolytes, usually sodium minus chloride plus bicarbonate. It estimates the contribution of unmeasured ions and helps classify metabolic acidosis. It is a calculation, not a separate substance measured directly in blood. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{History}
                    The anion-gap concept developed from applying electroneutrality to routine electrolyte measurements and recognizing that not all charged substances are measured on a standard panel. Its calculation and clinical use are described by \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}; there is no single FDA approval date for the calculation.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common reference interval without potassium is about 8--12 mmol/L, but the interval depends on the analyser, albumin concentration, and local method. A high gap suggests unmeasured acids such as lactate, ketones, retained acids in kidney failure, or toxic substances. A low gap may occur with low albumin, increased unmeasured cations, paraproteins, or analytical error. Albumin correction and the full acid--base pattern may be needed. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating sodium, chloride, and bicarbonate;
                      \item checking albumin and correcting the gap when appropriate;
                      \item measuring blood gases, lactate, ketones, glucose, and kidney function;
                      \item reviewing medicines and possible toxic exposures; or
                      \item checking protein studies when a paraprotein is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The anion gap identifies a pattern and not its cause. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to anion-gap calculation include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item rapid or deep breathing;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or abdominal pain;
                      \item weakness, fatigue, or confusion;
                      \item dehydration or prolonged diarrhea;
                      \item diabetes or suspected ketoacidosis;
                      \item kidney failure or severe infection;
                      \item suspected poisoning; or
                      \item an abnormal bicarbonate or electrolyte result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe breathing difficulty, confusion, persistent vomiting, or loss of consciousness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/nephrology/acid-base-regulation-and-disorders/acid-base-disorders}{MSD Manual}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Ionized calcium}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Ionized calcium measures the free, electrically charged calcium that is immediately available to cells. It is the biologically active part of blood calcium and can be more informative than total calcium when albumin or blood pH is abnormal. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus Electrolyte Panel} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/comprehensive-metabolic-panel-cmp/}{MedlinePlus CMP}.
                    \section{History}
                    Ionized calcium became practical for clinical laboratories with ion-selective electrode technology. Its modern role is described in electrolyte-testing guidance from \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific electrodes, reagents, and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common reference interval is about 1.12--1.32 mmol/L, but the sample must be collected and handled according to the laboratory's requirements. Low ionized calcium may occur with hypoparathyroidism, vitamin D deficiency, kidney disease, severe illness, or alkalosis and can cause neuromuscular irritability. High ionized calcium indicates true hypercalcemia and may occur with hyperparathyroidism, malignancy, or vitamin excess. pH and specimen handling can change the result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating ionized calcium with correct sample handling;
                      \item checking total calcium, albumin, magnesium, phosphate, vitamin D, and PTH;
                      \item checking kidney function and blood gases when pH disturbance is possible;
                      \item obtaining an ECG when calcium is substantially abnormal; or
                      \item evaluating malignancy, parathyroid disease, medicines, or kidney disease when indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A calcium result must be interpreted with symptoms and related mineral tests. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/comprehensive-metabolic-panel-cmp/}{MedlinePlus CMP}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to ionized-calcium testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item tingling around the mouth or in the fingers;
                      \item muscle cramps, twitching, or spasms;
                      \item weakness, constipation, or excessive thirst;
                      \item kidney stones or bone pain;
                      \item confusion, nausea, or vomiting;
                      \item seizures or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item critical illness or an abnormal blood pH; or
                      \item suspected parathyroid, kidney, or vitamin D disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Seizure, severe muscle spasm, confusion, or a serious heartbeat disturbance requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total calcium}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total calcium measures free calcium plus calcium bound to proteins and other molecules in blood. Calcium is needed for bones, nerves, muscles, and the heart. Total calcium is often part of a metabolic panel, but albumin and pH affect how it relates to biologically active calcium. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/comprehensive-metabolic-panel-cmp/}{MedlinePlus CMP}.
                    \section{History}
                    Calcium measurement became part of routine blood chemistry as colorimetric and later automated methods were developed. MedlinePlus describes its role in metabolic-panel testing; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific calcium reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult total-calcium interval is about 8.5--10.5 mg/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low total calcium may result from low albumin, vitamin D deficiency, hypoparathyroidism, kidney disease, magnesium disturbance, or severe illness. High total calcium may result from hyperparathyroidism, malignancy, vitamin excess, medicines, or dehydration. When albumin is abnormal or symptoms are strong, ionized calcium may be more useful. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/comprehensive-metabolic-panel-cmp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating total calcium and checking albumin;
                      \item measuring ionized calcium when the result may be misleading;
                      \item checking PTH, phosphate, magnesium, vitamin D, and kidney function;
                      \item reviewing medicines and supplements; or
                      \item obtaining ECG, urine calcium, or imaging when clinically indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Total calcium alone does not establish the cause of an abnormal result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/comprehensive-metabolic-panel-cmp/}{MedlinePlus CMP}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to total-calcium testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item tingling or numbness;
                      \item muscle cramps, twitching, or spasms;
                      \item weakness or fatigue;
                      \item constipation, nausea, or vomiting;
                      \item excessive thirst or urination;
                      \item kidney stones or bone pain;
                      \item confusion, seizures, or irregular heartbeat; or
                      \item suspected parathyroid, kidney, vitamin D, or malignancy-related disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Seizure, severe confusion, severe muscle spasm, or serious palpitations requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/comprehensive-metabolic-panel-cmp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Magnesium}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum magnesium measures a mineral needed for nerve, muscle, and heart function and for many enzyme reactions. It is commonly interpreted with potassium, calcium, kidney function, and the clinical setting. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus Electrolyte Panel}.
                    \section{History}
                    Magnesium became part of routine clinical electrolyte testing as chemical and automated analyser methods developed. Current use is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific magnesium reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult reference interval is about 1.7--2.2 mg/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low magnesium may result from poor intake, diarrhea, malabsorption, alcohol use, diuretics, or kidney losses and can cause weakness, tremor, seizures, arrhythmia, or low potassium and calcium. High magnesium usually results from kidney failure or excess magnesium and can cause low blood pressure, weakness, reduced reflexes, or respiratory depression. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating magnesium and checking for sample or medication effects;
                      \item checking potassium, calcium, phosphate, kidney function, and ECG;
                      \item reviewing diarrhea, alcohol use, nutrition, diuretics, antacids, laxatives, and supplements;
                      \item measuring urine magnesium when renal loss is suspected; or
                      \item urgent replacement, removal, or cardiac monitoring when levels are dangerous.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Magnesium treatment must account for kidney function and the severity of the abnormality. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to magnesium testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item weakness, tremor, or muscle cramps;
                      \item numbness or tingling;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or prolonged diarrhea;
                      \item palpitations or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item seizures or confusion;
                      \item kidney disease;
                      \item heavy alcohol use or poor nutrition; or
                      \item use of medicines or supplements that alter magnesium.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Seizure, severe weakness, fainting, or breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Phosphate}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum phosphate measures inorganic phosphate, which works with calcium in bone and is also needed for cellular energy and membrane function. The kidneys, parathyroid hormone, vitamin D, and bone turnover regulate phosphate. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus Electrolyte Panel}.
                    \section{History}
                    Phosphate measurement became part of mineral and renal chemistry testing as chemical assays and automated analysers developed. Its current role is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific phosphate reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult reference interval is about 2.5--4.5 mg/dL, but age and the laboratory method matter. Low phosphate may result from poor intake, alcohol use, vitamin D deficiency, hyperparathyroidism, refeeding, or renal loss and can cause weakness, impaired breathing, or bone disease. High phosphate commonly occurs with reduced kidney filtration, hypoparathyroidism, cell breakdown, or phosphate loading. Interpretation requires calcium, PTH, vitamin D, kidney function, and medication review. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating phosphate with calcium and albumin;
                      \item checking PTH, vitamin D, magnesium, kidney function, and alkaline phosphatase;
                      \item reviewing diet, supplements, antacids, laxatives, and kidney medicines;
                      \item measuring urine phosphate when renal loss is suspected; or
                      \item evaluating bone disease, endocrine disease, kidney failure, or cell breakdown.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Serum phosphate alone does not identify the cause of an abnormal result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to phosphate testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item muscle weakness or bone pain;
                      \item difficulty breathing from severe muscle weakness;
                      \item confusion, seizures, or altered mental state;
                      \item fractures or poor bone mineralization;
                      \item kidney disease or reduced kidney function;
                      \item suspected vitamin D or parathyroid disease;
                      \item severe malnutrition, alcohol use, or refeeding; or
                      \item major tissue injury or cell breakdown.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe weakness, breathing difficulty, seizure, or confusion requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/electrolyte-panel/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total protein}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total protein measures albumin plus globulins in the liquid part of blood. Albumin helps keep fluid inside blood vessels and carries many substances; globulins include immune and transport proteins. The test is used with other blood and urine tests when liver disease, kidney disease, nutritional problems, inflammation, or abnormal protein production is being considered. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Total-protein measurement developed from early chemical analysis of serum proteins and later separation of albumin and globulin fractions. Modern clinical use combines total protein with albumin, calculated globulin, or electrophoresis; these uses are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003483.htm}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``total protein''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult interval is about 6.0--8.3 g/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low total protein may reflect low albumin, protein loss through the kidney or gut, malnutrition, liver disease, or overhydration. High total protein may reflect dehydration, chronic inflammation, infection, or a monoclonal protein. Total protein does not identify which protein is abnormal; serum protein electrophoresis may be needed. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating total protein and checking albumin and A/G ratio;
                      \item performing serum protein electrophoresis and immunofixation;
                      \item checking liver tests, kidney function, urinalysis, and urine protein;
                      \item reviewing nutrition, fluid status, inflammation, and medicines; or
                      \item assessing for protein-losing, immune, liver, kidney, or plasma-cell disorders.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Total protein is a pattern-finding test and cannot diagnose a specific disorder by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/globulin-test/}{MedlinePlus Globulin Test}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to total-protein testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the feet, legs, or abdomen;
                      \item fatigue or unexplained weight loss;
                      \item loss of appetite, nausea, or vomiting;
                      \item jaundice or dark urine;
                      \item blood or persistent foam in the urine;
                      \item recurrent infections;
                      \item bone pain or unexplained anemia; or
                      \item an abnormal liver, kidney, or blood-count result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Total protein itself causes no symptoms. Severe swelling, jaundice, confusion, or reduced urine requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{47. Albumin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum albumin measures the main protein made by the liver. It helps keep fluid in blood vessels and carries hormones, medicines, vitamins, and other substances. Albumin is used with liver, kidney, nutritional, and inflammatory assessments, but a low value does not identify one cause. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/albumin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Albumin was separated from other serum proteins as protein chemistry and electrophoresis developed; the modern clinical test measures its concentration in serum. MedlinePlus describes its current laboratory use at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/albumin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``albumin''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult interval is about 3.5--5.0 g/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low albumin may occur with inflammation, liver disease, kidney or gastrointestinal protein loss, malnutrition, severe burns, pregnancy, or dilution. High albumin usually reflects dehydration or diarrhea rather than increased albumin production. Albumin changes slowly and is not a stand-alone nutrition score. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking total protein, globulin, A/G ratio, and liver tests;
                      \item testing urine albumin or protein and kidney function;
                      \item reviewing inflammation, nutrition, pregnancy, burns, fluid status, and medicines;
                      \item checking serum protein electrophoresis when globulins are high; or
                      \item assessing cirrhosis, nephrotic protein loss, or gastrointestinal loss when indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Albumin should not be used alone to diagnose malnutrition or liver failure. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/albumin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to albumin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the feet, legs, or abdomen;
                      \item jaundice or dark urine;
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or poor appetite;
                      \item unexplained weight loss;
                      \item blood or persistent foam in the urine;
                      \item diarrhea or suspected malabsorption;
                      \item severe burns or critical illness; or
                      \item known liver or kidney disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Albumin itself causes no symptoms. Severe swelling, jaundice, confusion, or reduced urine requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/albumin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Globulin, calculated}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Calculated globulin is estimated by subtracting albumin from total protein. It represents a mixture of proteins, including immunoglobulins and other alpha and beta proteins; it is not a direct measurement of each globulin fraction. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/globulin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Calculated globulin became a practical clinical measure after routine assays for total protein and albumin were established. Modern laboratories use it as a screening clue and use electrophoresis to separate protein fractions when needed. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/globulin-test/}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the calculation; FDA authorization applies to the underlying protein assays.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common calculated-globulin interval is about 2.0--3.5 g/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. Low globulin may reflect antibody deficiency, liver disease, malnutrition, or protein loss. High globulin may reflect chronic infection, inflammation, autoimmune disease, liver disease, or a monoclonal gammopathy. Calculated globulin inherits error from both total protein and albumin and cannot replace protein electrophoresis. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/globulin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating total protein and albumin;
                      \item performing serum protein electrophoresis and immunofixation;
                      \item measuring quantitative immunoglobulins;
                      \item checking liver, kidney, urine-protein, and nutritional findings; or
                      \item assessing recurrent infection, autoimmune disease, chronic inflammation, or a plasma-cell disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Calculated globulin alone cannot distinguish polyclonal inflammation from a monoclonal protein. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/protein-electrophoresis-by-immunofixation-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to calculated-globulin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item recurrent or unusual infections;
                      \item unexplained weight loss or fatigue;
                      \item swelling or persistent foam in the urine;
                      \item jaundice or chronic liver symptoms;
                      \item bone pain or unexplained anemia;
                      \item autoimmune symptoms such as joint pain or rash;
                      \item abnormal total protein or albumin; or
                      \item an unexplained abnormal blood count.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Calculated globulin itself causes no symptoms. Severe infection, jaundice, confusion, or reduced urine requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/globulin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Albumin-to-globulin ratio}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The albumin-to-globulin (A/G) ratio divides albumin by calculated globulin. It compares two broad groups of serum proteins and can point toward low albumin, increased globulins, or both. It is interpreted with total protein, albumin, electrophoresis, and the clinical picture. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    The A/G ratio became a routine calculated index after separate serum albumin and total-protein assays became available. Its current use as a screening clue and the role of electrophoresis are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the calculation; FDA authorization applies to the underlying assays.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common A/G ratio interval is about 1.0--2.5, but the laboratory interval must be used. A low ratio may reflect low albumin, increased globulins, liver disease, kidney or gastrointestinal protein loss, inflammation, or a monoclonal protein. A high ratio may reflect low globulins, antibody deficiency, or dehydration. The ratio is not diagnostic and can be distorted when either component is measured inaccurately. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing albumin, total protein, and calculated globulin separately;
                      \item performing serum protein electrophoresis and immunofixation;
                      \item checking urine protein, kidney function, and liver tests;
                      \item measuring quantitative immunoglobulins; or
                      \item assessing nutrition, inflammation, infection, autoimmune disease, or plasma-cell disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The A/G ratio is a clue about protein balance and does not identify a disease by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/globulin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to A/G-ratio testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swelling of the legs or abdomen;
                      \item fatigue or unexplained weight loss;
                      \item recurrent infections;
                      \item jaundice or dark urine;
                      \item blood or persistent foam in the urine;
                      \item bone pain or unexplained anemia;
                      \item chronic inflammation or autoimmune symptoms; or
                      \item abnormal albumin or total-protein results.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The A/G ratio itself causes no symptoms. Severe swelling, jaundice, confusion, or reduced urine requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/total-protein-and-albumin-globulin-a-g-ratio/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{AST-to-ALT ratio}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The AST-to-ALT ratio divides the aspartate aminotransferase result by the alanine aminotransferase result. It can add context to a pattern of enzyme elevation, but it is not a stand-alone liver diagnosis and may be misleading when either enzyme is only slightly elevated or comes from another tissue. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    AST and ALT assays became routine as enzyme methods developed in clinical chemistry. The ratio is a later interpretive calculation rather than a separate biological test. NIDDK describes AST and ALT as part of blood testing for liver disease; see \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological calculation ``AST-to-ALT ratio''; FDA authorization applies to the enzyme assays.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    There is no universal normal or diagnostic cutoff for the ratio. A ratio above about 2 can support alcohol-associated liver disease in an appropriate clinical setting, but it is not specific and can occur with advanced fibrosis, muscle injury, or other causes of AST elevation. A ratio below 1 often occurs when ALT predominates but does not identify the cause. Enzyme magnitude, alkaline phosphatase, bilirubin, albumin, INR, medicines, alcohol, viral risks, and imaging are usually more informative than the ratio alone. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating AST and ALT with the complete liver panel;
                      \item checking bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase, GGT, albumin, and INR;
                      \item reviewing alcohol, medicines, supplements, exercise, and muscle symptoms;
                      \item testing for viral hepatitis, autoimmune liver disease, or metabolic liver disease; or
                      \item using ultrasound or other imaging when obstruction, fatty liver, or structural disease is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The AST-to-ALT ratio should support, not replace, evaluation of the complete liver-test pattern. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to AST-to-ALT calculation include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item jaundice;
                      \item dark urine or pale stool;
                      \item abdominal pain or swelling;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or loss of appetite;
                      \item itching or unusual fatigue;
                      \item confusion or easy bleeding;
                      \item heavy alcohol use or medicine exposure;
                      \item muscle pain or recent strenuous exercise; or
                      \item an abnormal AST or ALT result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The ratio itself causes no symptoms. Jaundice with confusion, vomiting blood, black stool, or severe abdominal swelling requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total bilirubin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total bilirubin measures the main forms of bilirubin in blood: unconjugated bilirubin and conjugated bilirubin. Bilirubin is produced when old red blood cells are broken down; the liver processes it and removes it in bile. The test helps assess jaundice, red-cell breakdown, liver injury, and bile-flow obstruction. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Bilirubin testing developed from chemical methods for measuring bile pigments and became routine with automated liver panels. The current clinical role of bilirubin testing is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific bilirubin reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult total-bilirubin interval is about 0.2--1.2 mg/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. High bilirubin may result from hemolysis, impaired bilirubin processing, liver-cell injury, cholestasis, or bile-duct obstruction. A low value is usually not clinically important. The pattern of direct and indirect bilirubin with AST, ALT, ALP, GGT, CBC, and reticulocytes is more useful than total bilirubin alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating total bilirubin with direct and indirect fractions;
                      \item checking AST, ALT, ALP, GGT, albumin, total protein, and PT/INR;
                      \item checking CBC, reticulocyte count, LDH, haptoglobin, and a blood smear when hemolysis is possible;
                      \item testing for viral hepatitis, autoimmune liver disease, or inherited bilirubin disorders; or
                      \item using ultrasound or other imaging when obstruction is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Total bilirubin does not identify the cause of jaundice by itself. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to total-bilirubin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item yellow skin or yellowing of the whites of the eyes;
                      \item dark urine or pale stools;
                      \item abdominal pain or swelling;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or loss of appetite;
                      \item itching or unusual fatigue;
                      \item fever or suspected hepatitis;
                      \item anemia, rapid heartbeat, or suspected red-cell breakdown; or
                      \item an abnormal liver-enzyme or blood-count result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Total bilirubin itself causes no symptoms. Jaundice with confusion, vomiting blood, black stool, severe abdominal pain, or rapidly worsening illness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Direct bilirubin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Direct bilirubin measures bilirubin that has been processed by the liver and is water-soluble, mainly conjugated bilirubin. It helps distinguish problems with bilirubin processing or red-cell breakdown from impaired bile excretion or cholestasis. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Direct-bilirubin measurement developed as laboratory methods separated the water-soluble conjugated fraction from total bile pigments. It is now commonly reported with total bilirubin in liver panels. The clinical use is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}; there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement. FDA authorization applies to specific fractionation reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult direct-bilirubin interval is about 0.0--0.3 mg/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. High direct bilirubin supports impaired excretion, cholestasis, hepatocellular disease, or bile-duct obstruction, but can also rise in other liver conditions. Low direct bilirubin is usually not clinically important. Interpretation requires total and indirect bilirubin, AST, ALT, ALP, GGT, and symptoms. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating total, direct, and indirect bilirubin;
                      \item checking AST, ALT, ALP, GGT, albumin, total protein, and PT/INR;
                      \item performing an abdominal ultrasound when bile obstruction is possible;
                      \item testing for viral hepatitis, autoimmune disease, or drug-related injury; or
                      \item checking CBC, reticulocytes, LDH, and haptoglobin when hemolysis remains possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Direct bilirubin indicates a pattern, not a specific diagnosis. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to direct-bilirubin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item yellow skin or eyes;
                      \item dark urine or pale stools;
                      \item itching;
                      \item right-upper-abdominal pain;
                      \item fever, nausea, or vomiting;
                      \item abdominal swelling;
                      \item fatigue or loss of appetite; or
                      \item an abnormal total bilirubin, ALP, AST, ALT, or GGT result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Direct bilirubin itself causes no symptoms. Jaundice with fever, severe pain, confusion, vomiting blood, or black stool requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Alkaline phosphatase (ALP)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Alkaline phosphatase (ALP) is an enzyme found in many tissues, especially bile ducts and bone. The blood test helps identify a cholestatic or bile-flow pattern and can also rise from bone disease. ALP is interpreted with GGT, bilirubin, AST, ALT, age, pregnancy, and symptoms. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003470.htm}{MedlinePlus ALP}.
                    \section{History}
                    ALP measurement became a routine enzyme test as biochemical methods and automated liver panels developed. MedlinePlus describes ALP as a tissue enzyme and notes that isoenzyme testing can help identify its source. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``ALP''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult interval is approximately 44--147 U/L, but age, pregnancy, blood group, method, and laboratory interval matter. High ALP may occur with bile-duct obstruction or cholestasis, liver disease, bone growth, fracture healing, Paget disease, or other bone turnover. Low ALP may occur with malnutrition, zinc deficiency, hypothyroidism, or rare metabolic disease. GGT or ALP isoenzymes can help separate liver from bone sources. See \href{https://www.medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003470.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating ALP with bilirubin, GGT, AST, and ALT;
                      \item checking ALP isoenzymes when the source is unclear;
                      \item using abdominal ultrasound or other biliary imaging when obstruction is possible;
                      \item checking calcium, phosphate, vitamin D, PTH, and bone markers when bone disease is possible; or
                      \item testing for viral, autoimmune, or medicine-related liver disease when indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ALP alone cannot distinguish liver disease from bone disease. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/gamma-glutamyl-transferase-ggt-test/}{MedlinePlus GGT}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to ALP testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item yellow skin or eyes;
                      \item dark urine or pale stools;
                      \item itching;
                      \item right-upper-abdominal pain;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or fatigue;
                      \item bone pain, fractures, or abnormal growth;
                      \item pregnancy or known bone or liver disease; or
                      \item an abnormal bilirubin or other liver-enzyme result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ALP itself causes no symptoms. Jaundice with fever, severe pain, confusion, or vomiting blood requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Alanine aminotransferase (ALT)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Alanine aminotransferase (ALT) is an enzyme found mainly in liver cells. When liver cells are injured, ALT can leak into blood. ALT is usually measured with AST and the rest of a liver panel; it indicates possible cell injury but does not measure liver function by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/alt-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    ALT testing developed from enzyme-activity methods used in clinical chemistry and became routine with automated liver panels. MedlinePlus describes its current use with AST and other liver tests. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``ALT''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
