                    A common interval is approximately 7--56 U/L, but the laboratory interval and method must be used. High ALT may occur with fatty liver disease, viral or autoimmune hepatitis, medicine or toxin injury, ischemia, or other liver-cell injury. ALT is more liver-associated than AST but can be normal in clinically important liver disease and can rise without severe loss of function. Low ALT is usually not clinically important. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/alt-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/nafld-nash/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating ALT with AST, ALP, GGT, bilirubin, albumin, and PT/INR;
                      \item reviewing alcohol, medicines, supplements, weight, exercise, and viral-hepatitis risk;
                      \item testing for viral hepatitis, autoimmune liver disease, or metabolic disease;
                      \item using ultrasound or other imaging; or
                      \item urgent assessment when ALT is markedly elevated with jaundice, pain, or systemic illness.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ALT elevation does not identify the cause by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to ALT testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item jaundice;
                      \item dark urine or pale stools;
                      \item abdominal pain or swelling;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or loss of appetite;
                      \item itching or unusual fatigue;
                      \item heavy alcohol use or possible medicine/toxin exposure;
                      \item obesity, diabetes, or suspected fatty liver; or
                      \item an abnormal AST, ALP, bilirubin, or other liver result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ALT itself causes no symptoms. Jaundice with confusion, bleeding, severe abdominal pain, or persistent vomiting requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Aspartate aminotransferase (AST)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Aspartate aminotransferase (AST) is an enzyme found in liver, skeletal muscle, heart muscle, and red blood cells. A high blood value can therefore indicate liver-cell injury, muscle injury, hemolysis, or another tissue problem. AST is interpreted with ALT and the full clinical picture. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/cirrhosis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    AST testing developed from enzyme-activity assays in clinical chemistry and became routine in liver panels. MedlinePlus lists AST among common liver tests while noting that liver tests alone do not diagnose a specific disease. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``AST''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common interval is approximately 10--40 U/L, but the laboratory interval and method must be used. High AST may occur with liver disease, alcohol-associated disease, muscle injury, strenuous exercise, heart injury, hemolysis, or ischemia. Low AST is usually not clinically important. AST is less liver-specific than ALT, so an AST result cannot be interpreted as liver injury without considering muscle, heart, red-cell, and medication causes. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating AST with ALT, ALP, GGT, bilirubin, albumin, and PT/INR;
                      \item checking CK, LDH, and a blood sample for hemolysis when muscle or red-cell injury is possible;
                      \item reviewing alcohol, medicines, supplements, trauma, and vigorous exercise;
                      \item testing for viral, autoimmune, or metabolic liver disease; or
                      \item using liver or muscle imaging when the clinical examination indicates it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    AST alone cannot identify the injured tissue or the cause. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/liver-disease/autoimmune-hepatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to AST testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item jaundice or dark urine;
                      \item abdominal pain or swelling;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or fatigue;
                      \item muscle pain, weakness, or dark urine after muscle injury;
                      \item chest pain or shortness of breath;
                      \item heavy alcohol use or medicine exposure;
                      \item recent strenuous exercise, trauma, or seizure; or
                      \item an abnormal ALT or other liver-panel result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    AST itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, severe weakness, jaundice with confusion, or vomiting blood requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/liver-function-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) is an enzyme associated with liver and bile-duct cells. GGT is often measured with ALP and other liver tests to help decide whether an elevated ALP is more likely to come from liver/bile ducts or bone. GGT is sensitive but not specific. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/gamma-glutamyl-transferase-ggt-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    GGT testing developed from enzyme assays used to study hepatobiliary and microsomal enzyme activity and later became part of automated liver panels. MedlinePlus describes its current use with ALP and other liver tests. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``GGT''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Common reference intervals vary by sex and laboratory; one illustrative interval is about 9--48 U/L in men and 7--32 U/L in women. High GGT may occur with bile-duct or liver disease, alcohol use, medicines, or other conditions. A high GGT does not diagnose alcohol use disorder or prove liver disease, and a normal GGT does not exclude every liver problem. Low GGT is generally not clinically important. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/gamma-glutamyl-transferase-ggt-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating GGT with ALP, ALT, AST, and bilirubin;
                      \item reviewing alcohol, medicines, supplements, and metabolic risk;
                      \item checking ALP isoenzymes or bone tests when the ALP source is unclear;
                      \item using abdominal ultrasound or other imaging when bile obstruction is possible; or
                      \item testing for viral or autoimmune liver disease when the complete pattern supports it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    GGT should not be used alone to diagnose a liver disorder or alcohol use. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/gamma-glutamyl-transferase-ggt-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to GGT testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item jaundice;
                      \item dark urine or pale stools;
                      \item itching;
                      \item abdominal pain or swelling;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or fatigue;
                      \item an abnormal ALP or other liver-test result;
                      \item possible medicine or alcohol-related liver injury; or
                      \item monitoring known liver or bile-duct disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    GGT itself causes no symptoms. Jaundice with fever, severe abdominal pain, confusion, or bleeding requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/gamma-glutamyl-transferase-ggt-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) is an enzyme found in almost all tissues, especially muscle, liver, kidneys, and red blood cells. Tissue injury can release LDH into blood. The test detects a broad tissue-injury signal and cannot identify the injured organ by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/lactate-dehydrogenase-ldh-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    LDH measurement entered clinical chemistry as enzyme assays became available; the biological enzyme and its isoenzymes were studied before automated serum testing. The clinical test has no single inventor or single FDA approval date. FDA authorization applies to particular LDH reagents, analysers, and isoenzyme systems. MedlinePlus describes the current test and its broad tissue distribution at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/lactate-dehydrogenase-ldh-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common interval is about 140--280 U/L, but the laboratory interval and method must be used. High LDH may occur with hemolysis, liver or muscle injury, tissue ischemia, infection, kidney disease, or some cancers. A hemolyzed blood specimen can falsely raise LDH because red cells contain LDH. Low LDH is usually not clinically important. LDH is most useful when interpreted with symptoms, CBC, bilirubin, AST, ALT, CK, and other targeted tests. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/lactate-dehydrogenase-ldh-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating LDH when the specimen was hemolyzed or the result does not fit;
                      \item checking CBC, bilirubin, reticulocytes, haptoglobin, and blood smear for hemolysis;
                      \item checking AST, ALT, ALP, bilirubin, and liver imaging when liver disease is possible;
                      \item checking CK and muscle assessment when muscle injury is possible; or
                      \item ordering organ-specific imaging or tests based on the symptoms and other results.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    LDH alone does not diagnose cancer, hemolysis, or a particular organ injury. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/lactate-dehydrogenase-ldh-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to LDH testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item unexplained fatigue or weakness;
                      \item jaundice or dark urine;
                      \item abdominal pain or swelling;
                      \item muscle pain or weakness;
                      \item fever or suspected infection;
                      \item anemia or suspected red-cell breakdown;
                      \item chest pain or symptoms of tissue ischemia; or
                      \item monitoring a known cancer or other tissue-injury condition.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    LDH itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, severe weakness, jaundice with confusion, or sudden neurologic symptoms requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/lactate-dehydrogenase-ldh-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Amylase}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Amylase is a digestive enzyme made mainly by the pancreas and salivary glands. An amylase blood test measures the enzyme in blood; a urine test can provide additional information. The test is used mainly to investigate pancreatic disease, especially pancreatitis, but salivary-gland disease and other conditions can also alter it. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/amylase-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/pancreatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Amylase was recognized through the study of starch digestion and digestive secretions; blood and urine enzyme assays later became clinical chemistry tests. The clinical assay has no single FDA approval date. FDA authorization applies to specific amylase reagents and analysers. MedlinePlus describes the current blood and urine testing uses at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/amylase-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common blood-amylase interval is approximately 30--110 U/L, but the laboratory interval must be used. High amylase may occur in acute pancreatitis, salivary-gland disease, kidney failure, intestinal disease, medicines, or macroamylasemia. Low amylase can occur with advanced pancreatic damage or reduced pancreatic enzyme production. Amylase does not diagnose pancreatitis alone; lipase, symptoms, and imaging are often more informative. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/pancreatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking lipase, glucose, liver tests, triglycerides, calcium, and kidney function;
                      \item repeating amylase or measuring urine amylase when the timing is important;
                      \item checking for macroamylasemia when blood amylase is high without compatible illness;
                      \item using abdominal ultrasound, CT, or other imaging when pancreatitis or obstruction is suspected; or
                      \item evaluating salivary glands, medicines, gallstones, alcohol, or other causes according to the clinical history.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NIDDK states that pancreatitis diagnosis uses symptoms, history, laboratory tests, and imaging; amylase is not sufficient alone. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/pancreatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to amylase testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item sudden or persistent upper-abdominal pain;
                      \item pain spreading to the back;
                      \item nausea or vomiting;
                      \item fever or fast heartbeat;
                      \item abdominal tenderness or swelling;
                      \item jaundice;
                      \item greasy or foul-smelling stools;
                      \item salivary-gland swelling or pain; or
                      \item known pancreatitis, gallstones, pancreatic disease, or relevant medicine/alcohol exposure.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Amylase itself causes no symptoms. Severe persistent abdominal pain, repeated vomiting, fever with abdominal pain, fainting, or jaundice requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/amylase-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total cholesterol}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total cholesterol measures the cholesterol carried in several blood-lipoprotein particles. Cholesterol is needed for cell membranes and hormone production, but excess atherogenic cholesterol contributes to plaque formation in arteries. Total cholesterol is interpreted with LDL-C, HDL-C, triglycerides, non-HDL-C, diabetes, blood pressure, smoking, and overall cardiovascular risk. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/cholesterol-levels/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    Cholesterol measurement became a routine cardiovascular test during the development of clinical chemistry and population studies linking blood lipids with atherosclerosis. The CDC's standardization program now assesses the accuracy of total cholesterol, HDL-C, LDL-C, and triglyceride measurements. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``total cholesterol''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A desirable total-cholesterol value is usually below 200 mg/dL, but total cholesterol alone is not a treatment target for every person. A high value may reflect high LDL-C, remnant cholesterol, genetics, diet, hypothyroidism, kidney disease, or cholestatic liver disease. A low value may occur with malnutrition, malabsorption, hyperthyroidism, chronic illness, or liver disease. The atherosclerotic risk depends more directly on atherogenic particles and the person's baseline risk. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating a lipid panel when the result or preparation is uncertain;
                      \item checking LDL-C, HDL-C, triglycerides, non-HDL-C, and sometimes ApoB or Lp(a);
                      \item reviewing diabetes, thyroid, kidney, liver, medicines, diet, alcohol, and family history;
                      \item estimating cardiovascular risk and considering blood-pressure, glucose, or coronary-calcium assessment; or
                      \item repeating testing after a lifestyle or lipid-lowering intervention.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Total cholesterol alone does not determine whether medicine is needed. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to total-cholesterol testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item routine cardiovascular-risk assessment;
                      \item diabetes or prediabetes;
                      \item high blood pressure;
                      \item smoking or obesity;
                      \item a family history of high cholesterol or early heart disease;
                      \item known cardiovascular, kidney, or thyroid disease;
                      \item xanthomas or xanthelasma; or
                      \item monitoring lipid-lowering treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    High cholesterol usually causes no symptoms. Chest pressure, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment and is not diagnosed by cholesterol testing. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{LDL cholesterol}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    LDL cholesterol estimates the cholesterol carried in low-density lipoprotein particles. LDL is a major atherogenic particle and can contribute to plaque formation in arteries. LDL-C is used with overall cardiovascular risk to guide prevention and treatment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/cholesterol-levels/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    LDL-C became clinically useful as lipoprotein separation and cholesterol measurement improved. It may be measured directly or estimated from total cholesterol, HDL-C, and triglycerides; the method and triglyceride level affect accuracy. CDC and NHLBI describe standardization of LDL-C and related lipid measurements at \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``LDL-C''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    LDL-C below 100 mg/dL is often described as desirable, but the appropriate goal depends on cardiovascular risk; people with established atherosclerotic disease may have lower treatment goals. High LDL-C may be inherited or result from diet, hypothyroidism, kidney disease, diabetes, or medicines. Low LDL-C is usually favorable; unexpectedly low values may prompt assessment for malnutrition, malabsorption, hyperthyroidism, chronic illness, or liver disease. LDL-C is not identical to the number of atherogenic particles, so ApoB or non-HDL-C may add information when results are discordant. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://professional.heart.org/en/science-news/2026-guideline-on-the-management-of-dyslipidemia}{AHA guideline summary}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating the lipid panel and checking triglycerides and non-HDL-C;
                      \item measuring ApoB or Lp(a) when residual or inherited risk is suspected;
                      \item reviewing family history and considering familial-hypercholesterolemia assessment;
                      \item checking thyroid, kidney, liver, and diabetes tests; or
                      \item estimating cardiovascular risk and selecting lifestyle or lipid-lowering treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    LDL-C is important but should not be interpreted without the person's cardiovascular risk and treatment history. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to LDL-C testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item routine cardiovascular-risk assessment;
                      \item diabetes, kidney disease, or high blood pressure;
                      \item smoking, obesity, or metabolic syndrome;
                      \item a family history of high LDL-C or early heart disease;
                      \item premature coronary, cerebrovascular, or peripheral artery disease;
                      \item tendon xanthomas or yellow lipid deposits around the eyes; or
                      \item monitoring statin or other lipid-lowering treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    High LDL-C usually causes no symptoms. Chest pain, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{58. HDL cholesterol}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    HDL cholesterol measures cholesterol carried in high-density lipoprotein particles. HDL participates in reverse cholesterol transport, but a higher HDL-C value is not automatically protective and raising HDL-C alone has not become the main treatment goal. HDL-C is interpreted with LDL-C, triglycerides, non-HDL-C, and overall risk. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/cholesterol-levels/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    HDL-C measurement became part of lipid testing as lipoprotein fractions were separated and their relationships with atherosclerosis were studied. CDC's lipid standardization program includes HDL-C measurement. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``HDL-C''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    HDL-C above 40 mg/dL in men and above 50 mg/dL in women is often used as a general reference, while above 60 mg/dL has traditionally been considered favorable. These are not universal treatment targets. Low HDL-C often accompanies insulin resistance, obesity, smoking, and high triglycerides. Very high HDL-C is not guaranteed to reduce risk and may reflect genetics, alcohol, medicines, or disease. LDL-C and other atherogenic measures remain central to treatment decisions. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing the complete lipid panel and non-HDL-C;
                      \item assessing diet, physical activity, smoking, alcohol, weight, glucose, and blood pressure;
                      \item checking thyroid, liver, and kidney conditions when the pattern is unexpected;
                      \item measuring ApoB or Lp(a) when cardiovascular-risk refinement is appropriate; or
                      \item focusing treatment on overall risk and atherogenic lipids rather than trying to raise HDL-C alone.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    HDL-C is a risk marker, not a diagnosis by itself. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to HDL-C testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item routine cardiovascular-risk assessment;
                      \item diabetes, obesity, or metabolic syndrome;
                      \item smoking or physical inactivity;
                      \item high triglycerides or low HDL-C in a prior test;
                      \item a family history of early heart disease;
                      \item known cardiovascular or kidney disease; or
                      \item monitoring lifestyle or lipid treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    HDL-C itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment and is not diagnosed by HDL-C testing. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Triglycerides}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Triglycerides are fats carried in blood, mainly in triglyceride-rich lipoproteins. The body uses them for energy, but persistently high levels are associated with cardiovascular risk and very high levels can cause acute pancreatitis. Triglycerides are measured as part of a lipid panel. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/pancreatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Triglyceride measurement became routine as lipid chemistry and cardiovascular-risk studies developed. CDC and NHLBI standardization programs include triglyceride measurement and assess the accuracy of laboratory methods. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``triglycerides''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A fasting triglyceride value below 150 mg/dL is generally desirable, but the laboratory interval and fasting status matter. High triglycerides may accompany obesity, insulin resistance, diabetes, alcohol use, hypothyroidism, kidney disease, medicines, or inherited disorders. Very high levels increase pancreatitis risk. Low triglycerides are usually not clinically important but may occur with malnutrition or malabsorption. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating a fasting lipid panel when preparation or the result is uncertain;
                      \item checking glucose, HbA1c, thyroid, kidney, and liver tests;
                      \item reviewing alcohol, diet, medicines, weight, and family history;
                      \item measuring ApoB or non-HDL-C when atherogenic particle burden needs clarification; or
                      \item checking lipase and urgent abdominal assessment when pancreatitis is possible.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Triglycerides alone do not identify the cause or determine treatment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/pancreatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to triglyceride testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item routine cardiovascular-risk assessment;
                      \item diabetes, obesity, or metabolic syndrome;
                      \item a family history of severe hypertriglyceridemia;
                      \item pancreatitis or recurrent upper-abdominal pain;
                      \item alcohol use or a medicine that can raise triglycerides;
                      \item hypothyroidism, kidney disease, or liver disease; or
                      \item monitoring lipid-lowering treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    High triglycerides usually cause no symptoms. Severe persistent upper-abdominal pain, vomiting, fever, or fainting requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/digestive-diseases/pancreatitis/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Non-HDL cholesterol}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Non-HDL cholesterol is calculated by subtracting HDL-C from total cholesterol. It estimates cholesterol carried in all atherogenic particles, including LDL and triglyceride-rich remnants. It can be useful when triglycerides are elevated or when LDL-C does not fully represent particle-related risk. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/cholesterol-levels/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    Non-HDL-C became clinically useful as lipid testing and understanding of triglyceride-rich remnant particles developed. It is a calculation from routine lipid-panel results rather than a separate biological analyte. CDC and NHLBI describe standardization of the underlying lipid measurements at \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the calculation; FDA authorization applies to the underlying assays.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly used desirable value is below 130 mg/dL, but treatment goals depend on cardiovascular risk. High non-HDL-C suggests increased atherogenic cholesterol and may reflect high LDL-C, remnant particles, diabetes, obesity, hypothyroidism, kidney disease, or inherited dyslipidemia. Low values are usually not concerning but can accompany low lipid production or illness. Non-HDL-C can be calculated without relying on a fasting sample, but the entire lipid pattern matters. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing total cholesterol, LDL-C, HDL-C, and triglycerides;
                      \item repeating the panel and checking ApoB or Lp(a) when indicated;
                      \item reviewing diabetes, thyroid, kidney, liver, medicines, alcohol, and family history;
                      \item estimating cardiovascular risk and selecting lifestyle or lipid-lowering treatment; or
                      \item assessing for familial lipid disorders when levels are markedly abnormal.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Non-HDL-C is a risk measure and not a diagnosis by itself. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to non-HDL-C calculation include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item routine cardiovascular-risk assessment;
                      \item diabetes, obesity, or metabolic syndrome;
                      \item high triglycerides or high total cholesterol;
                      \item a family history of early heart disease;
                      \item known cardiovascular or kidney disease;
                      \item xanthomas or xanthelasma; or
                      \item monitoring lipid-lowering treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Non-HDL-C itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment and is not diagnosed by this calculation. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/cholesterollevelswhatyouneedtoknow.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Apolipoprotein B (ApoB)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Apolipoprotein B (ApoB) measures the amount of ApoB carried on atherogenic particles such as LDL, VLDL remnants, IDL, and lipoprotein(a). Because most atherogenic particles carry one ApoB molecule, ApoB estimates particle number, while LDL-C measures the cholesterol mass inside LDL particles. See \href{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/how-to-get-your-cholesterol-tested/apolipoprotein-b}{American Heart Association} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    ApoB testing developed from the biochemical characterization of lipoproteins and immunochemical methods that measure apolipoprotein concentration. The National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute includes ApoB in lipid standardization work. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``ApoB''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    There is no universal ApoB treatment target. A value of 130 mg/dL or higher has traditionally been treated as a risk-enhancing level, but interpretation depends on overall risk and the guideline used. High ApoB can reveal residual atherogenic-particle risk when LDL-C appears acceptable, especially with high triglycerides, diabetes, metabolic syndrome, or cardiovascular disease. Low ApoB is generally favorable; unexpectedly low values may occur with low lipoprotein production, malnutrition, malabsorption, severe liver disease, or inherited disorders. See \href{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/how-to-get-your-cholesterol-tested/apolipoprotein-b}{American Heart Association}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing LDL-C, non-HDL-C, triglycerides, HDL-C, and the complete lipid pattern;
                      \item assessing blood pressure, diabetes, kidney disease, smoking, weight, and family history;
                      \item repeating ApoB when treatment or the clinical state has changed;
                      \item measuring Lp(a) at least once or when inherited risk is suspected; or
                      \item estimating cardiovascular risk and deciding whether lifestyle or lipid-lowering treatment is appropriate.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ApoB refines risk assessment; it does not diagnose an arterial blockage or determine treatment by itself. See \href{https://professional.heart.org/en/science-news/2026-guideline-on-the-management-of-dyslipidemia}{AHA guideline summary}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to ApoB testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item diabetes or high triglycerides;
                      \item metabolic syndrome or obesity;
                      \item established cardiovascular disease;
                      \item cardiovascular disease at an unexpectedly young age;
                      \item a family history of premature heart disease;
                      \item discordant LDL-C and non-HDL-C results; or
                      \item assessment of residual risk during lipid treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ApoB itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment and is not diagnosed by ApoB testing. See \href{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/how-to-get-your-cholesterol-tested/apolipoprotein-b}{American Heart Association}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Lipoprotein(a)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Lipoprotein(a), written Lp(a), is an LDL-like particle with an additional apolipoprotein(a) attached to ApoB. Lp(a) is largely inherited and can contribute to atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease and calcific aortic-valve stenosis. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/news/2024/lipoproteina-what-know-about-elevated-levels}{NHLBI} and \href{https://professional.heart.org/en/science-news/lipoprotein-a-a-genetically-determined-causal-and-prevalent-risk-factor-for-ascvd}{American Heart Association}.
                    \section{History}
                    Lp(a) was identified as a distinct lipoprotein in the twentieth century as lipoprotein separation and immunologic methods advanced. Modern testing is complicated by variation in Apo(a) size and by differences between mass and particle-concentration assays. NHLBI describes standardization challenges at \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/events/2019/standardization-lipoproteina-measurement-humans}{NHLBI}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``Lp(a)''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Lp(a) may be reported in nmol/L or mg/dL, and the units should not be converted with one universal factor. A high Lp(a) is commonly considered a risk-enhancing finding, but the cutoff is assay- and guideline-dependent; one commonly used threshold is about 125 nmol/L or 50 mg/dL. Low Lp(a) is not known to cause disease. Lp(a) is usually measured once in adulthood because it is relatively stable, while the overall risk still depends on LDL-C, blood pressure, diabetes, smoking, family history, and other factors. See \href{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/about-cholesterol/cholesterol-top-10}{American Heart Association} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/news/2024/lipoproteina-what-know-about-elevated-levels}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item confirming the unit, assay, and laboratory interval;
                      \item reviewing LDL-C, non-HDL-C, ApoB, triglycerides, and global cardiovascular risk;
                      \item checking family history and considering testing of selected relatives;
                      \item intensifying management of modifiable risk factors, especially LDL-C, blood pressure, diabetes, and smoking; or
                      \item considering coronary-artery-calcium imaging when the treatment decision remains uncertain.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    There is currently no routine treatment that simply removes an inherited high Lp(a); management focuses on overall risk reduction. See \href{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/genetic-conditions/lipoprotein-a-risks}{American Heart Association}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to Lp(a) testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item premature coronary artery disease;
                      \item ischemic stroke or peripheral artery disease at a young age;
                      \item calcific aortic-valve stenosis;
                      \item a family history of premature cardiovascular disease;
                      \item familial hypercholesterolemia;
                      \item cardiovascular disease despite controlled LDL-C; or
                      \item routine once-in-a-lifetime risk assessment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Lp(a) itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, fainting, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/news/2024/lipoproteina-what-know-about-elevated-levels}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{High-sensitivity CRP (hs-CRP)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    High-sensitivity C-reactive protein (hs-CRP) measures very small concentrations of CRP. In a clinically stable person, it may add information about cardiovascular risk; it is different from standard CRP, which is generally used to assess more evident inflammation or infection. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/about-cholesterol/cholesterol-top-10}{American Heart Association}.
                    \section{History}
                    CRP was identified as an inflammation-related blood protein in the twentieth century, and higher-sensitivity assays were later developed to measure concentrations below the range of ordinary CRP assays. The clinical use of hs-CRP as a risk marker is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003356.htm}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``hs-CRP''; FDA authorization applies to specific high-sensitivity reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    In stable adults, below 1 mg/L is often considered lower relative risk, 1--3 mg/L average relative risk, and above 3 mg/L higher relative risk. These categories do not diagnose infection or cardiovascular disease. Acute infection, injury, obesity, smoking, autoimmune inflammation, insomnia, depression, and other conditions can raise hs-CRP. Values above 10 mg/L should generally be repeated after the person is clinically stable rather than used directly for cardiovascular-risk classification. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item asking about infection, injury, inflammatory disease, obesity, smoking, and medicines;
                      \item repeating hs-CRP when it is above 10 mg/L or the person is not clinically stable;
                      \item assessing LDL-C, ApoB, Lp(a), blood pressure, diabetes, kidney disease, and smoking;
                      \item estimating overall cardiovascular risk; or
                      \item using hs-CRP only as an additional risk marker, not as a reason to diagnose infection or start treatment alone.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    hs-CRP is not a substitute for standard CRP when evaluating an acute infection or inflammatory flare. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to hs-CRP testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item intermediate or uncertain cardiovascular risk;
                      \item a family history of premature cardiovascular disease;
                      \item diabetes, obesity, smoking, or high blood pressure;
                      \item a recent cardiovascular-risk assessment with discordant findings;
                      \item known inflammatory disease when a stable risk assessment is needed; or
                      \item a high result that needs confirmation after an acute illness.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    hs-CRP itself causes no symptoms. Chest pain, sudden weakness, or sudden breathing difficulty requires urgent medical assessment and is not diagnosed by hs-CRP testing. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) is made by the pituitary gland and signals the thyroid to make T4 and T3. TSH is usually the first blood test used to assess thyroid function. A result outside the laboratory interval is interpreted with free T4, symptoms, medicines, pregnancy status, and the clinical setting. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK} and \href{https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-function-tests/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{History}
                    TSH testing developed from twentieth-century research on pituitary control of the thyroid and from increasingly sensitive immunoassays. The modern test has no single inventor or single FDA approval date as a biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to specific TSH reagents, analysers, and point-of-care systems. The development of modern thyroid-test interpretation is summarized by \href{https://www.thyroid.org/professionals/laboratory-services-library/standardization-and-harmonization/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly used adult interval is about 0.4--4.0 mIU/L, but age, pregnancy, time of day, assay, and laboratory interval matter. High TSH usually supports primary hypothyroidism when free T4 is low, while low TSH usually supports hyperthyroidism when free T4 or T3 is high. Low TSH with low free T4 can indicate pituitary or hypothalamic disease. Severe illness, medicines, pregnancy, and assay interference can alter interpretation. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking free T4 and, when hyperthyroidism is suspected, total or free T3;
                      \item repeating TSH after an appropriate interval when the result is mildly abnormal;
                      \item checking thyroid peroxidase, thyroglobulin, or TSH-receptor antibodies;
                      \item reviewing thyroid medicines, biotin, pregnancy, severe illness, and pituitary symptoms; or
                      \item using thyroid ultrasound or radionuclide imaging when a nodule or hyperthyroidism cause requires evaluation.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    TSH alone cannot identify the cause of an abnormal thyroid result. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to TSH testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue or unexplained weight change;
                      \item feeling unusually cold or hot;
                      \item constipation or diarrhea;
                      \item palpitations, tremor, or slow heartbeat;
                      \item anxiety, depression, or difficulty concentrating;
                      \item hair loss, dry skin, or increased sweating;
                      \item irregular or heavy menstrual bleeding or fertility difficulty;
                      \item neck swelling, a thyroid lump, or trouble swallowing; or
                      \item pregnancy, a family history of thyroid disease, or thyroid-treatment monitoring.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    TSH itself causes no symptoms. Severe palpitations, chest pain, confusion, fainting, or severe breathlessness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Free thyroxine (free T4)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Free thyroxine (free T4) measures the unbound portion of T4 that is available to enter tissues. T4 is made by the thyroid and is converted in some tissues to T3. Free T4 is usually interpreted with TSH to assess thyroid function and to investigate abnormal TSH results. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK} and \href{https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-function-tests/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{History}
                    Free-T4 testing developed after thyroid-hormone assays and methods for accounting for binding proteins improved. Modern immunoassays are method-specific and can be affected by unusual binding or assay interference. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``free T4''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and analysers. See \href{https://www.thyroid.org/professionals/laboratory-services-library/standardization-and-harmonization/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult free-T4 interval is approximately 0.8--1.8 ng/dL, but the assay-specific interval must be used. Low free T4 with high TSH supports primary hypothyroidism; low free T4 with a low or non-high TSH raises concern for central hypothyroidism or severe illness. High free T4 with low TSH supports thyrotoxicosis, but medicines, pregnancy-related changes, and assay interference can alter results. Free T4 is not a stand-alone measure of thyroid health. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking TSH and, when needed, total or free T3;
                      \item repeating the assay or using another method when results conflict with symptoms;
                      \item reviewing levothyroxine, antithyroid medicines, biotin, pregnancy, and severe illness;
                      \item checking thyroid antibodies; or
                      \item evaluating pituitary disease or thyroid structural disease with targeted imaging when indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Free T4 interpretation depends on TSH and the clinical context. See \href{https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-function-tests/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to free-T4 testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or unexplained weight change;
                      \item heat or cold intolerance;
                      \item palpitations, tremor, or slow heartbeat;
                      \item constipation or diarrhea;
                      \item hair loss, dry skin, or excessive sweating;
                      \item irregular menstrual periods or fertility difficulty;
                      \item neck swelling or a thyroid lump;
                      \item an abnormal TSH; or
                      \item monitoring thyroid replacement or antithyroid treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Free T4 itself causes no symptoms. Severe palpitations, chest pain, confusion, fainting, or severe breathlessness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total triiodothyronine (T3)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total triiodothyronine (T3) measures bound plus unbound T3 in blood. T3 is the more biologically active thyroid hormone, and most circulating T3 is produced by conversion of T4 outside the thyroid. T3 testing is mainly useful when hyperthyroidism is suspected, particularly when T4 is normal but TSH is low. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/triiodothyronine-t3-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    T3 testing developed from the identification of thyroid hormones and later hormone-binding and immunoassay methods. Modern assays are method-specific, and there is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``total T3.'' FDA authorization applies to specific T3 reagents and analysers. The modern clinical role of T3 testing is described by \href{https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-function-tests/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult total-T3 interval is approximately 80--200 ng/dL, but age, pregnancy, binding proteins, medicines, and the laboratory interval matter. High T3 with low TSH supports T3-predominant hyperthyroidism or excess replacement. Low T3 can occur in hypothyroidism but also in severe non-thyroidal illness, so T3 is rarely useful for diagnosing hypothyroidism alone. Free-T3 immunoassays may be less reliable than free-T4 assays. See \href{https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-function-tests/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking TSH and free T4;
                      \item repeating T3 when illness, medicines, or assay interference could explain the result;
                      \item checking thyroid antibodies when Graves disease or autoimmune thyroid disease is possible;
                      \item reviewing thyroid medicines and biotin; or
                      \item using thyroid imaging when the cause of thyrotoxicosis needs to be identified.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    T3 alone does not diagnose a thyroid disorder and is usually not the first test for hypothyroidism. See \href{https://www.thyroid.org/thyroid-function-tests/}{American Thyroid Association}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to total-T3 testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item weight loss despite usual eating;
                      \item heat intolerance or increased sweating;
                      \item palpitations, tremor, or anxiety;
                      \item frequent bowel movements or diarrhea;
                      \item muscle weakness or difficulty sleeping;
                      \item irregular menstrual periods;
                      \item eye prominence or neck swelling;
                      \item low TSH with normal free T4; or
                      \item monitoring antithyroid or thyroid-replacement treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    T3 itself causes no symptoms. Severe palpitations, chest pain, fainting, confusion, or severe breathlessness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diagnostic-tests/thyroid}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Parathyroid hormone (PTH)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Parathyroid hormone (PTH) is made by four small parathyroid glands in the neck. It raises blood calcium and helps regulate phosphate through effects on bone, kidneys, and vitamin-D activation. PTH must be interpreted with calcium, phosphate, vitamin D, kidney function, and sometimes magnesium. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/parathyroid-hormone-pth-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/primary-hyperparathyroidism}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    The role of PTH in calcium regulation was established in 1925 when James B. Collip prepared a biologically active parathyroid extract. This history is reviewed in \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/23374734/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``PTH''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common interval is about 10--65 pg/mL, but assay and laboratory interval matter. High PTH with high calcium supports primary hyperparathyroidism; high PTH with low or normal calcium may reflect vitamin-D deficiency, kidney disease, low calcium intake, or malabsorption. Low PTH with low calcium supports hypoparathyroidism, although severe magnesium abnormality can interfere. PTH alone cannot diagnose the cause of a calcium disorder. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/parathyroid-hormone-pth-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/primary-hyperparathyroidism}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating PTH with calcium, albumin, phosphate, magnesium, and creatinine;
                      \item checking 25-hydroxyvitamin D and urine calcium;
                      \item reviewing kidney disease, medicines, calcium intake, and vitamin-D intake;
                      \item measuring ionized calcium when total calcium may be misleading; or
                      \item assessing bone density, kidney stones, and parathyroid structure when indicated.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    PTH results must be interpreted as a calcium--PTH relationship, not as an isolated number. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/primary-hyperparathyroidism}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to PTH testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item kidney stones;
                      \item bone pain, osteoporosis, or fractures;
                      \item muscle weakness or cramps;
                      \item constipation, nausea, or abdominal pain;
                      \item excessive thirst or urination;
                      \item tingling, spasms, or seizures;
                      \item chronic kidney disease or vitamin-D deficiency; or
                      \item an abnormal calcium or phosphate result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    PTH itself causes no symptoms. Seizure, severe confusion, severe muscle spasm, or inability to urinate requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/parathyroid-hormone-pth-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Morning cortisol}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    A morning cortisol test measures cortisol in blood near the time it is normally highest. Cortisol is made by the adrenal glands and helps regulate stress responses, blood pressure, glucose, and metabolism. A morning value is used mainly when adrenal insufficiency is suspected; a single value is not a complete test for Cushing syndrome. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/adrenal-insufficiency-addisons-disease/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    Cortisol was isolated during twentieth-century adrenal-steroid research, particularly work by Edward Kendall and Tadeus Reichstein; the history of corticosteroid development is summarized at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22018177/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``cortisol''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    An illustrative 8 a.m. interval is about 5--25 micrograms/dL, but time, assay, binding proteins, medicines, pregnancy, stress, and the laboratory interval matter. Low morning cortisol can support adrenal insufficiency, while high cortisol can occur with stress, illness, steroid medicines, or Cushing syndrome. Cortisol follows a daily rhythm, so a random result is difficult to interpret. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/adrenal-insufficiency-addisons-disease/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating morning cortisol with simultaneous ACTH;
                      \item performing an ACTH stimulation test when adrenal insufficiency is suspected;
                      \item measuring renin, aldosterone, electrolytes, or adrenal antibodies when indicated;
                      \item using late-night salivary cortisol, 24-hour urine free cortisol, or dexamethasone suppression testing when Cushing syndrome is suspected; or
                      \item reviewing glucocorticoid medicines and recent severe illness.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    NIDDK identifies the ACTH stimulation test as the test most often used to diagnose adrenal insufficiency. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/adrenal-insufficiency-addisons-disease/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to morning-cortisol testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item persistent fatigue or muscle weakness;
                      \item weight loss or loss of appetite;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or abdominal pain;
                      \item dizziness or low blood pressure;
                      \item darkening of the skin;
                      \item low sodium or high potassium;
                      \item easy bruising, purple stretch marks, or proximal weakness;
                      \item diabetes or high blood pressure with suspected cortisol excess; or
                      \item long-term glucocorticoid use or recent withdrawal.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Cortisol itself causes no symptoms. Severe vomiting, fainting, confusion, severe weakness, or shock requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/adrenal-insufficiency-addisons-disease/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{ACTH}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH) is made by the pituitary gland and signals the adrenal glands to make cortisol. ACTH is usually measured together with cortisol to help distinguish a primary adrenal problem from a pituitary or hypothalamic problem. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/adrenocorticotropic-hormone-acth/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/adrenal-insufficiency-addisons-disease/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{History}
                    ACTH was isolated and characterized during twentieth-century adrenal-hormone research, alongside the isolation of cortisol by laboratories including Kendall and Reichstein. This history is summarized at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/22018177/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``ACTH''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    An illustrative 8 a.m. interval is about 10--60 pg/mL, but ACTH varies by time, stress, illness, medicines, assay, and laboratory. High ACTH with low cortisol supports primary adrenal insufficiency; low or normal ACTH with low cortisol supports central adrenal insufficiency. Low ACTH with high cortisol may occur in adrenal cortisol excess, while high ACTH with high cortisol may occur in pituitary or ectopic ACTH excess. ACTH must not be interpreted alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/adrenocorticotropic-hormone-acth/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/cushings-syndrome}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item measuring morning cortisol at the same time;
                      \item performing an ACTH stimulation test when adrenal insufficiency is suspected;
                      \item reviewing glucocorticoids and medicines that affect the hypothalamic--pituitary--adrenal axis;
                      \item using pituitary or adrenal imaging when biochemical results support it; or
                      \item using dexamethasone suppression, late-night salivary cortisol, urine cortisol, or CRH testing when cortisol excess is suspected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ACTH is a localization aid within an endocrine evaluation, not a stand-alone diagnosis. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/endocrine-diseases/adrenal-insufficiency-addisons-disease/diagnosis}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to ACTH testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or weight loss;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or abdominal pain;
                      \item dizziness, fainting, or low blood pressure;
                      \item darkening of the skin;
                      \item high blood pressure, easy bruising, or purple stretch marks;
                      \item proximal muscle weakness;
                      \item low sodium, high potassium, or abnormal cortisol;
                      \item a pituitary or adrenal mass; or
                      \item long-term glucocorticoid treatment or withdrawal.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    ACTH itself causes no symptoms. Severe vomiting, fainting, confusion, severe weakness, or shock requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/adrenocorticotropic-hormone-acth/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) is released by the pituitary gland. In ovaries it supports follicle development; in testes it supports sperm production. FSH is interpreted with LH, sex hormones, age, pubertal stage, menstrual-cycle day, pregnancy status, and fertility history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/follicle-stimulating-hormone-fsh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/001195.htm}{MedlinePlus Hypogonadism}.
                    \section{History}
                    FSH was identified through twentieth-century study of pituitary gonadotropins and reproductive physiology; modern immunoassays later enabled routine blood measurement. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``FSH''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassays and analysers. MedlinePlus describes its modern clinical uses at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/follicle-stimulating-hormone-fsh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    FSH has no single normal range. Values vary with sex, age, puberty, menstrual-cycle phase, pregnancy, menopause, and assay. High FSH may indicate reduced ovarian or testicular function, menopause, primary ovarian insufficiency, gonadal injury, or some chromosomal conditions. Low FSH may indicate hypothalamic or pituitary dysfunction, undernutrition, excessive exercise, chronic illness, pregnancy, or hormonal suppression. FSH alone cannot diagnose infertility, menopause, or a pituitary disorder. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/follicle-stimulating-hormone-fsh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking LH and estradiol, progesterone, testosterone, or prolactin;
                      \item checking pregnancy testing and thyroid function when relevant;
                      \item assessing semen analysis in men or ovarian reserve and pelvic evaluation in women;
                      \item reviewing medicines, nutrition, exercise, chronic illness, and puberty stage; or
                      \item considering pituitary imaging or genetic testing when other findings support it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    FSH must be timed and interpreted for the clinical question; cycle-day and age-specific laboratory intervals take priority. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/001195.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to FSH testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item irregular or absent menstrual periods;
                      \item hot flashes or vaginal dryness;
                      \item infertility or repeated pregnancy loss;
                      \item low sperm count or infertility in a man;
                      \item reduced libido or low testosterone;
                      \item delayed or early puberty;
                      \item suspected ovarian or testicular failure; or
                      \item monitoring fertility treatment or menopause-related evaluation.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    FSH itself causes no symptoms. Severe pelvic pain, heavy bleeding, fainting, or neurologic symptoms requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/follicle-stimulating-hormone-fsh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Luteinising hormone (LH)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Luteinizing hormone (LH) is released by the pituitary gland. In women it helps regulate the menstrual cycle and triggers ovulation; in men it stimulates testicular testosterone production. LH is interpreted with FSH, sex hormones, age, pubertal stage, menstrual-cycle day, and fertility history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/luteinizing-hormone-lh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    LH was identified through twentieth-century study of pituitary gonadotropins and reproductive physiology; routine clinical measurement followed the development of hormone immunoassays. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``LH''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassays and analysers. MedlinePlus describes its current uses at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/luteinizing-hormone-lh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    LH has no single normal range. Values vary with sex, age, puberty, menstrual-cycle phase, pregnancy, menopause, and assay. High LH may occur around ovulation, after menopause, or with primary ovarian or testicular failure. Low LH may indicate hypothalamic or pituitary dysfunction, undernutrition, excessive exercise, chronic illness, or hormonal suppression. LH alone cannot diagnose PCOS, infertility, or a pituitary disorder. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/luteinizing-hormone-lh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking FSH and estradiol, progesterone, testosterone, or prolactin;
                      \item checking pregnancy testing and thyroid function when relevant;
                      \item assessing semen analysis, ovulation, ovarian reserve, or pelvic findings;
                      \item reviewing medicines, nutrition, exercise, chronic illness, and puberty stage; or
                      \item considering pituitary imaging or genetic testing when other findings support it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    An LH value must be timed and interpreted for the clinical question. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/luteinizing-hormone-lh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to LH testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item irregular or absent menstrual periods;
                      \item infertility or difficulty identifying ovulation;
                      \item hot flashes or vaginal dryness;
                      \item low testosterone, low libido, or erectile difficulty;
                      \item low sperm count or infertility;
                      \item delayed or early puberty;
                      \item suspected ovarian or testicular failure; or
                      \item monitoring fertility treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    LH itself causes no symptoms. Severe pelvic pain, heavy bleeding, fainting, or neurologic symptoms requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/luteinizing-hormone-lh-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total testosterone}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total testosterone measures both protein-bound and unbound testosterone in blood. Testosterone is involved in sexual development, libido, muscle and bone health, sperm production in males, and red-cell production. The sample is commonly collected in the morning because levels are often highest then. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/testosterone-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Testosterone was isolated and chemically characterized during early twentieth-century sex-hormone research; routine total-testosterone testing followed the development of biological and immunologic assays. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``total testosterone''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and analysers. The current clinical use and timing are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/testosterone-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Illustrative adult intervals are about 300--1000 ng/dL in men and 15--70 ng/dL in women, but sex, age, time of collection, assay, SHBG, medicines, and the laboratory interval matter. Low testosterone may reflect testicular failure, pituitary or hypothalamic disease, obesity, illness, medicines, or aging; diagnosis generally requires compatible symptoms and repeated low morning results. High testosterone may reflect treatment, anabolic steroids, ovarian or adrenal androgen excess, tumors, or assay interference. Total testosterone alone cannot identify the cause. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/testosterone-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/001195.htm}{MedlinePlus Hypogonadism}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating an early-morning total testosterone;
                      \item measuring free testosterone, SHBG, albumin, LH, FSH, and prolactin when appropriate;
                      \item checking thyroid, iron, CBC, liver, and kidney tests when indicated;
                      \item assessing semen analysis or ovarian/adrenal androgen tests according to sex and symptoms; or
                      \item using pituitary, testicular, ovarian, or adrenal imaging when biochemical findings support it.
                    \end{enumerate}
