                    MedlinePlus emphasizes that testosterone alone cannot diagnose a condition. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/testosterone-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to testosterone testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reduced libido or erectile difficulty;
                      \item infertility or low sperm count;
                      \item loss of body hair, reduced muscle, or low bone density;
                      \item fatigue or anemia without another explanation;
                      \item delayed or early puberty;
                      \item irregular periods, acne, excess facial hair, or scalp hair loss;
                      \item suspected testicular, ovarian, adrenal, or pituitary disease; or
                      \item monitoring testosterone treatment or anabolic-steroid exposure.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Testosterone itself causes no symptoms. Severe pelvic or testicular pain, sudden neurologic symptoms, or severe bleeding requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/testosterone-levels-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Fasting insulin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Fasting insulin measures insulin in blood after fasting. Insulin lowers blood glucose by helping cells take up and store glucose. The result is most useful with simultaneous glucose and, in selected cases, C-peptide; insulin alone does not diagnose insulin resistance or diabetes. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/insulin-in-blood/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Insulin was discovered through the Toronto work of Frederick Banting and Charles Best in 1921, with important contributions from John Macleod and James Collip. FDA records describe this history at \href{https://www.fda.gov/about-fda/fda-history-exhibits/100-years-insulin}{FDA}. The modern blood assay has no single FDA approval date; FDA authorization applies to particular insulin reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A fasting interval of about 2--25 microIU/mL is often quoted, but assay and laboratory intervals vary. High insulin with normal or high glucose may occur with insulin resistance, obesity, medicines, or endogenous hyperinsulinism. Low insulin may occur with beta-cell failure or exogenous insulin use. Insulin changes quickly, is affected by meals and stress, and is difficult to interpret without glucose and C-peptide. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/insulin-in-blood/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking simultaneous glucose and C-peptide;
                      \item reviewing diabetes medicines, injected insulin, fasting duration, and symptoms;
                      \item checking HbA1c, kidney and liver tests, and diabetes autoantibodies when indicated;
                      \item using supervised testing during documented hypoglycemia; or
                      \item considering imaging when endogenous insulin excess suggests an insulin-producing tumor.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Insulin results alone cannot diagnose insulin resistance or an insulinoma. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/insulin-in-blood/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to fasting-insulin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item recurrent sweating, shaking, hunger, or palpitations;
                      \item confusion, seizure, or loss of consciousness during low glucose;
                      \item obesity or features of insulin resistance;
                      \item diabetes with uncertain type;
                      \item unexpectedly low or high glucose;
                      \item suspected insulin-producing tumor;
                      \item monitoring a metabolic or diabetes treatment; or
                      \item exposure to injected insulin or glucose-lowering medicines.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe confusion, seizure, or loss of consciousness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diabetes/overview/preventing-problems/low-blood-glucose-hypoglycemia}{NIDDK}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{C-peptide}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    C-peptide is released in equal amounts with the body's own insulin when proinsulin is split. Because injected insulin does not contain C-peptide and C-peptide remains in blood longer than insulin, the test helps estimate the pancreas's endogenous insulin production. It is interpreted with simultaneous glucose and treatment history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-peptide-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    C-peptide emerged from the 1960s--1970s biochemical work showing how proinsulin is processed; Donald Steiner is closely associated with the discovery and characterization of proinsulin and C-peptide. This history is reviewed at \href{https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC2396242/}{PMC}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to specific C-peptide assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common fasting interval is approximately 0.8--3.5 ng/mL, but the laboratory interval and whether the sample was fasting or stimulated must be used. Low C-peptide with high glucose supports low endogenous insulin production, as in advanced type 1 diabetes or beta-cell failure. High C-peptide with high glucose supports endogenous insulin production with insulin resistance; high C-peptide during hypoglycemia can support inappropriate endogenous insulin secretion. Kidney failure can raise C-peptide because clearance is reduced. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-peptide-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking simultaneous glucose and insulin;
                      \item repeating fasting or stimulated C-peptide when timing or treatment affects interpretation;
                      \item checking diabetes autoantibodies, HbA1c, kidney function, and liver tests;
                      \item reviewing injected insulin and glucose-lowering medicines; or
                      \item performing supervised hypoglycemia evaluation and pancreatic imaging when endogenous hyperinsulinism is suspected.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    C-peptide is a measure of endogenous insulin production, not a direct measure of insulin sensitivity. See \href{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/diabetes/overview/what-is-diabetes/type-1-diabetes}{NIDDK}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to C-peptide testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item uncertain distinction between type 1 and type 2 diabetes;
                      \item recurrent sweating, shaking, hunger, or palpitations;
                      \item confusion, seizure, or loss of consciousness during low glucose;
                      \item diabetes treated with insulin;
                      \item suspected insulin-producing tumor;
                      \item unexpected loss of glucose control;
                      \item possible beta-cell failure; or
                      \item monitoring treatment after pancreatic disease or surgery.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe confusion, seizure, or loss of consciousness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-peptide-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Prolactin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Prolactin is a hormone made by the pituitary gland. It supports breast development and milk production during pregnancy and after birth. In nonpregnant people, an abnormal prolactin result can affect reproductive hormones and may point to a pituitary, thyroid, kidney, liver, or medicine-related problem. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prolactin-levels/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Prolactin was recognized as a distinct pituitary hormone in animal studies in the early 1930s, and human prolactin was later purified and distinguished from growth hormone. This history is described at \href{https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/7768067/}{PubMed}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``prolactin''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Prolactin varies by sex, pregnancy, breastfeeding, time of day, stress, exercise, medicines, and assay. High prolactin may occur with pregnancy, breastfeeding, prolactinoma, hypothyroidism, kidney or liver disease, chest-wall stimulation, or medicines. Low prolactin is uncommon and may reflect pituitary damage, but it is usually less clinically important. A mildly high result often needs repeat early-morning testing and consideration of macroprolactin. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prolactin-levels/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating prolactin after rest and according to laboratory timing instructions;
                      \item checking pregnancy, TSH, kidney and liver tests, and medicines;
                      \item testing for macroprolactin when symptoms and the result do not match;
                      \item checking LH, FSH, estradiol, testosterone, and other pituitary hormones; or
                      \item pituitary MRI when persistent elevation and the clinical picture suggest a prolactinoma or other pituitary lesion.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Prolactin alone cannot identify the cause of an elevation. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prolactin-levels/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to prolactin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item irregular or absent menstrual periods;
                      \item infertility;
                      \item unexpected milk production;
                      \item low libido or erectile difficulty;
                      \item breast enlargement;
                      \item headache or visual-field changes;
                      \item low testosterone or other low pituitary hormones; or
                      \item monitoring a known prolactinoma.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Sudden severe headache, new visual loss, confusion, or weakness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/prolactin-levels/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Ferritin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Ferritin is a protein that stores iron. A ferritin blood test estimates stored iron, but ferritin also rises as part of the body's response to inflammation. It is one of the most useful first tests when iron deficiency or iron overload is being considered. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/ferritin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus Iron Tests}.
                    \section{History}
                    Ferritin was identified as an intracellular iron-storage protein in twentieth-century biochemical research; clinical immunoassays later made serum ferritin measurement practical. The test has no single FDA approval date as a biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to specific ferritin reagents and analysers. MedlinePlus describes its current clinical role at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/ferritin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Ferritin ranges vary by sex, age, inflammation, liver disease, and laboratory method. Low ferritin strongly supports depleted iron stores, although inflammation can sometimes make a low result less obvious. High ferritin may reflect inflammation, infection, liver disease, obesity, malignancy, iron overload, or recent iron treatment; it does not prove iron overload. Serum iron, TIBC, transferrin saturation, CBC, and the clinical picture are needed. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking CBC, MCV, RDW, serum iron, TIBC, and transferrin saturation;
                      \item checking CRP or another inflammation marker when ferritin is high;
                      \item looking for menstrual, gastrointestinal, nutritional, or other blood loss;
                      \item checking liver tests and transfusion or iron-treatment history; or
                      \item considering genetic iron-overload testing when the complete pattern supports it.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Ferritin alone cannot identify the cause of anemia or high iron stores. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/ferritin-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to ferritin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or shortness of breath;
                      \item dizziness, headache, or palpitations;
                      \item pale skin or brittle nails;
                      \item unusual cravings such as ice or nonfood substances;
                      \item heavy menstrual bleeding or suspected gastrointestinal blood loss;
                      \item joint pain, abdominal pain, or liver abnormalities;
                      \item a family history of iron overload; or
                      \item an abnormal CBC or iron panel.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Ferritin itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathlessness, chest pain, fainting, or black or bloody stool requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Serum iron}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Serum iron measures iron circulating in blood, much of it bound to transferrin at the time of collection. It changes during the day and after food or supplements, so it should be interpreted with ferritin, TIBC, transferrin saturation, and the CBC. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Serum-iron measurement developed from chemical methods for quantifying iron in biological fluids and later became part of automated iron panels. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``serum iron''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers. MedlinePlus describes the test at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003488.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Illustrative intervals are about 59--158 micrograms/dL in men and 37--145 micrograms/dL in women, but the laboratory interval and collection conditions matter. Low serum iron may occur with iron deficiency, blood loss, pregnancy, malabsorption, or inflammation. High serum iron may follow recent iron ingestion and occur with iron overload, hemolysis, liver injury, poisoning, ineffective erythropoiesis, or transfusion. A single serum-iron value is not enough to diagnose deficiency or overload. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003488.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking ferritin, TIBC, transferrin saturation, CBC, and reticulocytes;
                      \item repeating the test under the laboratory's timing and preparation instructions;
                      \item checking CRP, liver, kidney, and nutritional findings;
                      \item investigating menstrual or gastrointestinal blood loss; or
                      \item assessing transfusion, iron-treatment, poisoning, or inherited iron overload.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Serum iron must be interpreted as part of an iron panel. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to serum-iron testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue or weakness;
                      \item shortness of breath, dizziness, or palpitations;
                      \item pale skin or brittle nails;
                      \item unusual cravings for ice or nonfood substances;
                      \item heavy menstrual bleeding or suspected gastrointestinal blood loss;
                      \item liver disease, joint pain, or abdominal pain;
                      \item repeated transfusions or iron treatment; or
                      \item an abnormal ferritin, CBC, or iron-panel result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Serum iron itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathlessness, chest pain, fainting, or suspected iron poisoning requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Total iron-binding capacity (TIBC)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Total iron-binding capacity (TIBC) estimates how much additional iron can bind to transferrin in blood. It is an indirect measure of transferrin availability and is interpreted with ferritin, serum iron, and transferrin saturation. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    TIBC developed from laboratory methods that measure the iron-binding capacity of serum and became part of standard iron studies. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``TIBC''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003488.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common interval is approximately 250--450 micrograms/dL, but the laboratory interval must be used. High TIBC commonly supports iron deficiency because more transferrin is available to bind iron. Low TIBC may occur with inflammation, malnutrition, liver disease, protein loss, or reduced transferrin production and can complicate iron-deficiency interpretation. TIBC is not interpreted alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking ferritin, serum iron, transferrin saturation, and CBC;
                      \item checking CRP, albumin, liver tests, and kidney tests;
                      \item repeating the panel when timing, illness, or treatment affects results;
                      \item investigating blood loss, diet, malabsorption, or inflammation; or
                      \item assessing iron overload when TIBC is low and transferrin saturation is high.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    TIBC is a binding-capacity measure and does not diagnose anemia by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to TIBC testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or shortness of breath;
                      \item dizziness, headache, or palpitations;
                      \item pale skin or brittle nails;
                      \item heavy menstrual bleeding or suspected gastrointestinal blood loss;
                      \item chronic inflammation, liver disease, or malnutrition;
                      \item repeated transfusions or iron therapy; or
                      \item an abnormal CBC, ferritin, or serum-iron result.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    TIBC itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathlessness, chest pain, fainting, or black or bloody stool requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Transferrin saturation (TSAT)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Transferrin saturation (TSAT) is calculated as serum iron divided by TIBC, multiplied by 100. It estimates the percentage of transferrin-binding sites occupied by iron. TSAT is interpreted with ferritin, CBC, inflammation, liver tests, and treatment history. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003488.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    TSAT became a practical clinical index after serum-iron and iron-binding-capacity measurements were combined into routine iron studies. There is no single FDA approval date for the calculation ``TSAT''; FDA authorization applies to the underlying iron and TIBC assays. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Illustrative intervals are about 20--50\% in men and 15--45\% in women, but the laboratory interval and collection conditions matter. TSAT below about 20\% supports insufficient circulating iron for red-cell production, especially with low ferritin, but inflammation can also lower it. TSAT above about 45--50\% may suggest iron overload, but recent iron intake, liver injury, hemolysis, transfusion, and ineffective erythropoiesis can alter it. Confirmation usually requires repeat testing and ferritin. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003488.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating serum iron and TIBC with ferritin;
                      \item checking CBC, reticulocytes, CRP, liver, kidney, and nutritional findings;
                      \item reviewing recent iron ingestion, transfusions, and iron treatment;
                      \item investigating menstrual or gastrointestinal blood loss; or
                      \item considering hereditary hemochromatosis testing when high TSAT persists.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    TSAT is an iron-balance index and is not diagnostic by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to TSAT testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or shortness of breath;
                      \item pallor, dizziness, headache, or palpitations;
                      \item heavy menstrual bleeding or suspected gastrointestinal blood loss;
                      \item joint pain, abdominal pain, or liver abnormalities;
                      \item repeated transfusions or iron treatment;
                      \item a family history of iron overload; or
                      \item an abnormal ferritin, serum iron, TIBC, or CBC.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    TSAT itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathlessness, chest pain, fainting, black stool, or suspected iron poisoning requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{25-hydroxyvitamin D3}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    The 25-hydroxyvitamin D [25(OH)D] test measures the main circulating storage form of vitamin D. It is the preferred blood marker for vitamin-D status; it is different from the active 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D test. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/vitamin-d-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND/HealthProfessional/}{NIH Office of Dietary Supplements}.
                    \section{History}
                    Vitamin D and its metabolites were identified during twentieth-century research into rickets and calcium metabolism. Measurement of 25(OH)D developed as methods for vitamin-D metabolites improved. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``25(OH)D''; FDA authorization applies to specific immunoassay or chromatography-based systems. NIH describes current assay variability at \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND/HealthProfessional/}{ODS}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    NIH guidance considers below 12 ng/mL (30 nmol/L) associated with deficiency, 12 to below 20 ng/mL generally inadequate, and 20 ng/mL or more adequate for most people. Values above 50 ng/mL may be associated with adverse effects. Assays vary, optimal concentrations are not established for every person, and routine testing is not recommended for every healthy adult. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND/HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item reviewing diet, sun exposure, supplements, malabsorption, liver disease, kidney disease, and medicines;
                      \item checking calcium, phosphate, PTH, alkaline phosphatase, and kidney function;
                      \item repeating 25(OH)D after a treatment interval when monitoring is clinically indicated;
                      \item assessing bone density or imaging when bone disease is suspected; or
                      \item investigating hypercalcemia when 25(OH)D is very high.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    25(OH)D should not be interpreted or treated using a result alone. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/vitamin-d-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to 25(OH)D testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item bone pain or fractures;
                      \item muscle weakness or cramps;
                      \item poor bone mineralization or osteoporosis;
                      \item malabsorption or bariatric surgery;
                      \item chronic kidney or liver disease;
                      \item limited sun exposure or high-risk nutritional status;
                      \item monitoring treatment for documented deficiency; or
                      \item excessive vitamin-D supplementation or unexplained hypercalcemia.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    25(OH)D itself causes no symptoms. Severe weakness, confusion, vomiting, dehydration, or an irregular heartbeat with high calcium requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND/HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Vitamin B12}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Vitamin B12 testing measures cobalamin in blood. Vitamin B12 is needed for DNA synthesis, healthy red-blood-cell formation, and normal nervous-system function. A blood level is useful but may not completely reflect tissue status, especially when the result is borderline or symptoms are strong. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Vitaminb12-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003705.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Vitamin B12 was identified during the investigation of pernicious anemia, and its isolation and structure were established in twentieth-century nutritional biochemistry. The modern clinical test has no single FDA approval date; FDA authorization applies to specific cobalamin assays and analysers. NIH summarizes the current biological role and confirmatory markers at \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Vitaminb12-HealthProfessional/}{ODS}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common serum interval is about 200--900 pg/mL, but the laboratory interval and clinical context matter. Low B12 may cause megaloblastic anemia, glossitis, numbness, tingling, balance problems, cognitive change, or neuropathy. High B12 commonly reflects supplementation but can also occur with liver disease, kidney disease, inflammation, or malignancy. Methylmalonic acid and homocysteine can support deficiency, but kidney disease and other conditions affect these markers. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Vitaminb12-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking CBC, MCV, reticulocytes, folate, and blood smear;
                      \item measuring methylmalonic acid and homocysteine when B12 status is uncertain;
                      \item reviewing diet, metformin, acid-suppressing medicines, gastrointestinal surgery, and malabsorption;
                      \item testing for intrinsic-factor or parietal-cell antibodies when pernicious anemia is possible; or
                      \item evaluating liver, kidney, inflammatory, or malignant causes of unexplained high B12.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Serum B12 alone cannot identify the cause of deficiency or a high value. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003705.htm}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to B12 testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or pallor;
                      \item sore or smooth tongue;
                      \item numbness, tingling, or burning sensations;
                      \item balance or walking problems;
                      \item memory, mood, or concentration changes;
                      \item unexplained macrocytosis or anemia;
                      \item vegan diet, malabsorption, or gastrointestinal surgery; or
                      \item long-term metformin or acid-suppressing treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Vitamin B12 itself causes no symptoms. New severe weakness, confusion, inability to walk, or shortness of breath requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Vitaminb12-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Folate}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Folate testing measures folate, a B vitamin needed for DNA synthesis and red-blood-cell production. Folate deficiency can produce megaloblastic anemia and is especially important before and during pregnancy because adequate folate reduces neural-tube-defect risk. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Folate-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/vitamin-b-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Folate was identified through research into nutritional causes of megaloblastic anemia in the twentieth century; the term folate reflects its presence in leafy plant foods. The clinical test has no single FDA approval date; FDA authorization applies to specific serum or red-cell folate assays and analysers. Current functions and deficiency information are summarized by \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Folate-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A serum folate value above about 4 ng/mL is often used as a general reference, but the laboratory interval and specimen type matter. Low folate may cause megaloblastic anemia, glossitis, fatigue, and elevated homocysteine, and may result from poor intake, alcohol use, malabsorption, or medicines. High folate usually reflects supplementation. Folate treatment can improve anemia while failing to correct neurologic injury from an unrecognized B12 deficiency, so B12 should be considered before treating unexplained megaloblastic anemia. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Folate-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking CBC, MCV, reticulocytes, blood smear, and B12;
                      \item checking homocysteine when deficiency is uncertain;
                      \item reviewing diet, alcohol, malabsorption, pregnancy, and medicines;
                      \item checking methylmalonic acid when B12 deficiency needs distinction; or
                      \item assessing gastrointestinal disease or other causes of malabsorption.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Folate alone should not be used to explain every macrocytic anemia. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Folate-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to folate testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fatigue, weakness, or pallor;
                      \item sore tongue or mouth ulcers;
                      \item shortness of breath or palpitations from anemia;
                      \item unexplained macrocytosis;
                      \item poor diet or heavy alcohol use;
                      \item malabsorption or gastrointestinal surgery;
                      \item pregnancy or plans for pregnancy; or
                      \item treatment with medicines that interfere with folate metabolism.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Folate itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathlessness, chest pain, fainting, or rapidly worsening neurologic symptoms requires prompt medical assessment. See \href{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/Folate-HealthProfessional/}{NIH ODS}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{C-reactive protein (CRP)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    C-reactive protein (CRP) is a liver-made acute-phase protein that rises with many types of inflammation. The test can show that inflammation is present, but it usually cannot identify its location or cause. Standard CRP and hs-CRP are different uses of CRP measurement. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    CRP was first described in the 1930s as a serum protein reacting with the C-polysaccharide of pneumococcus. It later became a general laboratory marker of inflammation. The historical and clinical distinction between CRP and hs-CRP is described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003356.htm}{MedlinePlus}. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``CRP''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common standard-CRP reference is below 5 mg/L, but the laboratory interval and clinical setting matter. High CRP may occur with infection, autoimmune inflammation, tissue injury, obesity, or cancer; it is not specific for bacterial infection. Low CRP means little measurable CRP at that time but does not exclude localized or early disease. CRP should be interpreted with symptoms and targeted tests. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating CRP to follow a known inflammatory or infectious process;
                      \item checking CBC, ESR, cultures, urinalysis, imaging, or pathogen-specific tests according to symptoms;
                      \item reviewing autoimmune symptoms, medicines, obesity, smoking, and recent injury;
                      \item checking organ-specific tests when CRP is high; or
                      \item using hs-CRP only for stable cardiovascular-risk assessment, not acute infection.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    CRP does not identify the cause or location of inflammation by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to CRP testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fever or chills;
                      \item pain, swelling, redness, or warmth;
                      \item fatigue or weakness;
                      \item cough or breathing difficulty;
                      \item abdominal pain, vomiting, or diarrhea;
                      \item painful urination;
                      \item joint pain or rash; or
                      \item monitoring a known infection, autoimmune disease, or inflammatory condition.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    CRP itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathing difficulty, confusion, fainting, severe pain, or rapidly worsening infection requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Procalcitonin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Procalcitonin is a peptide that is normally present at very low levels but can rise when many tissues respond to a serious bacterial infection. The test is mainly used in very ill people to support assessment of sepsis and, with other evidence, to guide some antibiotic decisions. It does not replace examination, cultures, imaging, or clinical judgment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/procalcitonin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Procalcitonin testing developed from research into calcitonin and the tissue response to systemic bacterial infection. Its clinical use expanded with sensitive immunoassays for sepsis assessment. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``procalcitonin''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and intended uses. MedlinePlus describes the current test at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/procalcitonin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A healthy-adult value is often below 0.05 ng/mL, but assay, illness stage, kidney function, trauma, surgery, burns, and other conditions matter. High procalcitonin can support serious bacterial infection or sepsis but can also rise without infection. Low procalcitonin lowers the probability of some systemic bacterial infections but does not exclude early, localized, viral, or atypical infection. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/procalcitonin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating procalcitonin to assess the trend when clinically appropriate;
                      \item checking cultures, CBC, CRP, lactate, kidney function, and organ function;
                      \item obtaining imaging or source-specific tests;
                      \item reviewing recent surgery, trauma, burns, shock, kidney disease, and medicines; or
                      \item deciding on antibiotics from the whole clinical picture rather than the result alone.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Procalcitonin cannot by itself prove or exclude sepsis. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/procalcitonin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to procalcitonin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item fever or chills;
                      \item confusion or unusual drowsiness;
                      \item fast heartbeat or breathing;
                      \item low blood pressure or fainting;
                      \item extreme pain or severe weakness;
                      \item reduced urine;
                      \item a suspected serious bacterial infection; or
                      \item monitoring severe infection or sepsis treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Sepsis is an emergency. Confusion, severe breathing difficulty, fainting, blue or clammy skin, or markedly reduced urine requires immediate medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/procalcitonin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{High-sensitivity troponin I or T}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    High-sensitivity cardiac troponin I or T measures a small amount of a contractile protein released when heart muscle cells are injured. It is the preferred blood biomarker for suspected acute myocardial infarction, but an elevated result means myocardial injury, not automatically a heart attack. Diagnosis requires the timing and change in troponin plus symptoms, ECG, imaging, or other evidence of ischemia. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/troponin-test/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    Cardiac troponin testing developed after cardiac muscle proteins were identified and antibodies made it possible to distinguish cardiac injury from skeletal-muscle injury. High-sensitivity assays later improved detection of small concentrations. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``cardiac troponin''; FDA authorization applies to specific assays and analysers.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    The decision limit is the assay-specific 99th percentile, not one universal number. A rise or fall with at least one value above that limit supports acute myocardial injury; ischemia is needed to call it myocardial infarction. High troponin can also occur with myocarditis, heart failure, pulmonary embolism, kidney disease, sepsis, tachyarrhythmia, or strenuous illness. A low serial result can make acute infarction less likely when timing is appropriate. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}{NHLBI} and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/troponin-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating high-sensitivity troponin at an assay-specific interval;
                      \item obtaining an ECG and assessing symptoms and cardiovascular risk;
                      \item checking kidney function, CBC, electrolytes, CK, and other tests for alternative injury;
                      \item using echocardiography, coronary imaging, or other imaging when indicated; or
                      \item evaluating pulmonary embolism, myocarditis, heart failure, sepsis, or arrhythmia when the pattern does not fit infarction.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Troponin should not be interpreted without timing, ECG, symptoms, and the serial change. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to troponin testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item chest pressure, pain, or tightness;
                      \item pain spreading to the arm, shoulder, back, neck, or jaw;
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item sweating, nausea, or vomiting;
                      \item fainting, marked weakness, or unusual fatigue;
                      \item rapid or irregular heartbeat;
                      \item suspected myocarditis, pulmonary embolism, or sepsis; or
                      \item monitoring after a cardiac procedure or known myocardial injury.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Troponin itself causes no symptoms. New chest pressure, severe breathlessness, fainting, or sudden weakness requires immediate emergency assessment. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Creatine kinase (CK)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Creatine kinase (CK) is an enzyme found mainly in skeletal muscle, with smaller amounts in heart and brain. Muscle-cell injury can release CK into blood. The test is mainly used for skeletal-muscle injury and disease; troponin is preferred when heart-muscle injury is being evaluated. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    CK testing developed from enzyme chemistry and later separation of CK isoenzymes. Automated CK assays became part of clinical laboratory testing. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``CK''; FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers. MedlinePlus describes the current assay and its limitations at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Illustrative adult ranges are about 39--308 U/L in men and 26--192 U/L in women, but age, sex, muscle mass, exercise, race/ethnicity, assay, and laboratory interval matter. High CK may occur after strenuous exercise, trauma, seizures, medicines such as statins, myositis, muscular dystrophy, or rhabdomyolysis. CK can rise late after injury and does not identify its source or cause without clinical context. Low CK is usually not clinically important. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeating CK after rest when exercise or specimen timing may explain elevation;
                      \item checking creatinine, potassium, phosphate, calcium, urine, and ECG when rhabdomyolysis is possible;
                      \item reviewing statins, other medicines, trauma, seizures, and exercise;
                      \item checking CK isoenzymes, aldolase, autoimmune tests, or genetic testing for selected muscle disorders; or
                      \item measuring troponin rather than relying on CK for suspected heart injury.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    CK alone cannot diagnose rhabdomyolysis or identify the injured tissue. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to CK testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item muscle pain, cramps, or tenderness;
                      \item muscle weakness;
                      \item dark or cola-colored urine;
                      \item falls or difficulty climbing stairs;
                      \item swollen legs or feet;
                      \item recent trauma, burns, seizure, or extreme exercise;
                      \item statin or other potentially muscle-toxic medicine; or
                      \item suspected inflammatory or inherited muscle disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe muscle pain, dark urine, markedly reduced urine, chest pain, or severe weakness requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP) is a hormone released when the heart walls are stretched or under pressure. A BNP blood test helps assess whether symptoms may be due to heart failure, but it is interpreted with examination, ECG, imaging, kidney function, age, rhythm, and body weight. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    BNP was identified through research into cardiac natriuretic hormones in the late twentieth century, and blood immunoassays later made it clinically available. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``BNP''; FDA authorization applies to specific BNP assays and analysers. MedlinePlus describes the current use of BNP and NT-proBNP at \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A BNP below about 100 pg/mL can make acute heart failure less likely in many settings, but assay and clinical context matter. High BNP may support heart failure or cardiac wall stress, but it can also occur with kidney disease, pulmonary disease, age, atrial fibrillation, or other cardiac conditions. Obesity can lower BNP. BNP does not by itself diagnose heart failure or show its type. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item checking ECG, chest imaging, oxygen level, kidney function, and electrolytes;
                      \item obtaining echocardiography to assess heart structure and pumping;
                      \item reviewing age, obesity, atrial fibrillation, kidney disease, lung disease, and medicines;
                      \item comparing BNP with prior values when monitoring known heart failure; or
                      \item evaluating other causes of breathlessness when BNP and symptoms do not agree.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    BNP is a supporting biomarker, not a substitute for cardiac imaging and clinical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms and situations that may lead to BNP testing include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath, especially when lying down;
                      \item waking at night gasping for air;
                      \item swelling of the feet, ankles, or abdomen;
                      \item rapid weight gain from fluid;
                      \item fatigue or reduced exercise tolerance;
                      \item cough or a feeling of chest congestion;
                      \item known heart failure or heart disease; or
                      \item monitoring response to heart-failure treatment.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    BNP itself causes no symptoms. Severe breathlessness, chest pain, fainting, blue lips, or rapidly worsening swelling requires urgent medical assessment. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{CK-MB}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    CK-MB is one form of creatine kinase associated more with heart muscle than some other tissues. It can rise after heart-muscle injury, but skeletal-muscle injury can also raise it. Current practice generally prefers cardiac troponin because it is more specific for myocardial injury. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus} and \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                    \section{History}
                    CK isoenzyme testing was developed to improve the interpretation of total CK. CK-MB became an earlier cardiac marker before cardiac troponin assays became widely available. There is no single FDA approval date for ``CK-MB'' as a biological concept; authorization applies to individual assay systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A commonly used reference limit is below about 5 ng/mL, but the laboratory's assay-specific interval takes precedence. A high CK-MB may occur with myocardial injury, skeletal-muscle injury, surgery, or kidney disease. CK-MB is not sufficient to diagnose myocardial infarction; symptoms, ECG, serial troponin, and evidence of ischemia are more important. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item serial high-sensitivity troponin and ECG;
                      \item total CK and assessment for skeletal-muscle injury;
                      \item kidney function, electrolytes, and urine when muscle breakdown is possible;
                      \item echocardiography or coronary imaging when indicated; or
                      \item review of exercise, trauma, surgery, medicines, and timing of symptoms.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item chest pain, pressure, or tightness;
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item sweating, nausea, or fainting;
                      \item unexplained muscle pain or weakness;
                      \item recent surgery, trauma, or intense exercise; or
                      \item a suspected or monitored heart-muscle injury.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    A CK-MB result requires urgent clinical interpretation when chest pain or severe breathlessness is present. See \href{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}{NHLBI}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{N-terminal pro-BNP (NT-proBNP)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    NT-proBNP is an inactive fragment released when heart muscle is stretched. The blood test helps assess possible heart failure and is interpreted with symptoms, examination, ECG, imaging, kidney function, age, rhythm, and body weight. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    NT-proBNP testing followed the discovery of cardiac natriuretic hormones and the development of immunoassays. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to each named assay and analyser.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    In many outpatient settings, NT-proBNP below about 125 pg/mL makes chronic heart failure less likely; acute-care cutoffs are age- and setting-dependent. High values may occur with heart failure or other cardiac strain, but also with kidney disease, age, atrial fibrillation, and lung disease. Obesity can lower the result. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include ECG, chest imaging, oxygen measurement, kidney and electrolyte tests, echocardiography, review of rhythm and medicines, and comparison with earlier results. Other causes of breathlessness are considered if the result and symptoms do not agree.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath during activity or at rest;
                      \item difficulty breathing when lying flat;
                      \item waking at night gasping for air;
                      \item swelling of the feet, ankles, or abdomen;
                      \item rapid weight gain from fluid;
                      \item fatigue or reduced exercise tolerance; or
                      \item known heart failure requiring monitoring.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Myoglobin}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Myoglobin is an oxygen-binding protein in skeletal and heart muscle. Injury can release it into blood, and the kidneys remove it into urine. It can rise early after muscle injury, but it cannot reliably distinguish heart from skeletal muscle injury. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Myoglobin was recognized as a muscle oxygen-binding protein before laboratory immunoassays allowed its measurement in blood. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement; FDA authorization applies to specific test systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Illustrative adult serum intervals are about 28--72 ng/mL in men and 25--58 ng/mL in women, but method and laboratory ranges vary. A high result may occur after trauma, strenuous exercise, seizures, surgery, myocardial injury, or rhabdomyolysis. A low result is usually not clinically important. CK and clinical assessment are generally more useful for muscle injury, while troponin is preferred for suspected heart injury.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include repeat CK, creatinine, potassium, phosphate, calcium, urine testing, ECG, and review of trauma, exercise, seizures, and medicines. Persistent weakness may lead to specialist muscle testing, autoimmune tests, or genetic testing.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item muscle pain or tenderness;
                      \item muscle weakness;
                      \item dark or cola-colored urine;
                      \item reduced urine output;
                      \item recent trauma, burn, seizure, or extreme exercise; or
                      \item chest pain or suspected muscle breakdown.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Severe muscle pain, dark urine, or reduced urine requires urgent medical assessment.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Immunoglobulin G (IgG)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    IgG is the most common antibody in blood and helps provide longer-term protection against germs. Quantitative IgG measures its concentration; it does not identify a specific infection by itself. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/immunoglobulins-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    IgG was distinguished from other antibody classes during twentieth-century immunology. Modern nephelometric and immunochemical assays quantify it; FDA authorization applies to individual systems, not to one universal ``IgG test'' date.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult interval is about 700--1600 mg/dL, but age and laboratory method matter. Low IgG can occur with antibody deficiency, protein loss, malnutrition, some medicines, or blood cancers. High IgG can occur with chronic infection, autoimmune disease, liver disease, or a monoclonal gammopathy; a total result cannot distinguish polyclonal from monoclonal increase.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include repeat quantitative immunoglobulins, serum protein electrophoresis, immunofixation, free light chains, vaccine-response testing, CBC, kidney/liver tests, and evaluation for protein loss or recurrent infection.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeated sinus, ear, chest, or other infections;
                      \item infections that are unusually severe or prolonged;
                      \item unexplained weight loss or night sweats;
                      \item bone pain or anemia;
                      \item swelling or protein loss; or
                      \item suspected autoimmune disease or immune deficiency.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Immunoglobulin A (IgA)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    IgA is an antibody important in the lining of the airways and digestive tract. Blood IgA testing helps assess antibody deficiency and some immune or inflammatory disorders; it is not the same as a disease-specific IgA antibody test. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/immunoglobulins-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    IgA was identified as a distinct antibody class in twentieth-century immunology. Current quantitative assays use immunochemical methods; FDA authorization belongs to specific test systems rather than to one universal approval date.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult interval is about 70--400 mg/dL, though age and method matter. Low IgA may be associated with recurrent mucosal infections and can affect interpretation of IgA-based celiac tests. High IgA may occur with chronic infection, inflammation, liver disease, or a monoclonal gammopathy.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include IgG and IgM, tissue-transglutaminase IgG when celiac disease is suspected, serum protein electrophoresis, immunofixation, CBC, and assessment for infection, liver disease, or protein loss.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item repeated sinus, ear, chest, or intestinal infections;
                      \item chronic diarrhea or poor absorption;
                      \item abdominal pain, weight loss, or anemia;
                      \item joint pain or unexplained inflammation; or
                      \item suspected celiac disease, immune deficiency, or gammopathy.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Immunoglobulin M (IgM)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    IgM is commonly produced early in an immune response. Quantitative IgM measures the total amount in blood; it does not prove that a particular infection is recent. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/immunoglobulins-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    IgM was identified as a distinct antibody class during twentieth-century immunology. Modern immunochemical methods quantify it. FDA authorization applies to specific reagents and analysers, not to a single universal IgM approval date.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A common adult interval is about 40--230 mg/dL, but age and method matter. Low IgM may occur with immune deficiency, protein loss, or medicines. High IgM may occur with infection, chronic inflammation, liver disease, or an IgM monoclonal gammopathy.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include repeat immunoglobulins, serum protein electrophoresis, immunofixation, free light chains, disease-specific antibody or PCR tests, CBC, and liver/kidney assessment.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item recurrent or prolonged infections;
                      \item fever or unexplained inflammation;
                      \item swollen lymph nodes or enlarged spleen;
                      \item anemia, unusual bleeding, or fatigue; or
                      \item suspected immune deficiency or monoclonal gammopathy.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Complement C3}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    C3 is a major protein of the complement system, a group of proteins that helps antibodies and immune cells fight infection. Measuring C3 can show whether complement is being consumed or is increased during inflammation. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/complement-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Complement was described in nineteenth-century studies of serum's ability to help antibodies kill microbes. C3 measurement became possible with immunochemical laboratory methods. FDA authorization applies to specific assays.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    An illustrative adult interval is about 90--180 mg/dL, but the laboratory interval takes precedence. Low C3 may occur with lupus, immune-complex kidney disease, severe infection, or inherited complement deficiency. High C3 is usually an acute-phase response and is nonspecific.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include C4, CH50, ANA and other autoimmune tests, urinalysis, urine protein, creatinine, blood counts, and tests directed at infection or kidney inflammation.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item joint pain or swelling;
                      \item unexplained fever, fatigue, or inflammation;
                      \item facial or leg swelling;
                      \item blood or protein in urine;
                      \item rash or sensitivity to sunlight; or
                      \item recurrent serious infections or monitoring lupus.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Complement C4}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    C4 is one of the complement proteins. C4 measurement is often used with C3 to assess complement consumption in immune-complex disease and to monitor conditions such as lupus. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/complement-blood-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    C4 testing grew from complement research and later immunochemical measurement of individual complement proteins. FDA authorization applies to named test systems, not to one universal approval date.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    An illustrative adult interval is about 10--40 mg/dL, but methods and reference intervals differ. Low C4 may occur with lupus, cryoglobulinemia, hereditary angioedema, or complement consumption. High C4 may accompany inflammation and is usually nonspecific.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include C3, CH50, ANA, disease-specific antibodies, cryoglobulins, urinalysis, kidney tests, and evaluation for recurrent swelling or infection.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item joint pain or swelling;
                      \item rash or photosensitivity;
                      \item facial or leg swelling;
                      \item abdominal pain or unexplained swelling episodes;
                      \item blood or protein in urine; or
                      \item monitoring lupus or another immune-complex disorder.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Antinuclear antibody (ANA)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    ANA testing looks for antibodies that react with cell nuclei. It is a screening test for some systemic autoimmune diseases, not a general test for all illness. A positive result can occur in healthy people, so it must be interpreted with symptoms and examination. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/ana-antinuclear-antibody-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    ANA was developed from observations of abnormal nuclear-reacting antibodies in lupus and later standardized with indirect immunofluorescence and other immunoassays. FDA authorization applies to individual platforms and reagents.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Results are reported as positive or negative and often as a titer and pattern. There is no single normal titer for every method. A positive ANA may support lupus, scleroderma, Sjogren disease, or another connective-tissue disease, but also occurs with infection, medicines, and healthy aging. A negative result lowers but does not eliminate the possibility of autoimmune disease.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include disease-specific antibodies, complement, CBC, urinalysis, kidney tests, ESR or CRP, and specialist assessment. ANA should not be used as a stand-alone diagnosis.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item persistent joint pain or swelling;
                      \item unexplained fever or fatigue;
                      \item rash or sensitivity to sunlight;
                      \item mouth or nose ulcers;
                      \item fingers changing color in cold conditions;
                      \item dry eyes or dry mouth;
                      \item chest pain with breathing; or
                      \item blood or protein in urine.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Rheumatoid factor (RF)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Rheumatoid factor is an antibody directed against part of another antibody. The RF blood test helps evaluate rheumatoid arthritis when joint symptoms fit, but RF is not specific and can be positive without rheumatoid arthritis. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/rheumatoid-factor-rf-test/}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    RF was identified through studies of serum factors in rheumatoid arthritis. Modern assays use immunochemical methods. FDA authorization applies to specific RF assays rather than to one universal approval date.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Many laboratories use a cutoff near 14 IU/mL, but the assay interval controls interpretation. A high RF may occur in rheumatoid arthritis, other autoimmune disease, chronic infection, liver disease, or some cancers. A low or negative RF does not exclude rheumatoid arthritis.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include anti-CCP, ESR, CRP, CBC, imaging of affected joints, joint-fluid analysis, and tests for infection or other autoimmune disease. Anti-CCP is generally more specific for rheumatoid arthritis than RF.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item persistent joint pain;
                      \item swollen or warm joints;
                      \item morning stiffness lasting a long time;
                      \item symptoms in the hands, wrists, or feet;
                      \item fatigue or low-grade fever; or
                      \item suspected rheumatoid arthritis or another inflammatory disease.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody (anti-CCP)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Anti-CCP testing looks for antibodies to citrullinated proteins. It helps evaluate rheumatoid arthritis, especially when persistent inflammatory joint symptoms are present. A positive result is not by itself a diagnosis, and a negative result does not exclude rheumatoid arthritis. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/rheumatoidarthritis.html}{MedlinePlus}.
                    \section{History}
                    Anti-CCP assays developed from research into citrullinated proteins and their immune response in rheumatoid arthritis. FDA authorization applies to individual assays and platforms.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    The laboratory's cutoff and assay matter. A positive anti-CCP is relatively specific for rheumatoid arthritis and may be associated with a greater risk of persistent or erosive disease, but it must fit the clinical picture. A negative result can occur in seronegative disease.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include RF, ESR, CRP, CBC, joint examination, ultrasound or X-ray, MRI, and joint-fluid analysis when another diagnosis is possible. The test does not determine treatment by itself.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item swollen or warm joints;
                      \item persistent pain in hands, wrists, or feet;
                      \item prolonged morning stiffness;
                      \item reduced joint movement;
                      \item fatigue or low-grade fever; or
                      \item suspected inflammatory arthritis.
                    \end{enumerate}
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{HIV-1/2 antigen/antibody screen}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    An HIV antigen/antibody screening test looks for HIV p24 antigen and antibodies to HIV-1 and HIV-2. A laboratory blood test is a screening step; a reactive result requires the CDC-recommended follow-up algorithm. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hiv/testing/index.html}{CDC}.
                    \section{History}
                    HIV testing progressed from antibody tests to combination antigen/antibody assays, which shorten the time to detection, and nucleic-acid tests, which can detect viral RNA earlier. FDA authorization applies to each specific test; there is no single FDA approval date for ``HIV testing.''
                    \section{Modern view}
                    A nonreactive result means the test found no evidence at that time, but a recent exposure may fall within the window period. A laboratory antigen/antibody test from a vein usually detects infection 18--45 days after exposure; the exact window depends on the assay and exposure. A reactive screening result is preliminary until the follow-up algorithm is completed. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hiv/testing/index.html}{CDC}.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include an HIV-1/HIV-2 antibody differentiation test, HIV nucleic-acid testing when needed, repeat testing after the window period, and assessment for other sexually transmitted infections, hepatitis, tuberculosis, pregnancy, and urgent post-exposure prophylaxis when relevant.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item a recent possible sexual or needle exposure;
                      \item fever, rash, sore throat, or swollen lymph nodes;
                      \item night sweats or unexplained weight loss;
                      \item recurrent or unusual infections;
                      \item a new sexually transmitted infection;
                      \item pregnancy or planned pregnancy; or
                      \item routine screening or monitoring of known HIV.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    No symptom can establish or exclude HIV. Testing after a possible exposure should follow CDC window-period guidance.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg)}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    HBsAg testing looks for a protein on the surface of hepatitis B virus. A positive result suggests current hepatitis B infection, while a negative result does not show whether a person is immune. CDC recommends interpreting HBsAg with total anti-HBc and anti-HBs. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hepatitis-b/hcp/diagnosis-testing/index.html}{CDC}.
                    \section{History}
                    HBsAg became the basis of hepatitis B diagnosis after the surface antigen was identified in blood and serologic assays were developed. Modern immunoassays and nucleic-acid tests are authorized as individual FDA-regulated products; there is no single approval date for the biological marker.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    The expected result is nonreactive. A reactive result may represent acute or chronic infection and needs confirmation and the complete hepatitis B serologic panel. A negative HBsAg does not exclude infection during a very early period, and it does not prove vaccine protection or past recovery.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
