                    Follow-up may include repeat or confirmatory HBsAg, total anti-HBc, IgM anti-HBc when acute infection is considered, anti-HBs, HBV DNA, ALT and AST, bilirubin, and assessment for liver inflammation and household or sexual contacts. Vaccination is considered when the panel shows susceptibility.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item yellow skin or eyes;
                      \item dark urine or pale stools;
                      \item unusual tiredness;
                      \item nausea, vomiting, or poor appetite;
                      \item abdominal or joint pain;
                      \item a known exposure to blood or body fluids; or
                      \item pregnancy, routine screening, or monitoring of known hepatitis B.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    Hepatitis B can be present without symptoms, so symptoms are not required for testing. See \href{https://www.cdc.gov/hepatitis-b/hcp/diagnosis-testing/index.html}{CDC}.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\IfSubStr{#1}{Hematocrit}{%
  \ifparameterdetail\else
    \parameterdetailtrue
%
                    Hematocrit is important because it estimates how much of the blood volume is red blood cells. It helps identify and follow anemia and erythrocytosis, but it does not identify the cause by itself. A low or high result may reflect a change in red-cell mass, a change in plasma water, or both. MedlinePlus explains that hematocrit must be interpreted with the person's symptoms and other blood results.
                    \section{History}
                    The original hematocrit method separated blood in a graduated tube by centrifugation and measured the packed red-cell layer. Modern CBC analysers usually calculate hematocrit from the red-cell count and mean cell volume, while direct packed-cell-volume methods remain useful in some settings. The \href{https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK259/}{NCBI Bookshelf} describes both the centrifugation method and automated calculation. There is no single FDA approval date for the biological measurement ``hematocrit'' or ``PCV''; FDA regulation applies to specific analysers and test systems.
                    \section{Modern view}
                    Hematocrit is interpreted together with hemoglobin, RBC count, MCV, and the patient's hydration status. A low value may reflect anemia or dilution, while a high value may reflect dehydration or increased red-cell production. Values also vary with sex, age, pregnancy, smoking, and altitude. A result outside the laboratory range is a clue for evaluation, not a diagnosis.
                    \section{What the doctor may do next}
                    Follow-up may include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item hemoglobin, RBC count, MCV, and other CBC results;
                      \item reticulocyte count to assess new red-cell production;
                      \item iron, vitamin B12, and folate tests when anemia is possible;
                      \item blood-smear examination; or
                      \item assessment for bleeding, dehydration, low oxygen, kidney disease, or a bone-marrow disorder when the results suggest one of these causes.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    MedlinePlus lists CBC-related tests, reticulocyte testing, iron testing, and vitamin testing as possible parts of evaluating an abnormal red-cell result.
                    \section{Symptoms that may lead to testing}
                    Symptoms that may lead to a hematocrit test include:
                    \begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
                      \item shortness of breath;
                      \item tiredness or weakness;
                      \item headache;
                      \item dizziness;
                      \item pale skin;
                      \item abnormal heartbeat;
                      \item itching after a shower or bath;
                      \item blurred or double vision; or
                      \item unusual bleeding.
                    \end{enumerate}
                    The first five symptoms are listed by MedlinePlus for anemia; the later symptoms may occur with polycythemia. Symptoms are not specific to an abnormal hematocrit.
                  
  \fi
}{}%
\ifparameterdetail\else
%
                    This test is important because it measures the feature described above. Its result must be read with the other results in the same profile, the person's symptoms, and the laboratory's reference range.
                  
\fi
}

\title{Common Laboratory Tests from Blood\\
\large Initial Draft: 100 Common Parameters}
\author{Medical Reference Team}
\date{\today}

\begin{document}

\sloppy

\maketitle

\tableofcontents

\clearpage

\chapter*{Book Summary}
This initial draft lists 100 commonly reported blood-test measurements. Some
use whole blood, while others use the liquid part of blood called serum or
plasma. It is written as a plain-language reference for patients, students,
and health professionals. It covers blood cells, clotting, body chemistry,
cholesterol, hormones, vitamins, inflammation, heart risk, immunity, and
infection.

\chapter{Introduction}

Laboratory medicine uses samples such as blood and urine to measure cells,
chemicals, proteins, hormones, antibodies, microorganisms, medicines, and
genetic material. These tests can help detect a condition, support a diagnosis,
plan treatment, check whether treatment is working, or monitor a known illness.
The same result must always be interpreted with the person's symptoms, medical
history, examination, and the laboratory's own reference range. These points
are described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/laboratorytests.html}{MedlinePlus}
and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/how-to-understand-your-lab-results/}{NIH guidance on understanding results}.

There are very many blood and urine tests. The list below is an orientation to
the main groups. It is not a list of tests that every person needs, and it is
not a recommendation for a full health package. Some examples are routine;
others are used only when a particular symptom, disease, exposure, family
history, treatment, or clinical question makes them appropriate. The FDA
describes laboratory tests performed on human specimens as in-vitro diagnostic
tests and emphasizes that their intended use and specimen type matter
(\href{https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/products-and-medical-procedures/in-vitro-diagnostics}{FDA IVD information}).

\section{Tests from blood}

Blood tests examine what is present in blood at the time the sample is taken.
Common groups and examples include:

\begin{description}[leftmargin=3.2cm,style=nextline]
  \item[Hematology] Complete blood count (CBC), hemoglobin, hematocrit, red-cell count, white-cell count and differential, platelet count, erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR), reticulocyte count, and peripheral blood smear.
  \item[Diabetes] Fasting glucose, random glucose, post-meal glucose, HbA1c, insulin, and C-peptide.
  \item[Kidney function] Creatinine, urea or blood urea nitrogen (BUN), estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR), uric acid, and cystatin C.
  \item[Liver function] ALT, AST, alkaline phosphatase, GGT, bilirubin, albumin, and total protein.
  \item[Lipids] Total cholesterol, LDL cholesterol, HDL cholesterol, triglycerides, ApoB, ApoA1, and lipoprotein(a).
  \item[Electrolytes and minerals] Sodium, potassium, chloride, bicarbonate, calcium, phosphate, and magnesium.
  \item[Thyroid] TSH, free T4, free T3, and thyroid antibodies.
  \item[Hormones] Testosterone, estrogen or estradiol, progesterone, LH, FSH, prolactin, cortisol, ACTH, PTH, and hCG.
  \item[Cardiac markers] Troponin I or T, BNP or NT-proBNP, and CK-MB.
  \item[Inflammation] CRP, high-sensitivity CRP, ESR, and procalcitonin.
  \item[Iron and anemia] Ferritin, serum iron, TIBC, transferrin, transferrin saturation, vitamin B12, and folate.
  \item[Coagulation] PT/INR, aPTT, fibrinogen, and D-dimer.
  \item[Autoimmune tests] ANA, anti-dsDNA, rheumatoid factor, anti-CCP, ANCA, and complement C3 and C4.
  \item[Infections] HIV, hepatitis B and C, dengue, malaria, syphilis, and pathogen-specific antibody, antigen, or PCR tests.
  \item[Cancer-related markers] PSA, AFP, CEA, CA-125, CA 19-9, CA 15-3, and other markers used only in selected clinical situations. Most are not general cancer-screening tests.
  \item[Nutrition] Vitamin D, vitamin B12, folate, iron studies, and selected trace elements.
  \item[Toxicology and medicines] Alcohol and tests for therapeutic, toxic, or recreational substances.
  \item[Genetic and molecular tests] DNA tests, chromosome tests, mutation panels, cell-free DNA, and other molecular assays.
  \item[Blood banking] ABO and Rh typing, antibody screen, and crossmatch.
  \item[Microbiology] Blood culture, pathogen PCR, and other molecular tests.
\end{description}

MedlinePlus provides patient-level descriptions of many of these blood tests,
including CBC, glucose, cholesterol, thyroid, kidney, liver, clotting,
infection, hormone, and genetic tests (\href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/}{MedlinePlus Medical Tests}).

\section{Tests from urine}

Urine tests examine what the kidneys and urinary tract release into urine. They
can also measure substances that the body is filtering or removing. A routine
urinalysis may include physical, chemical, and microscopic examination of the
sample, as described by \href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003579.htm}{MedlinePlus Urinalysis}.

Common groups and examples include:

\begin{description}[leftmargin=3.2cm,style=nextline]
  \item[Routine urinalysis] Colour, appearance, specific gravity, and pH.
  \item[Protein] Total protein, albumin, and the albumin-to-creatinine ratio (ACR).
  \item[Glucose and metabolism] Glucose and ketones.
  \item[Microscopy] Red cells, white cells, epithelial cells, casts, crystals, bacteria, and yeast.
  \item[Kidney assessment] Urine creatinine, protein-to-creatinine ratio, ACR, 24-hour protein, and creatinine clearance.
  \item[Urinary infection] Leukocyte esterase, nitrite, microscopy, culture, and antibiotic-susceptibility testing.
  \item[Pregnancy] hCG.
  \item[Electrolytes] Sodium, potassium, chloride, calcium, phosphate, and other measurements.
  \item[Endocrine] Urinary cortisol, metanephrines, catecholamines, and 5-HIAA.
  \item[Toxicology] Screening for drugs and their metabolites.
  \item[Cancer and urology] Urine cytology and selected molecular tests.
  \item[Metabolic disorders] Porphyrins, amino acids, organic acids, and specialized metabolite testing.
  \item[STIs and infections] Molecular tests for organisms such as Chlamydia and gonorrhea.
  \item[24-hour urine studies] Protein, calcium, oxalate, citrate, uric acid, creatinine, cortisol, and other timed measurements.
\end{description}

The practical distinction is that blood tests measure substances circulating in
the body at collection, while urine tests can show urinary-tract findings and
substances being filtered or excreted. This is a useful general distinction,
not an absolute rule: the clinical meaning depends on the specific test,
sample, method, timing, and patient. MedlinePlus explains that urine testing
can identify cells, chemicals, proteins, glucose, crystals, and microorganisms
(\href{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003579.htm}{Urinalysis}).

For an encyclopedia of medical tests, these groups can be expanded much
further. Blood and urine support many specialized molecular, genetic,
microbiological, toxicological, and metabolic assays. The number of tests
depends on whether related methods, specimen types, analytes, panels, and
laboratory-developed assays are counted separately. This book therefore uses
100 common blood parameters as its starting scope, while recognizing that the
full field is much larger.

\section{How to use this draft}
The normal ranges in this report are examples, mainly for adults. They are not
diagnoses and they are not universal cutoffs. A suitable range may change with
age, sex, pregnancy, menstrual-cycle timing, altitude, posture, fasting,
medicines, the testing method, and the person's health. Children, older adults,
pregnant people, and people with certain illnesses may need different ranges.
Always compare a result with the range printed on the laboratory report. Some
values are calculated from other results. A blood test by itself does not prove
a disease.

Medical words are explained in simple language where possible. For example,
``low'' means below the laboratory's stated range and ``high'' means above it;
neither word automatically means that a person is sick. Ask the ordering doctor
or a qualified laboratory professional to explain an unexpected result.

\section{Evidence and Citation Policy}

This report is being prepared as a source-based reference, not as free-form
medical advice. Every factual statement about a test must be traceable to an
identified source. Sources should be appropriate to the claim: government
agencies, official regulatory records, national health institutes,
peer-reviewed medical literature, or established medical reference texts.

The preferred sources for this report are the Centers for Disease Control and
Prevention (CDC) for public-health and laboratory guidance; the U.S. Food and
Drug Administration (FDA) for in-vitro diagnostic devices, clearances,
approvals, and safety notices; MedlinePlus and other National Institutes of
Health (NIH) services for patient-level test explanations; the National
Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK) for kidney and
metabolic guidance; the National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute (NHLBI) for
lipids and cardiovascular risk; and the NIH Office of Dietary Supplements for
vitamin and mineral evidence. For Indian regulatory and laboratory-quality
claims, the preferred sources are CDSCO and NABL. Peer-reviewed articles and
established references such as the Merck Manual may be used when an official
agency does not provide the needed historical or clinical detail.

These sources have different roles. A CDC recommendation is not an FDA
product clearance. An FDA clearance is not a recommendation that every person
should have the test. A MedlinePlus explanation is not a substitute for the
patient's laboratory-specific reference range or clinical assessment.

Each test entry must have sources for its purpose, reference range, low and
high results, symptoms, history, modern use, regulatory status, and follow-up.
A source for one part of a test does not automatically support every other
part. Reference ranges must be linked to the laboratory, method, age, sex, and
population for which they were reported. If a reliable source cannot be found,
the statement must be removed or clearly labelled as uncertain; it must not be
filled with a general statement.

This file is a complete working draft of the 100-parameter blood-test book.
The chapter text uses source links and the source list below; numerical ranges
remain illustrative and must be checked against the performing laboratory's
method, population, and current guidance before publication. The basophil
entry illustrates the intended standard: its history, clinical interpretation,
symptom statement, and FDA explanation are separately sourced.

21 & Prothrombin time (PT) & Citrated plasma; seconds & 11--14 & Extrinsic/common coagulation pathway \\

\section{Thyroid Profile}

A thyroid profile is a group of blood tests used to assess thyroid hormone
production and pituitary regulation. The profile covered in this draft includes
total triiodothyronine (T3), total thyroxine (T4), and thyroid-stimulating
hormone (TSH). Other laboratories may instead report free T4, free T3, or
thyroid antibodies. The results must be interpreted together, because illness,
pregnancy, medicines, and binding-protein changes can alter individual results.

\subsection*{What the profile measures}
\textbf{TSH ultrasensitive:} TSH is produced by the pituitary gland and
stimulates the thyroid to produce T4 and T3. It is commonly the first test used
to assess thyroid function. An abnormal TSH is usually interpreted with T4,
T3, symptoms, medicines, and the patient's clinical setting.

\textbf{Total T3:} T3 is the more biologically active thyroid hormone. Much of
the circulating T3 is formed when T4 is converted to T3 in peripheral tissues.
Total T3 includes both protein-bound and unbound hormone and can be affected by
changes in binding proteins.

\textbf{Total T4:} T4 is the main hormone released by the thyroid gland. It is
converted in tissues to T3 and helps regulate metabolic rate, temperature,
heart rate, growth, and development. Total T4 also includes bound and unbound
hormone; free T4 may be preferred when binding proteins are altered.

\subsection*{Ranges for the total profile}
There is no single normal range for all laboratories. Use the interval printed
on the patient's report. The interval may differ with the method, age, sex,
pregnancy, and thyroid medicines, particularly for children, older adults, and
pregnant people.

\subsection*{Common thyroid disorders and symptoms}
\textbf{Hyperthyroidism} occurs when thyroid hormone activity is excessive. It
may be associated with weight loss, anxiety, tremor, heat intolerance,
palpitations or increased heart rate, frequent stools, sweating, and sometimes
eye prominence in Graves disease.

\textbf{Hypothyroidism} occurs when thyroid hormone activity is insufficient. It
may be associated with weight gain, fatigue, constipation, cold intolerance,
dry skin, hair changes, menstrual irregularity, slowed heart rate, and low
mood.

\textbf{Goiter} is enlargement of the thyroid and may occur with iodine
imbalance, autoimmune disease, nodules, or other thyroid disorders. Neck
swelling, pressure, difficulty swallowing, or breathing difficulty requires
clinical assessment.

\textbf{Thyroid cancer} is evaluated primarily with examination, ultrasound,
and, when indicated, fine-needle aspiration. A thyroid profile can assess
thyroid function but cannot by itself diagnose or exclude thyroid cancer.

\subsection*{Who may need testing}
Testing may be considered for symptoms suggesting thyroid dysfunction, a family
history of thyroid disease, a thyroid or neck lump, unexplained changes in heat
or cold tolerance, menstrual or fertility problems, pregnancy, or use of
medicines that affect thyroid function. Pregnancy-specific interpretation is
important because thyroid requirements and reference intervals change during
pregnancy.

\subsection*{Preparation and healthy-living considerations}
Fasting requirements depend on the laboratory and the other tests ordered.
Patients should
report thyroid medicines, amiodarone, steroids, supplements, and especially
biotin because biotin can interfere with some immunoassays. Adequate but not
excessive iodine intake, a balanced diet, regular activity, adequate sleep, and
stress management support general health; iodine supplements should not be
started without clinical advice because both deficiency and excess can disturb
thyroid function.

\subsection*{Core components}
The core thyroid parameters are described individually in the test guides below.
\iffalse
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.25\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Typical adult interval} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Typical adult interval} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
TSH & Serum; mIU/L & 0.4--4.0 & First-line screening test for thyroid dysfunction \\
Free T4 & Serum; ng/dL & 0.8--1.8 & Measures circulating unbound thyroxine \\
Total or free T3 & Serum; ng/dL or pg/mL & Total 80--200 ng/dL & Helps evaluate suspected or treated hyperthyroidism \\
Anti-thyroid peroxidase antibody (anti-TPO) & Serum; IU/mL & Negative or assay-specific & Supports autoimmune thyroiditis \\
Thyroglobulin antibody (TgAb) & Serum; IU/mL & Negative or assay-specific & Supports autoimmune thyroid disease; may interfere with thyroglobulin \\
TSH-receptor antibody (TRAb) & Serum; IU/L & Negative or assay-specific & Supports Graves disease \\
\end{longtable}
\fi

\subsection*{Common interpretation patterns}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item \textbf{Primary hypothyroidism:} TSH is high and free T4 is low. If free T4 remains normal, the pattern is usually called subclinical hypothyroidism.
  \item \textbf{Primary hyperthyroidism:} TSH is low and free T4 and/or T3 is high. An isolated T3 elevation may occur in early or T3-predominant hyperthyroidism.
  \item \textbf{Central hypothyroidism:} Free T4 is low with a TSH that is low, normal, or only mildly high. Pituitary or hypothalamic disease should be considered.
  \item \textbf{Autoimmune thyroiditis:} Anti-TPO and/or TgAb may be positive, particularly in Hashimoto thyroiditis, but antibody positivity alone does not establish the degree of thyroid dysfunction.
  \item \textbf{Graves disease:} A suppressed TSH with elevated thyroid hormones and a positive TRAb supports the diagnosis.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Preparation and follow-up}
Most thyroid blood tests do not require fasting. Biotin-containing supplements can
interfere with some immunoassays and should be disclosed; the laboratory or
clinician may advise temporarily withholding biotin before collection. Results
should also be interpreted with thyroid-replacement medicines, amiodarone,
glucocorticoids, dopamine-related drugs, pregnancy, and acute illness in mind.
Abnormal results may be followed by repeat TSH and free T4, T3, thyroid
antibodies, medication review, and thyroid ultrasound or radionuclide imaging
when clinically indicated.

\section{Liver Functions}

Liver function testing is a group of blood tests used to identify liver-cell
injury, impaired bile flow, and reduced hepatic synthetic function. The profile
covered in this draft includes albumin, alkaline phosphatase, direct bilirubin,
total bilirubin, GGT, total protein, AST/SGOT, ALT/SGPT, indirect bilirubin,
globulin, the albumin-to-globulin ratio, and the AST-to-ALT ratio. Fasting
requirements depend on the laboratory and the other tests ordered.
Prothrombin time (PT) or INR is particularly useful for assessing the liver's
ability to synthesize clotting factors, but it is not included in every LFT
package. No single result measures every aspect of liver function, and
abnormalities should be interpreted with symptoms, medication history, imaging,
and trends over time.

\subsection*{Core components}
The core liver parameters are described individually in the test guides below.
\iffalse
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.25\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Typical adult interval} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Typical adult interval} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
ALT & Serum or plasma; U/L & 7--56 & Relatively sensitive marker of hepatocellular injury \\
AST & Serum or plasma; U/L & 10--40 & Liver or muscle injury marker; interpret with ALT and CK \\
ALP & Serum or plasma; U/L & 44--147 & Cholestasis or increased bone turnover \\
GGT & Serum or plasma; U/L & Male 9--48; female 7--32 & Supports hepatic or biliary source of an elevated ALP \\
Total bilirubin & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & 0.2--1.2 & Excretion of heme breakdown products and jaundice assessment \\
Direct bilirubin & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & 0.0--0.3 & Conjugated bilirubin and biliary excretion \\
Albumin & Serum or plasma; g/dL & 3.5--5.0 & Longer-term hepatic synthesis and oncotic pressure \\
Total protein & Serum or plasma; g/dL & 6.0--8.3 & Albumin plus globulin concentration \\
PT/INR & Citrated plasma; seconds or ratio & PT 11--14; INR 0.8--1.2 & Rapid assessment of clotting-factor synthesis \\
\end{longtable}
\fi

\subsection*{Common interpretation patterns}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item \textbf{Hepatocellular pattern:} ALT and AST are disproportionately higher than ALP. Causes include viral hepatitis, fatty liver disease, ischemic injury, alcohol-associated disease, and medication or toxin injury.
  \item \textbf{Cholestatic pattern:} ALP and GGT are disproportionately higher than ALT and AST. Biliary obstruction, cholangitis, infiltrative disease, and some medicines are possible causes.
  \item \textbf{Hyperbilirubinemia:} Predominantly indirect bilirubin suggests increased production or impaired uptake/conjugation; predominantly direct bilirubin suggests impaired hepatic excretion or cholestasis.
  \item \textbf{Reduced synthetic function:} A prolonged PT/INR and low albumin may indicate impaired synthesis, although nutrition, inflammation, kidney loss, anticoagulants, and acute illness can also affect these results.
  \item \textbf{AST greater than ALT:} This pattern can occur with alcohol-associated liver disease, advanced fibrosis, or muscle injury; it is not diagnostic by itself.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Preparation and follow-up}
Fasting is not always required, but the laboratory may recommend it when liver
tests are ordered with a lipid or metabolic panel. Alcohol, strenuous exercise,
recent meals, supplements, acetaminophen, statins, antibiotics, and herbal
products should be documented. Depending on the pattern and severity, follow-up
may include repeat testing, hepatitis serology, iron studies, autoimmune liver
antibodies, creatine kinase, abdominal ultrasound, elastography, or specialist
review. A rapidly rising INR, severe jaundice, confusion, bleeding, or marked
aminotransferase elevation requires prompt clinical assessment.

\section{Lipid Profile}

A lipid profile measures circulating cholesterol and triglycerides to estimate
atherosclerotic cardiovascular risk and to monitor lipid-lowering treatment.
The report commonly includes total cholesterol, LDL cholesterol, HDL
cholesterol, and triglycerides. Non-HDL cholesterol and apolipoprotein B can
provide additional information about the number of atherogenic particles.
Treatment targets are individualized according to cardiovascular history,
diabetes, kidney disease, age, and overall risk.

\subsection*{What is Lipid Profile Advanced?}
An advanced lipid profile is an expanded blood assessment of cholesterol,
triglycerides, and atherogenic lipoproteins. In addition to total cholesterol,
HDL cholesterol, LDL cholesterol, and triglycerides, it may report very-low-
density lipoprotein (VLDL), non-HDL cholesterol, apolipoprotein B, or
lipoprotein(a). The exact contents vary by laboratory and package.

\textbf{HDL cholesterol} is associated with reverse cholesterol transport, but
a high HDL value does not eliminate cardiovascular risk. \textbf{LDL
cholesterol} is a major atherogenic marker and is commonly used to guide
treatment. \textbf{VLDL} carries triglycerides and is often estimated from
triglycerides; it is not simply a direct measure of recently eaten fat.
\textbf{Triglycerides} are circulating fats influenced by diet, alcohol,
diabetes, obesity, medicines, and inherited lipid disorders. \textbf{Total
cholesterol} combines cholesterol carried in several lipoprotein fractions.

\subsection*{Illustrative desirable levels}
Common adult goals are total cholesterol below 200 mg/dL, triglycerides below
150 mg/dL, and LDL cholesterol below 100 mg/dL for many people. HDL cholesterol
above 40 mg/dL in men and above 50 mg/dL in women is commonly considered
favorable; values above 60 mg/dL may be associated with lower risk but are not a
stand-alone treatment target. People with diabetes, established cardiovascular
disease, familial hypercholesterolemia, or very high risk may have substantially
lower individualized LDL targets. The laboratory's reference interval and the
clinician's risk-based treatment goal should not be confused.

\subsection*{Preparation before an advanced lipid profile}
Many routine lipid profiles can be collected without fasting. A fasting sample
of about 8--12 hours may be requested when triglycerides are high, a severe
lipid disorder is suspected, or the laboratory specifically requires it.
Alcohol, recent illness, major weight change, intense exercise, and medicines
can affect results. Patients should not stop prescribed medicines without
medical advice and should report all medicines and supplements.

\subsection*{Why it is ordered and who may need it}
Lipid testing supports routine cardiovascular risk assessment, monitoring of
high cholesterol or cardiovascular disease, evaluation of diabetes or obesity,
assessment of smoking-related risk, and investigation of a family history of
premature cardiovascular disease or severe hypercholesterolemia. It may also
be repeated to assess response to diet, exercise, or lipid-lowering treatment.
High cholesterol is often asymptomatic; chest pain, breathlessness, or fatigue
requires medical assessment and should not be attributed to cholesterol alone.
Xanthomas or a strong family history may suggest an inherited lipid disorder.

\subsection*{Reducing cardiovascular risk}
Heart-healthy measures include more vegetables, fruit, legumes, whole grains,
and soluble fiber; replacement of saturated and trans fats with unsaturated
fats; regular physical activity; weight management; smoking cessation; and
moderation or avoidance of alcohol. Omega-3-rich foods may help dietary quality,
but supplements and medicines should be discussed with a clinician. A repeat
profile and evaluation for secondary causes such as diabetes, hypothyroidism,
kidney disease, liver disease, and medicines may be appropriate.

\section{HbA1c and Diabetes}

\subsection*{What is HbA1c?}
Hemoglobin A1c, also called glycated or glycosylated hemoglobin, measures the
percentage of hemoglobin with glucose attached to it. Because red blood cells
usually circulate for about two to three months, HbA1c estimates average blood
glucose over that period, with greater weighting toward the most recent weeks.
It is used to screen for diabetes and prediabetes and to monitor diabetes
treatment. It does not replace assessment of symptoms, glucose measurements,
medicines, or complications.

\subsection*{Diabetes and its main types}
\textbf{Type 1 diabetes} is an autoimmune condition in which insulin-producing
pancreatic beta cells are destroyed; it can occur in children or adults.
\textbf{Type 2 diabetes} involves insulin resistance and progressive impairment
of insulin production and is the most common form. \textbf{Prediabetes} means
glucose is above the usual range but below the diagnostic threshold for
diabetes; progression can often be reduced through weight management, physical
activity, nutrition, and appropriate medical care. Other forms include
gestational diabetes, monogenic diabetes, and diabetes caused by medicines or
pancreatic disease.

\subsection*{HbA1c interpretation}
For most adults who are not pregnant, an HbA1c below 5.7 percent is generally
in the normal range, 5.7--6.4 percent is consistent with prediabetes, and 6.5
percent or higher is in the diabetes range. A diabetes-range result is usually
confirmed with repeat HbA1c or another accepted glucose test unless there is
clear symptomatic hyperglycemia. For many adults with established diabetes, a
common treatment goal is below 7 percent, but targets are individualized for
age, pregnancy, hypoglycemia risk, comorbidities, life expectancy, and treatment
burden.

HbA1c is reported as a percentage. It may also be converted to estimated
average glucose in mg/dL; for example, approximately 5.7 percent corresponds
to 117 mg/dL and 6.5 percent to 140 mg/dL. These are estimates, not separate
reference ranges, and should not be reported as mg/L.

\subsection*{Symptoms and who may need testing}
Possible symptoms of significant hyperglycemia include:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=*]
  \item frequent urination;
  \item excessive thirst or hunger;
  \item fatigue;
  \item blurred vision;
  \item unexplained weight loss;
  \item slow-healing wounds;
  \item numbness; and
  \item recurrent infections.
\end{enumerate}
HbA1c screening may be
appropriate for adults with symptoms, age-related risk, obesity, hypertension,
abnormal lipids, a family history of diabetes, polycystic ovary syndrome,
cardiovascular disease, prior gestational diabetes, or low physical activity.
The appropriate screening interval depends on previous results and risk.

\subsection*{Important limitations}
HbA1c may be misleading when red-cell survival or hemoglobin structure is
abnormal. Blood loss, recent transfusion, hemolysis, iron deficiency, vitamin
B12 deficiency, kidney failure, pregnancy, hemoglobin variants, and some ethnic
or genetic factors can alter the relationship between HbA1c and actual glucose.
If HbA1c does not match home glucose or symptoms, clinicians may use fasting
glucose, an oral glucose-tolerance test, fructosamine, glycated albumin, or
continuous glucose monitoring.

\subsection*{Nutrition and self-care}
Helpful measures include non-starchy vegetables, legumes, whole grains,
adequate protein, fiber-rich foods, and unsaturated fats. Sugary drinks,
excessive refined carbohydrates, highly processed foods, and excess alcohol can
worsen glycemic control. Regular physical activity, prescribed medicines,
adequate sleep, smoking cessation, and periodic glucose monitoring support
diabetes care. Medicines should not be changed based on an HbA1c result without
clinical guidance.

\subsection*{Core components}
The core lipid parameters are described individually in the test guides below.
\iffalse
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.25\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Desirable or typical value} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Desirable or typical value} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule

\endlastfoot
Total cholesterol & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & $<200$ & Overall cholesterol burden \\
LDL cholesterol & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & Often $<100$; lower targets for high risk & Primary atherogenic treatment target \\
HDL cholesterol & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & Male $\geq40$; female $\geq50$ & Risk assessment and lipid pattern \\
Triglycerides & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & $<150$ fasting & Metabolic risk and pancreatitis risk \\
Non-HDL cholesterol & Calculated; mg/dL & Often $<130$ & Total atherogenic cholesterol \\
Apolipoprotein B & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & About 60--130 & Atherogenic particle number \\

\end{longtable}
\fi

\subsection*{Interpretation and preparation}
High LDL cholesterol, high triglycerides, elevated lipoprotein(a), or low HDL
cholesterol may increase cardiovascular risk, but treatment decisions use the
complete risk profile rather than one result. A fasting sample is preferred
when triglycerides are very high, when a metabolic disorder is suspected, or
when required by local protocol; many routine lipid profiles can be collected
without fasting. Recent illness, alcohol, pregnancy, weight change, and
medicines should be considered. Follow-up may include a repeat profile,
secondary-cause evaluation (glucose, TSH, liver and kidney tests), ApoB or
lipoprotein(a), and assessment of adherence and response to treatment.

\section{Complete Blood Count / Hemogram Profile}

The listed parameters form a complete blood count with differential and red-cell
indices. The profile includes absolute basophil, eosinophil, lymphocyte,
monocyte, and neutrophil counts; the corresponding basophil, eosinophil,
lymphocyte, monocyte, and segmented-neutrophil percentages; hemoglobin; total
leukocyte count; MCH; MCHC; MCV; mean platelet volume; packed cell volume;
platelet count; RBC count; and red-cell distribution width. Packed cell volume
is another name commonly used for hematocrit.

\subsection*{Absolute counts and percentages}
The total leukocyte count measures the number of white cells in a volume of
blood. Differential percentages describe the proportion of each white-cell
type, while absolute counts describe the actual number of cells. Absolute
counts are usually more clinically useful when the total leukocyte count is
very high or low. A normal percentage can coexist with an abnormal absolute
count, so both should be reviewed together.

Neutrophils and segmented neutrophils are important for bacterial and fungal
defense; low absolute neutrophils increase infection risk. Lymphocytes support
adaptive and antiviral immunity; monocytes participate in phagocytosis and
chronic inflammation; eosinophils are associated with allergy and parasites;
and basophils participate in histamine release and immune signaling. Low values
are often nonspecific, while persistent or marked elevations require clinical
correlation.

\subsection*{Red-cell and platelet parameters}
Hemoglobin, RBC count, and packed cell volume assess oxygen-carrying red-cell
mass. MCV classifies red cells as microcytic, normocytic, or macrocytic; MCH and
MCHC describe hemoglobin content and concentration; RDW measures variation in
cell size. Platelet count estimates clot-forming cells, while mean platelet
volume provides a clue to platelet size and production. Abnormal results may
reflect anemia, blood loss, nutritional deficiency, inflammation, marrow
disease, bleeding, consumption, or platelet-production disorders.

\subsection*{Mentzer Index}
The Mentzer Index is calculated as MCV divided by the RBC count, with the RBC
count expressed in millions per microliter. A value below approximately 13 may
favor thalassemia trait, while a value above approximately 13 may favor iron
deficiency; the index is only a screening aid and is not diagnostic. Ferritin,
serum iron, TIBC, transferrin saturation, blood smear, and hemoglobin analysis
may be needed to distinguish iron deficiency from thalassemia or other causes
of microcytosis.

The CBC should be interpreted using the laboratory's age-, sex-, pregnancy-, and
method-specific intervals. See the individual test guides for the low and high
implications of each parameter. General CBC reference information is available
from MedlinePlus at \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/complete-blood-count-cbc/}.

\section{Iron Studies: Serum Iron, TIBC, and Transferrin Saturation}

Serum iron, TIBC, and transferrin saturation are complementary measurements,
not independent diagnostic tests. A practical iron assessment usually combines
them with ferritin, a complete blood count, red-cell indices, and the clinical
context. Ferritin is often the best indicator of stored iron, but inflammation,
liver disease, infection, and malignancy can raise ferritin and conceal iron
deficiency. Serum iron also changes during the day and after recent iron intake.

For this reason, a low serum iron with high TIBC and low TSAT is a pattern that
supports iron deficiency, while low serum iron with low or normal TIBC and a
normal or high ferritin more often supports inflammation or chronic disease.
High serum iron with high TSAT and high ferritin raises concern for iron
overload. These are patterns, not diagnoses; bleeding, menstruation, pregnancy,
malabsorption, liver disease, supplements, and transfusion history should be
considered.

The laboratory may request a morning sample and, in some circumstances, fasting
for about 12 hours. Patients should report iron supplements and medicines but
should not stop prescribed treatment unless instructed. When results are
unexpected, repeat testing under standardized conditions may be appropriate.

\section{Kidney Tests}

Kidney testing evaluates filtration, waste excretion, electrolyte regulation,
acid--base balance, and urinary protein loss. Serum creatinine and the
estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) are commonly used to assess
filtration. BUN, electrolytes, bicarbonate, cystatin C, and urine studies add
important context. A single abnormal result should usually be confirmed and
compared with previous values.

\subsection*{What is Kidney Function Test Advanced?}
An advanced kidney-function profile assesses how effectively the kidneys filter
blood, remove nitrogenous waste, regulate electrolytes, and maintain mineral
and acid--base balance. The profile covered in this draft includes serum BUN,
total calcium, chloride, creatinine, inorganic phosphorus, sodium, urea, uric
acid, the BUN-to-creatinine ratio, the urea-to-creatinine ratio, and eGFR. The
exact contents vary between laboratories. Urinalysis and urine
albumin-to-creatinine ratio are often added
because kidney disease may cause urine abnormalities before blood values become
abnormal.

\subsection*{What the main measurements mean}
\textbf{BUN and urea} measure nitrogenous waste from protein metabolism.
\textbf{Creatinine} is a muscle-derived waste product used to estimate
filtration. \textbf{eGFR} estimates the volume of plasma filtered by the
glomeruli each minute and is reported in mL/min/1.73 m$^2$. \textbf{Sodium,
chloride, calcium, and phosphorus} help assess fluid, electrolyte, mineral,
and bone regulation. \textbf{Uric acid} is a purine breakdown product.
\textbf{BUN-to-creatinine and urea-to-creatinine ratios} can provide context
about hydration and blood flow to the kidneys, but neither ratio diagnoses
kidney disease on its own.

\subsection*{Illustrative ranges and interpretation}
Common adult examples include creatinine about 0.6--1.3 mg/dL in men and
0.6--1.1 mg/dL in women, BUN about 6--20 mg/dL, and eGFR generally above 90
mL/min/1.73 m$^2$. An eGFR below 60 that persists for at least three months, or
other evidence of kidney damage such as albuminuria, may support chronic kidney
disease. An eGFR of 15 or less indicates severe loss of filtration and requires
urgent specialist assessment; it does not by itself determine the need for
dialysis. Reference intervals vary with age, sex, muscle mass, diet, pregnancy,
medicines, and laboratory method.

\subsection*{Urine testing and symptoms}
Urinalysis may detect blood, protein, glucose, infection markers, casts, or
crystals. Blood in urine can occur with infection, stones, or kidney disease;
protein or albumin in urine may indicate glomerular damage; and glucose may
occur with diabetes or, less commonly, tubular disease. Testing is particularly
important for people with diabetes, high blood pressure, cardiovascular disease,
a family history of kidney disease, or older age. Symptoms requiring assessment
include reduced or frequent urination, painful urination, visible blood in
urine, swelling of the feet or ankles, fatigue, nausea, appetite loss, cramps,
sleep problems, or flank pain. Early kidney disease may have no symptoms.

\subsection*{Preparation and kidney protection}
Fasting is not always required, but the laboratory may give specific
instructions. Report recent strenuous exercise, high meat intake, supplements,
and medicines such as nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, diuretics, or
creatine products. Do not deliberately overhydrate before testing. Kidney
protection includes controlling blood glucose and blood pressure, maintaining a
healthy weight, avoiding tobacco, limiting unnecessary NSAID use, following
prescribed medicines, and drinking an individualized amount of fluid. People
with kidney, heart, or liver disease may need fluid restrictions rather than a
fixed daily volume.

\subsection*{Core components}
The core kidney parameters are described individually in the test guides below.
\iffalse
\begin{longtable}{@{}L{0.25\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.22\textwidth} L{0.25\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Typical adult interval} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
\textbf{Test} & \textbf{Specimen} & \textbf{Typical adult interval} & \textbf{Main purpose} \\
\midrule
\endhead
\bottomrule
\endlastfoot
Creatinine & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & Male 0.74--1.35; female 0.59--1.04 & Filtration marker \\
eGFR & Calculated; mL/min/1.73 m$^2$ & $\geq90$ generally normal & Chronic kidney disease staging \\
BUN & Serum or plasma; mg/dL & 7--20 & Nitrogen waste and hydration assessment \\
BUN-to-creatinine ratio & Calculated; ratio & About 10:1--20:1 & Helps assess prerenal influences \\
Cystatin C & Serum or plasma; mg/L & About 0.6--1.3 & Alternative filtration marker \\
Sodium & Serum or plasma; mmol/L & 135--145 & Water balance and kidney handling \\
Potassium & Serum or plasma; mmol/L & 3.5--5.0 & Renal excretion and cardiac risk \\
