\subsection*{Interpretation and follow-up}
An increased creatinine or reduced eGFR may reflect chronic kidney disease,
acute kidney injury, dehydration, urinary obstruction, or medication effects.
Creatinine is influenced by muscle mass, diet, and some medicines; cystatin C
may help when creatinine-based estimates are less reliable. Persistent eGFR
below 60, or other evidence of kidney damage for at least three months, may
support chronic kidney disease. Follow-up commonly includes urinalysis, urine
albumin-to-creatinine ratio, repeat creatinine and eGFR, electrolyte testing,
renal ultrasound, medication review, and blood-pressure assessment. A rapid
fall in eGFR, severe hyperkalemia, marked acidosis, very low urine output, or
fluid-overload symptoms requires urgent clinical evaluation.

\section{A Short History and the Modern Use of These Tests}

Blood testing began with simple observations, such as looking at blood cells
under a microscope and measuring substances with chemical reactions. Modern
machines can measure many substances from a small sample, use calibration
materials, and flag results that need review. A result is still a measurement
made by a particular method; it is not a complete picture of a person's health.

The modern approach differs by test group:

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item \textbf{Blood counts:} The machine counts and sizes blood cells. A
  blood-film review may be needed when the count is unusual or the machine
  gives a warning.
  \item \textbf{Kidney, liver, and electrolyte tests:} These are usually
  chemical measurements in serum or plasma. Doctors read several results
  together and may repeat them to see whether a change is temporary.
  \item \textbf{Hormone tests:} Results can change during the day, the
  menstrual cycle, pregnancy, or after medicines. The timing of the sample is
  therefore important.
  \item \textbf{Cholesterol and heart-risk tests:} Modern care looks at total
  risk, not one number alone. Blood pressure, diabetes, smoking, family history,
  and previous heart disease also matter.
  \item \textbf{Vitamin, iron, and inflammation tests:} A high or low result
  can have several causes. Doctors combine it with symptoms, related tests, and
  medical history before recommending treatment.
\end{itemize}

The best follow-up is often a repeat test or a related test, not an immediate
conclusion. A doctor may also review medicines, food, recent exercise,
infection, dehydration, pregnancy, or the way the sample was collected.

\section{Individual Test Guides}

The following entries use a four-part format for each parameter. The first
three parts explain the measurement, its usual range, and the meaning of low or
high results. The fourth part explains why that particular measurement matters.
The profile sections above give the symptoms, reasons for ordering, possible
conditions being investigated, follow-up tests, test history, and modern view
for groups of related tests. This avoids repeating the same general paragraph
under every analyte. The ranges are illustrative adult intervals; the range
printed by the performing laboratory and the person's clinical situation take
priority.

\parameterentry{CBC differential percentage: Basophils}{This is one part of a white-cell differential. It shows the percentage of white blood cells that are basophils. Basophils are a small group of white blood cells involved in immune and allergic reactions. The result is usually reported with the total white-cell count and the absolute counts of the five main white-cell types. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/white-blood-count-wbc/}{MedlinePlus White Blood Count} and the \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/overview-of-white-blood-cell-disorders}{MSD Manual overview of white-cell disorders}.}{There is no single range that applies to every laboratory. The Merck Manual describes basophils as less than 3 percent of circulating white blood cells, or about 0--300 cells per microlitre. Many laboratories use a narrower percentage interval, so the range printed on the report must be used for that result.}{A low percentage usually has little meaning by itself. It may occur when another white-cell type makes up a larger share of the total, or during some infections, acute allergic reactions, or overactive thyroid states. The total white-cell count and absolute basophil count are needed for interpretation.}{A high percentage may occur with an underactive thyroid or a bone-marrow disorder. Marked basophilia is described in myeloproliferative neoplasms such as polycythemia vera and myelofibrosis. It may also accompany allergic or inflammatory conditions, but the percentage alone cannot identify the cause. See \href{https://www.merckmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/basophilic-disorders}{Merck Manual Basophilic Disorders}.}
\parameterentry{CBC differential percentage: Eosinophils}{This is one part of a white-cell differential. It shows the percentage of white blood cells that are eosinophils. Eosinophils take part in allergic inflammation and in the body's response to some parasites. The result is usually reported with the total white-cell count and the absolute eosinophil count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/white-blood-count-wbc/}{MedlinePlus White Blood Count} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/professional/hematology-and-oncology/eosinophilic-disorders/eosinophilia}{MSD Manual Eosinophilia}.}{There is no single percentage range for every laboratory. Many laboratories report about 0--6 percent, but the laboratory's own range must be used. Clinical eosinophilia is usually defined using the absolute eosinophil count: more than 500 cells per microlitre is eosinophilia, while 500--1,500 is mild, 1,500--5,000 is moderate, and more than 5,000 is severe according to the MSD Manual.}{A low percentage is usually not a problem by itself. It may simply reflect a higher percentage of another white-cell type. A low result is interpreted with the absolute count and the rest of the CBC, not alone.}{A high percentage may occur with an allergic disorder, a medicine reaction, a parasitic infection, an autoimmune or inflammatory disease, or some cancers and bone-marrow disorders. The absolute eosinophil count is needed to judge how large the increase is and whether it is persistent.}
\parameterentry{CBC differential percentage: Lymphocytes}{This is one part of a white-cell differential. It shows the percentage of white blood cells that are lymphocytes. Lymphocytes include B cells, T cells, and natural-killer cells, which help the immune system respond to infections and other threats. The result is reported with the total white-cell count and the absolute lymphocyte count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential} and \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/lymphocytic-leukocytosis}{MSD Manual Lymphocytic Leukocytosis}.}{MedlinePlus lists approximately 20--40 percent as a general adult percentage range for lymphocytes, but ranges vary between laboratories and with age. The absolute lymphocyte count is also important; the MSD Manual gives an adult reference count of about 1,000--4,800 cells per microlitre. Use the range printed by the laboratory that performed the test.}{A low percentage may occur because another white-cell type has increased, even when the absolute lymphocyte count is not low. A genuinely low absolute count may occur with some viral or bacterial infections, HIV, undernutrition, autoimmune disease, corticosteroid or chemotherapy treatment, or other disorders. The percentage alone cannot diagnose the cause.}{A high percentage may occur with a viral infection, including mononucleosis, and with some bacterial infections. A persistent high absolute count may also occur with a lymphoid blood cancer such as leukemia or lymphoma. The absolute count, blood smear, symptoms, and duration are needed for interpretation.}
\parameterentry{CBC differential percentage: Monocytes}{This is one part of a white-cell differential. It shows the percentage of white blood cells that are monocytes. Monocytes help the immune system respond to germs and clear away dead cells. The result is reported with the total white-cell count and the absolute monocyte count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential}.}{MedlinePlus gives about 2--8 percent as a general percentage range for monocytes, but ranges vary by laboratory, age, and method. The laboratory's own range must be used. A low absolute monocyte count, called monocytopenia, is generally defined as fewer than 200 cells per microlitre in the MSD Manual.}{A low percentage is often not meaningful by itself because another white-cell type may make up a larger share. A genuinely low absolute count may occur with a bloodstream infection, chemotherapy, corticosteroid or immunoglobulin treatment, or a bone-marrow disorder.}{A high percentage may occur with a long-term infection, an autoimmune or inflammatory disease, a blood disorder, or some cancers. A high percentage does not by itself prove that the absolute monocyte count is high; the total white-cell count and absolute count are needed. See \href{https://www.msdmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/monocyte-disorders}{MSD Manual Monocyte Disorders}.}
\parameterentry{CBC differential percentage: Segmented neutrophils}{This is one part of a white-cell differential. It shows the percentage of white blood cells that are mature neutrophils. Neutrophils are an important early defense against many bacterial and fungal infections. The result is reported with the total white-cell count and the absolute neutrophil count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/blood-differential/}{MedlinePlus Blood Differential}.}{MedlinePlus gives about 40--60 percent as a general adult percentage range for neutrophils, but the laboratory's own range must be used. The absolute neutrophil count is also important; the MSD Manual describes a usual lower limit of about 1,500 cells per microlitre, with variation between people and laboratories.}{A low percentage may occur because another white-cell type has increased, even when the absolute neutrophil count is not low. A genuinely low absolute count is neutropenia. Causes include some viral infections, chemotherapy or radiation, medicines, severe bacterial infection, vitamin deficiency, autoimmune disease, or reduced blood-cell production.}{A high percentage may occur with acute infection, inflammation, physical stress, injury, smoking, steroid treatment, thyroid inflammation, or a blood or bone-marrow disorder. The percentage alone cannot show whether the absolute neutrophil count is high or identify the cause.}
\parameterentry{Mentzer Index}{The Mentzer Index is a calculation: MCV divided by the red blood cell count. MCV is the average size of red blood cells, measured in femtolitres, and the RBC count is reported in millions of cells per microlitre. The calculation is used as an initial clue when the red cells are small, a pattern called microcytosis. It is mainly used to help distinguish iron-deficiency anemia from a thalassemia trait. See the original Mentzer publication listed in PubMed and \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}{MedlinePlus Iron Tests}.}{The Mentzer Index has no disease-specific normal range. A value below about 13 has traditionally been used to favour thalassemia trait, while a value above about 13 has traditionally been used to favour iron deficiency. These are screening cutoffs, not diagnostic limits. Their accuracy varies with age, population, the cause of microcytosis, and whether more than one condition is present.}{A low index may occur when the RBC count is relatively preserved despite small red cells, which can occur with thalassemia trait. It does not prove thalassemia. Confirmation may require hemoglobin analysis and, in selected cases, genetic testing.}{A high index may occur when the RBC count is lower in relation to the MCV, which can occur with iron deficiency. It does not prove iron deficiency. Confirmation requires iron studies, especially ferritin, interpreted with hemoglobin and the rest of the CBC.}

\parameterentry{1. White blood cell count (WBC)}{Counts all white blood cells in a blood sample. White blood cells help the body fight infections and respond to inflammation. The total WBC count is different from a differential count, which shows the amounts or percentages of neutrophils, lymphocytes, monocytes, eosinophils, and basophils. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/white-blood-count-wbc/}{MedlinePlus White Blood Count}.}{A commonly used adult range is about 4,000--11,000 cells per microlitre, or $4.0$--$11.0 \times10^3/\mu$L. The correct range depends on age, pregnancy, population, laboratory method, and the laboratory's own reference interval.}{A low WBC count is called leukopenia. It may occur with some viral infections, HIV, autoimmune disease, bone-marrow disease, cancer treatment, or medicines. A low count can make it harder to fight infection, but the risk depends especially on which white-cell type is low and on the absolute neutrophil count.}{A high WBC count is called leukocytosis. It may occur with infection, inflammation, physical stress, smoking, pregnancy, steroid treatment, or leukemia and other cancers. A high count does not identify the cause; the differential, symptoms, medicines, and trend are needed.}
\parameterentry{2. Red blood cell count (RBC)}{Counts the red blood cells in a blood sample. Red blood cells contain hemoglobin and carry oxygen from the lungs to the body's cells. The RBC count is usually measured as part of a complete blood count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/red-blood-cell-rbc-count/}{MedlinePlus Red Blood Cell Count}.}{MedlinePlus gives common adult ranges of about 4.2--5.7 million cells per microlitre for males and 3.8--5.1 million cells per microlitre for females. Ranges vary between laboratories and with age, pregnancy, altitude, and other factors. Use the range printed on the laboratory report.}{A low RBC count may occur with anemia, blood loss, pregnancy, kidney failure, poor nutrition, leukemia, multiple myeloma, or reduced red-cell production. The RBC count must be interpreted with hemoglobin, hematocrit, MCV, reticulocytes, and the clinical history.}{A high RBC count may occur with dehydration, smoking, lung or heart disease, sleep apnea, high altitude, testosterone or anabolic-steroid use, kidney cancer, or polycythemia vera. A high result may reflect reduced plasma water rather than a true increase in red-cell mass.}
\parameterentry{3. Hemoglobin}{Measures the hemoglobin concentration available to carry oxygen.}{Male 13.5--17.5; female 12.0--15.5 g/dL.}{Low hemoglobin indicates anemia from blood loss, deficiency, inflammation, kidney disease, or marrow disease.}{High hemoglobin may reflect dehydration, hypoxia, lung disease, smoking, or polycythemia.}
\parameterentry{4. Hematocrit}{Measures the percentage of blood made up of red blood cells. It is also called packed cell volume (PCV). If the hematocrit is 42, about 42 percent of the blood volume is red blood cells. It is usually reported as part of a complete blood count. See \href{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/hematocrit-test/}{MedlinePlus Hematocrit Test}.}{MedlinePlus gives common adult examples of about 37--48 percent for males and 34--43 percent for females. Other sources and laboratories may use different intervals. Age, sex, pregnancy, smoking, altitude, method, and hydration affect the result; use the range printed on the laboratory report.}{A low hematocrit may occur with anemia, recent or long-term blood loss, pregnancy-related dilution, overhydration, reduced red-cell production, kidney disease, cancer, or bone-marrow disease. It must be interpreted with hemoglobin, RBC count, and red-cell indices.}{A high hematocrit may occur when the body makes too many red cells, as in polycythemia, or when plasma is reduced, especially with dehydration. Lung disease, heart disease, sleep-related breathing problems, smoking, high altitude, and some medicines can also raise it. A high value does not by itself prove polycythemia.}
\parameterentry{5. Mean corpuscular volume (MCV)}{Measures average red-cell size and classifies anemia.}{80--100 fL.}{Low MCV suggests iron deficiency, thalassemia, or some chronic diseases.}{High MCV suggests vitamin B12 or folate deficiency, alcohol use, liver disease, hypothyroidism, medicines, or marrow disease.}
\parameterentry{6. Mean corpuscular hemoglobin (MCH)}{Measures the average hemoglobin amount in each red cell.}{27--33 pg/cell.}{Low MCH usually accompanies microcytosis and hypochromia, especially iron deficiency.}{High MCH commonly accompanies macrocytosis; it is interpreted with MCV rather than alone.}
\parameterentry{7. Mean corpuscular hemoglobin concentration (MCHC)}{Measures hemoglobin concentration within packed red cells.}{32--36 g/dL.}{Low MCHC suggests hypochromic anemia, commonly iron deficiency or thalassemia.}{High MCHC may occur with spherocytosis, red-cell dehydration, or analytical interference.}
\parameterentry{8. Red-cell distribution width (RDW)}{Measures variation in red-cell size.}{11.5--14.5 percent.}{A low RDW is usually not clinically important.}{High RDW indicates anisocytosis and may suggest iron, B12, or folate deficiency, mixed anemia, or recent transfusion.}
\parameterentry{9. Platelet count}{Counts platelets, the small blood cells that help stop bleeding.}{150--400 $\times10^3/\mu$L.}{A low count can increase the risk of bruising or bleeding. Causes include some infections, medicines, immune problems, or reduced blood-cell production.}{A high count may occur with inflammation or iron deficiency. Less often, it is caused by a bone-marrow disorder.}
\parameterentry{10. Mean platelet volume (MPV)}{Shows the average size of the platelets that help stop bleeding. It gives a clue about how quickly the body is making new platelets.}{7.5--11.5 fL.}{A low value may occur when the bone marrow is making fewer platelets.}{A high value may mean that many young platelets are being released, often after platelets have been destroyed or used up.}
\parameterentry{11. Neutrophil count}{Measures neutrophils, the main rapid cellular defense against bacteria and fungi.}{1.5--7.5 $\times10^3/\mu$L.}{Neutropenia increases infection risk, especially when severe or prolonged.}{Neutrophilia may accompany bacterial infection, inflammation, stress, steroids, smoking, or marrow disease.}
\parameterentry{12. Lymphocyte count}{Counts the white blood cells that help recognize viruses and other threats. These include B cells, T cells, and natural-killer cells.}{1.0--4.0 $\times10^3/\mu$L.}{A low count may occur after steroid treatment, during serious illness, with weak immunity, poor nutrition, or chemotherapy.}{A high count is common with viral infection. If it remains high, the doctor may check for a blood or lymph-node disorder.}
\parameterentry{13. Monocyte count}{Measures monocytes that participate in phagocytosis and chronic inflammation.}{0.2--0.8 $\times10^3/\mu$L.}{Low values are often nonspecific but may occur with marrow suppression.}{Monocytosis may occur with chronic infection, autoimmune disease, recovery from neutropenia, or leukemia.}
\parameterentry{14. Eosinophil count}{Measures eosinophils involved in allergic and parasitic responses.}{0.0--0.5 $\times10^3/\mu$L.}{Low values are generally not clinically significant.}{Eosinophilia suggests allergy, drug reaction, parasitic infection, eosinophilic disease, or selected malignancies.}
\parameterentry{15. Basophil count}{Measures basophils involved in histamine release and immune signaling.}{0.0--0.1 $\times10^3/\mu$L.}{Low values are usually not clinically significant.}{Basophilia may occur with allergy, chronic inflammation, hypothyroidism, or myeloproliferative disease.}
\parameterentry{16. Reticulocyte count}{Measures newly produced red cells and the marrow response to anemia.}{0.5--2.5 percent, interpreted with the absolute count.}{A low response suggests inadequate production from deficiency, kidney disease, inflammation, or marrow failure.}{A high response suggests blood loss, hemolysis, or recovery after treatment.}
\parameterentry{17. Reticulocyte hemoglobin (Ret-He)}{Estimates the hemoglobin content of newly released red cells and recent iron availability.}{Approximately 28--35 pg, assay dependent.}{Low Ret-He suggests iron-restricted erythropoiesis, sometimes before anemia develops.}{High values are usually not concerning and may reflect iron repletion or reticulocytosis.}
\parameterentry{18. Erythrocyte sedimentation rate (ESR)}{Measures the rate at which red cells settle and provides a nonspecific inflammation signal.}{Male 0--15; female 0--20 mm/h.}{A low ESR is usually not clinically important.}{High ESR may occur with inflammation, infection, autoimmune disease, anemia, kidney disease, age, or malignancy.}
\parameterentry{19. Peripheral blood smear}{Examines blood-cell size, shape, inclusions, parasites, and abnormal cells microscopically.}{A normal smear has expected cell size, shape, colour, maturity, and distribution for the person's age and clinical context. The report may be descriptive rather than a single number.}{A finding described as reduced cells, pale cells, abnormal maturation, or missing cell types may support anemia, deficiency, infection, marrow suppression, or a technical problem. The wording of the report and the CBC are needed; a smear does not have a simple ``low'' value.}{Abnormal forms, blasts, fragmented red cells, parasites, marked variation, or abnormal platelets may suggest a blood, marrow, infectious, or red-cell disorder. These findings require correlation and, when appropriate, confirmatory tests; they do not establish a diagnosis alone.}
\parameterentry{20. Immature granulocyte percentage}{Estimates early granulocytes released from marrow.}{Usually less than 1 percent.}{A low value is generally reassuring but does not exclude infection.}{An increased value suggests marrow stress, severe infection, inflammation, or recovery from neutropenia.}

\parameterentry{21. Prothrombin time (PT)}{Measures the extrinsic and common coagulation pathways.}{Approximately 11--14 seconds.}{A shortened PT is rarely clinically important.}{A prolonged PT may reflect warfarin, vitamin K deficiency, liver disease, disseminated coagulation, or factor deficiency.}
\parameterentry{22. International normalized ratio (INR)}{Standardizes PT results between laboratories and monitors vitamin K antagonist therapy.}{0.8--1.2 without anticoagulation; treatment targets are individualized.}{A low INR during warfarin therapy may mean inadequate anticoagulation and increased thrombosis risk.}{A high INR increases bleeding risk and may result from excess warfarin, illness, interactions, or liver dysfunction.}
\parameterentry{23. Activated partial thromboplastin time (aPTT)}{Measures the intrinsic and common coagulation pathways.}{25--35 seconds.}{A shortened aPTT is usually nonspecific.}{A prolonged aPTT may reflect heparin, factor deficiency, lupus anticoagulant, inhibitors, or severe liver disease.}
\parameterentry{24. Fibrinogen}{Measures a soluble protein converted to fibrin during clot formation.}{200--400 mg/dL.}{Low fibrinogen may cause bleeding in consumption, severe liver disease, dilution, or inherited deficiency.}{High fibrinogen is an acute-phase response and may accompany inflammation, infection, pregnancy, or cardiovascular risk.}
\parameterentry{25. D-dimer}{Detects fragments produced when cross-linked fibrin is degraded.}{Usually below 500 FEU ng/mL, age and assay dependent.}{A low result can help exclude venous thromboembolism when pretest probability is low.}{High D-dimer is nonspecific and occurs with thrombosis, infection, inflammation, pregnancy, cancer, trauma, or aging.}
\parameterentry{26. Thrombin time}{Measures conversion of fibrinogen to fibrin after added thrombin.}{Approximately 14--19 seconds.}{A short result is rarely significant.}{A prolonged result suggests heparin or direct thrombin inhibitor effect, low or abnormal fibrinogen, or fibrin degradation products.}
\parameterentry{27. Factor VIII activity}{Measures activity of coagulation factor VIII.}{50--150 percent.}{Low activity supports hemophilia A or acquired factor VIII inhibition.}{High activity can occur with inflammation, pregnancy, or increased thrombosis risk.}
\parameterentry{28. von Willebrand factor antigen}{Measures the amount of von Willebrand protein.}{Approximately 50--200 percent, assay dependent.}{Low antigen supports quantitative von Willebrand disease.}{High antigen is an acute-phase response and may occur with inflammation, stress, pregnancy, or endothelial activation.}
\parameterentry{29. Antithrombin activity}{Measures a natural anticoagulant that inhibits thrombin and factor Xa.}{80--120 percent.}{Low activity may cause inherited or acquired thrombophilia and heparin resistance.}{High values are usually not clinically important.}
\parameterentry{30. Lupus anticoagulant screen}{Screens for phospholipid-dependent inhibitors associated with antiphospholipid syndrome.}{Negative.}{A negative result reduces support for lupus anticoagulant but does not exclude all antiphospholipid antibodies.}{A positive result may indicate antiphospholipid syndrome but requires confirmatory testing and persistent positivity.}

\parameterentry{31. Fasting glucose}{Measures circulating glucose after a defined fast.}{70--99 mg/dL.}{Low glucose may cause sweating, tremor, confusion, seizures, or coma and may result from medicines, endocrine disease, liver disease, or sepsis.}{High glucose suggests diabetes, stress hyperglycemia, endocrine disease, or medication effect.}
\parameterentry{Additional diabetes parameter: Estimated average glucose (eAG)}{Converts an HbA1c result into an approximate average glucose concentration so it can be compared with glucose-meter readings. It is calculated, not directly measured.}{There is no separate reference range; examples are approximately 117 mg/dL for HbA1c 5.7 percent, 126 mg/dL for 6 percent, and 154 mg/dL for 7 percent.}{A low eAG reflects a low HbA1c estimate but may be misleading when red-cell survival is shortened or hypoglycemia is recurrent.}{A high eAG reflects a higher HbA1c estimate and sustained average hyperglycemia, but it may be inaccurate with anemia, hemoglobin variants, kidney disease, liver disease, transfusion, or pregnancy.}
\parameterentry{32. Hemoglobin A1c (HbA1c)}{Estimates average glycemia over approximately two to three months.}{About 4.0--5.6 percent in people without diabetes.}{Low values may reflect recurrent hypoglycemia or shortened red-cell survival and can be misleading.}{High values indicate chronic hyperglycemia and increased risk of diabetes complications.}
\parameterentry{Additional kidney profile: Urea}{Measures the complete urea molecule produced by hepatic protein metabolism and excreted mainly by the kidneys. It differs from BUN, which reports only the nitrogen component of urea.}{Laboratory- and unit-dependent; urea is often reported in mmol/L, while BUN is reported in mg/dL. The conversion and local interval must be checked before comparison.}{Low urea may occur with low protein intake, severe liver dysfunction, malnutrition, or overhydration.}{High urea may occur with reduced filtration, dehydration, gastrointestinal bleeding, high protein intake, catabolism, or corticosteroid effect.}
\parameterentry{Additional kidney profile: BUN/creatinine ratio}{Calculates BUN divided by creatinine and provides limited context about hydration, renal perfusion, and nitrogen production.}{Often approximately 10:1--20:1 when both results are expressed in conventional units.}{A low ratio may occur with low protein intake, liver disease, or reduced urea production.}{A high ratio may occur with dehydration, reduced renal perfusion, gastrointestinal bleeding, catabolism, or high protein intake; it is not diagnostic alone.}
\parameterentry{33. Blood urea nitrogen (BUN)}{Measures nitrogen waste generated from protein metabolism and excreted by the kidneys.}{7--20 mg/dL.}{Low BUN may occur with low protein intake, liver disease, or overhydration.}{High BUN may reflect reduced filtration, dehydration, gastrointestinal bleeding, catabolism, or high protein intake.}
\parameterentry{34. Creatinine}{Measures a muscle-derived waste product used to estimate filtration.}{Male 0.74--1.35; female 0.59--1.04 mg/dL.}{Low creatinine commonly reflects low muscle mass or malnutrition.}{High creatinine suggests reduced filtration, acute kidney injury, obstruction, dehydration, or medication effect.}
\parameterentry{35. Estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR)}{Estimates kidney filtration from serum creatinine and demographic variables; some equations also use cystatin C. It is an estimate, not a direct measurement of filtration.}{G1: $\geq90$; G2: 60--89; G3a: 45--59; G3b: 30--44; G4: 15--29; G5: $<15$ mL/min/1.73 m$^2$. Chronic kidney disease requires persistent abnormality or another marker of kidney damage, not eGFR alone.}{Low eGFR suggests reduced filtration, but acute kidney injury, dehydration, unusual muscle mass, diet, medicines, and unstable creatinine can distort the estimate.}{An apparently high eGFR may reflect hyperfiltration or estimation limitations and is not automatically evidence of unusually healthy kidneys.}
\parameterentry{36. Uric acid}{Measures the end product of purine metabolism.}{Male 3.4--7.0; female 2.4--6.0 mg/dL.}{Low uric acid may occur with urate-lowering therapy, some inherited disorders, or increased renal loss.}{High uric acid increases gout and uric-acid stone risk and may accompany kidney disease or high cell turnover.}
\parameterentry{37. Sodium}{Measures the main extracellular cation and reflects water balance.}{135--145 mmol/L.}{Hyponatremia may cause headache, confusion, seizures, and falls and may result from excess water, SIADH, heart failure, or sodium loss.}{Hypernatremia usually reflects water deficit and may cause thirst, weakness, confusion, or neurologic injury.}
\parameterentry{38. Potassium}{Measures an essential intracellular cation affecting muscle and cardiac electrical activity.}{3.5--5.0 mmol/L.}{Hypokalemia may cause weakness, cramps, ileus, and arrhythmias.}{Hyperkalemia can cause life-threatening conduction abnormalities and may result from kidney failure, medicines, acidosis, or hemolysis.}
\parameterentry{39. Chloride}{Measures an extracellular anion involved in fluid and acid--base balance.}{98--106 mmol/L.}{Low chloride may accompany vomiting, diuretics, or metabolic alkalosis.}{High chloride may occur with dehydration, saline administration, renal tubular disorders, or metabolic acidosis.}
\parameterentry{40. Bicarbonate (total CO$_2$)}{Estimates the major serum buffer and helps assess metabolic acid--base status.}{22--29 mmol/L.}{Low bicarbonate suggests metabolic acidosis or compensation for respiratory alkalosis.}{High bicarbonate suggests metabolic alkalosis or compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis.}
\parameterentry{41. Anion gap}{Calculates the difference between measured cations and anions to estimate unmeasured acids.}{8--12 mmol/L without potassium.}{A low gap may reflect low albumin, paraproteins, or analytical error.}{A high gap suggests lactic acidosis, ketoacidosis, kidney failure, toxins, or other unmeasured acids.}
\parameterentry{42. Total calcium}{Measures bound plus free circulating calcium.}{8.5--10.5 mg/dL.}{Low calcium may cause tingling, cramps, tetany, seizures, or arrhythmia and may reflect vitamin D, parathyroid, kidney, or albumin abnormalities.}{High calcium may cause stones, constipation, confusion, weakness, and arrhythmia and may reflect hyperparathyroidism or malignancy.}
\parameterentry{43. Ionized calcium}{Measures biologically active, unbound calcium.}{1.12--1.32 mmol/L.}{Low ionized calcium increases neuromuscular irritability and may occur in critical illness, alkalosis, or parathyroid disease.}{High ionized calcium supports true hypercalcemia and may result from parathyroid disease, malignancy, or vitamin excess.}
\parameterentry{44. Magnesium}{Measures a mineral important for neuromuscular and cardiac function.}{1.7--2.2 mg/dL.}{Low magnesium may cause tremor, weakness, seizures, refractory hypokalemia, and arrhythmias.}{High magnesium usually reflects kidney failure or excess replacement and can cause hypotension, weakness, and respiratory depression.}
\parameterentry{45. Phosphate}{Measures inorganic phosphate involved in bone, energy, and cell function.}{2.5--4.5 mg/dL.}{Low phosphate may cause weakness, respiratory failure, bone disease, or impaired energy metabolism.}{High phosphate commonly reflects kidney failure, cell breakdown, hypoparathyroidism, or phosphate loading.}
\parameterentry{46. Total protein}{Measures albumin plus globulin proteins in serum.}{6.0--8.3 g/dL.}{Low protein may reflect malnutrition, liver synthesis failure, kidney or gastrointestinal loss, or inflammation.}{High protein may reflect dehydration, chronic inflammation, or monoclonal gammopathy.}
\parameterentry{47. Albumin}{Measures the major plasma protein synthesized by the liver.}{3.5--5.0 g/dL.}{Low albumin may reflect inflammation, liver disease, malnutrition, kidney loss, or gastrointestinal loss and can cause edema.}{High albumin usually reflects dehydration rather than increased production.}
\parameterentry{Additional liver profile: Globulin, calculated}{Estimates circulating globulins by subtracting albumin from total protein. It includes immunoglobulins and other proteins and is not a direct measurement of each fraction.}{Approximately 2.0--3.5 g/dL, laboratory dependent.}{Low globulin may reflect antibody deficiency, protein loss, malnutrition, or impaired production.}{High globulin may reflect chronic inflammation, infection, autoimmune disease, liver disease, or monoclonal gammopathy; protein electrophoresis may be needed.}
\parameterentry{Additional liver profile: Albumin-to-globulin ratio (A/G ratio)}{Calculates albumin divided by calculated globulin and compares two major serum-protein groups.}{Approximately 1.0--2.5, laboratory dependent.}{A low A/G ratio may reflect low albumin, increased globulins, chronic inflammation, liver disease, kidney or gastrointestinal protein loss, or gammopathy.}{A high A/G ratio may reflect low globulin, antibody deficiency, or dehydration and should be interpreted with albumin and total protein.}
\parameterentry{Additional liver profile: AST-to-ALT ratio}{Calculates AST divided by ALT to provide context for an aminotransferase pattern.}{No universal normal or diagnostic cutoff; the ratio depends on both enzyme values and the assay.}{A low ratio is usually not interpreted alone and may occur when ALT predominates in hepatocellular injury.}{A ratio above about 2 can support, but cannot diagnose, alcohol-associated liver disease; it may also occur with advanced fibrosis, muscle injury, or other causes of AST elevation.}
\parameterentry{48. Total bilirubin}{Measures unconjugated and conjugated bilirubin.}{0.2--1.2 mg/dL.}{Low bilirubin is generally not significant.}{High bilirubin may cause jaundice and reflect hemolysis, impaired conjugation, liver injury, or biliary obstruction.}
\parameterentry{49. Direct bilirubin}{Measures conjugated bilirubin that has been processed by the liver.}{0.0--0.3 mg/dL.}{Low direct bilirubin is generally not significant.}{High direct bilirubin suggests hepatocellular excretory dysfunction or cholestasis.}
\parameterentry{50. Alkaline phosphatase (ALP)}{Measures enzymes concentrated in bile ducts and bone.}{44--147 U/L.}{Low ALP may occur with malnutrition, zinc deficiency, hypothyroidism, or rare metabolic disease.}{High ALP suggests cholestasis or increased bone turnover; GGT or imaging helps identify the source.}
\parameterentry{51. Alanine aminotransferase (ALT)}{Measures an enzyme relatively concentrated in liver cells.}{7--56 U/L.}{Low ALT is usually not clinically important.}{High ALT suggests hepatocellular injury from fatty liver, viral hepatitis, medicines, toxins, or ischemia.}
\parameterentry{52. Aspartate aminotransferase (AST)}{Measures an enzyme found in liver, skeletal muscle, cardiac muscle, and red cells.}{10--40 U/L.}{Low AST is usually not clinically important.}{High AST may reflect liver injury, muscle injury, hemolysis, alcohol-associated disease, or ischemia.}
\parameterentry{53. Gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT)}{Measures an enzyme associated with biliary epithelium and microsomal enzyme induction.}{Male 9--48; female 7--32 U/L.}{Low GGT is generally not significant.}{High GGT supports a hepatobiliary source of ALP elevation but is also increased by alcohol and medicines.}
\parameterentry{54. Lactate dehydrogenase (LDH)}{Measures an enzyme released with tissue injury and cell breakdown.}{140--280 U/L.}{Low LDH is rarely clinically important.}{High LDH is nonspecific and may reflect hemolysis, liver or muscle injury, tissue ischemia, infection, or malignancy.}
\parameterentry{55. Amylase}{Measures an enzyme produced mainly by pancreas and salivary glands.}{30--110 U/L.}{Low amylase may occur with advanced pancreatic damage or rarely with low enzyme production.}{High amylase may occur in pancreatitis, salivary disease, kidney failure, or macroamylasemia.}

\parameterentry{56. Total cholesterol}{Measures cholesterol carried in all major lipoprotein fractions.}{Desirable below 200 mg/dL.}{Very low cholesterol may accompany malnutrition, hyperthyroidism, malabsorption, or chronic illness.}{High cholesterol increases atherosclerotic risk and may result from genetics, diet, hypothyroidism, kidney disease, or cholestasis.}
\parameterentry{57. LDL cholesterol}{Estimates cholesterol in atherogenic low-density lipoprotein particles.}{Often below 100 mg/dL; targets depend on cardiovascular risk.}{Low LDL is usually beneficial, though unexpectedly low values may prompt assessment for malnutrition or illness.}{High LDL increases atherosclerotic cardiovascular risk and may require lifestyle and lipid-lowering therapy.}
\parameterentry{58. HDL cholesterol}{Measures cholesterol carried in high-density lipoprotein particles.}{Male at least 40; female at least 50 mg/dL.}{Low HDL is associated with metabolic and cardiovascular risk but is not usually treated as an isolated target.}{High HDL is not automatically protective; very high values may be influenced by genetics, alcohol, or disease.}
\parameterentry{59. Triglycerides}{Measures circulating neutral fats carried mainly in VLDL and chylomicrons.}{Below 150 mg/dL fasting.}{Low triglycerides are usually not clinically important but may occur with malnutrition or malabsorption.}{High triglycerides increase metabolic risk; very high levels increase pancreatitis risk.}
\parameterentry{60. Non-HDL cholesterol}{Calculates total cholesterol minus HDL cholesterol and estimates all atherogenic cholesterol.}{Often below 130 mg/dL, risk dependent.}{Low values are generally not concerning.}{High non-HDL cholesterol indicates increased atherogenic particle cholesterol and cardiovascular risk.}
\parameterentry{61. Apolipoprotein B (ApoB)}{Measures apolipoprotein B on circulating atherogenic particles, including LDL, VLDL, IDL, and some remnant particles. It therefore estimates particle number, whereas LDL cholesterol measures the amount of cholesterol carried within LDL particles.}{There is no single universal treatment target. A laboratory may report a reference interval around 60--130 mg/dL, while clinical goals depend on overall cardiovascular risk and the treatment guideline used.}{Low ApoB is generally favorable and is not usually a clinical problem by itself. Unexpectedly low values may occur with low lipoprotein production, malnutrition, malabsorption, severe liver disease, or some inherited disorders and should be interpreted with the full clinical picture.}{High ApoB indicates a greater number of atherogenic particles and may reveal residual risk when LDL cholesterol appears acceptable. It is particularly useful to consider with high triglycerides, type 2 diabetes, metabolic syndrome, cardiovascular-kidney-metabolic syndrome, or discordance between LDL-C and non-HDL-C. An ApoB value of 130 mg/dL or higher has traditionally been treated as a risk-enhancing level, not as a stand-alone diagnosis or automatic treatment decision.}
\parameterentry{62. Lipoprotein(a) [Lp(a)]}{Measures an inherited atherogenic lipoprotein particle.}{Desirable below 75 nmol/L, assay dependent.}{Low Lp(a) is not known to cause disease.}{High Lp(a) is an inherited risk factor for atherosclerotic disease and aortic-valve stenosis.}
\parameterentry{63. High-sensitivity CRP (hs-CRP)}{Measures very small increases in C-reactive protein and may add information to cardiovascular-risk assessment in clinically stable people. It is different from a standard CRP test, which is generally used to assess more evident inflammation or infection.}{For cardiovascular-risk interpretation in a stable person: below 1 mg/L is lower relative risk, 1--3 mg/L is average relative risk, and above 3 mg/L is higher relative risk. These are not disease-diagnostic cutoffs.}{A low hs-CRP does not exclude all inflammation or cardiovascular disease; it means little measurable hs-CRP was present at collection.}{An elevated value may reflect infection, autoimmune inflammation, obesity, smoking, tissue injury, or increased cardiovascular risk. Values above 10 mg/L should generally be repeated when the person is clinically stable rather than used directly for cardiovascular-risk stratification. The result must be interpreted with age, blood pressure, lipids, diabetes, smoking, and overall risk.}

\parameterentry{64. Thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)}{Measures pituitary stimulation of the thyroid and is the usual first-line thyroid test.}{0.4--4.0 mIU/L, age and pregnancy dependent.}{Low TSH suggests hyperthyroidism, excess replacement, or pituitary disease.}{High TSH suggests primary hypothyroidism, thyroid failure, or inadequate replacement.}
\parameterentry{65. Free thyroxine (free T4)}{Measures unbound thyroxine available to tissues.}{0.8--1.8 ng/dL, assay dependent.}{Low free T4 supports hypothyroidism; with a non-high TSH, central hypothyroidism is possible.}{High free T4 supports thyrotoxicosis, excess replacement, or assay interference.}
\parameterentry{66. Total triiodothyronine (T3)}{Measures circulating T3, the more active thyroid hormone.}{80--200 ng/dL.}{Low T3 may occur in hypothyroidism or non-thyroidal illness and is not diagnostic alone.}{High T3 supports T3-predominant hyperthyroidism or excess replacement.}
\parameterentry{67. Parathyroid hormone (PTH)}{Measures the hormone regulating calcium and phosphate balance.}{10--65 pg/mL.}{Low PTH may cause hypocalcemia and suggests hypoparathyroidism or severe magnesium disturbance.}{High PTH may reflect primary hyperparathyroidism, vitamin D deficiency, or chronic kidney disease.}
\parameterentry{68. Morning cortisol}{Measures adrenal glucocorticoid production near its daily peak.}{Approximately 5--25 $\mu$g/dL at 8 a.m., assay dependent.}{Low cortisol may indicate adrenal insufficiency, especially with compatible symptoms.}{High cortisol may reflect stress, medicines, Cushing syndrome, or altered binding proteins.}
\parameterentry{69. ACTH}{Measures pituitary stimulation of the adrenal cortex.}{Approximately 10--60 pg/mL at 8 a.m.}{Low ACTH may accompany pituitary disease or exogenous glucocorticoid suppression.}{High ACTH may occur in primary adrenal insufficiency or ACTH-dependent Cushing syndrome.}
\parameterentry{70. Fasting insulin}{Measures circulating insulin after fasting.}{Approximately 2--25 $\mu$IU/mL.}{Low insulin may occur with pancreatic beta-cell failure or exogenous insulin use.}{High insulin suggests insulin resistance, obesity, medications, or endogenous hyperinsulinism.}
\parameterentry{71. C-peptide}{Measures endogenous insulin production because it is released with native insulin.}{Approximately 0.8--3.5 ng/mL fasting.}{Low C-peptide indicates low endogenous insulin production or suppression by exogenous insulin.}{High C-peptide suggests insulin resistance or endogenous hyperinsulinism.}
\parameterentry{72. Prolactin}{Measures pituitary hormone affecting lactation and reproductive function.}{Male 4--15; female 4--23 ng/mL, nonpregnant and assay dependent.}{Low prolactin is rarely clinically important.}{High prolactin may reflect pregnancy, medicines, hypothyroidism, pituitary adenoma, stress, or kidney disease.}
\parameterentry{73. Follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH)}{Measures follicle-stimulating hormone released by the pituitary gland. In women it stimulates ovarian follicle development; in men it supports sperm production. It is usually interpreted with LH and, when relevant, estradiol, progesterone, testosterone, semen analysis, or thyroid testing.}{Highly dependent on sex, age, puberty, menstrual-cycle phase, pregnancy, menopause, and assay. Illustrative adult values include male 1.5--12.4 IU/L, menstruating female 4.7--21.5 IU/L, and postmenopausal female 25.8--134.8 IU/L. The laboratory's interval and cycle-day instructions take precedence.}{Low FSH may reflect hypothalamic or pituitary dysfunction, pregnancy, rapid weight loss, very low body weight, excessive exercise, chronic illness, or hormonal suppression. In combination with low sex hormones, it may suggest secondary hypogonadism.}{High FSH may indicate menopause, primary ovarian insufficiency, impaired ovarian function, primary testicular failure, some chromosomal disorders, gonadal injury from chemotherapy or radiation, or certain tumors. High FSH in a child with early pubertal signs may support central precocious puberty.}
\parameterentry{74. Luteinising hormone (LH)}{Measures luteinizing hormone released by the pituitary gland. In women it helps regulate the menstrual cycle and triggers ovulation; in men it stimulates testicular testosterone production. It is usually interpreted with FSH and, when relevant, estradiol, progesterone, or testosterone.}{Highly dependent on sex, age, menstrual-cycle phase, and assay. Illustrative adult values are male 1.8--8.6 IU/L; female 5--25 IU/L before menopause with a higher mid-cycle surge; and approximately 7.7--58.5 IU/L after menopause. The laboratory's interval and cycle-day instructions take precedence.}{Low LH in adults may suggest hypothalamic or pituitary dysfunction, suppression by sex hormones, severe undernutrition, excessive exercise, chronic illness, or stress. In a person with missed periods or low testosterone, it may indicate secondary hypogonadism.}{High LH may occur around ovulation, after menopause, with primary ovarian or testicular failure, some chromosomal conditions, or certain causes of early puberty. Persistently high LH in reproductive-age women may occur with PCOS, but LH alone cannot diagnose PCOS.}
\parameterentry{75. Total testosterone}{Measures circulating testosterone, mostly protein bound.}{Adult male about 300--1000; female 15--70 ng/dL, time and assay dependent.}{Low values may indicate hypogonadism, pituitary disease, illness, or medication effect.}{High values may reflect therapy, anabolic steroids, ovarian or adrenal androgen excess, or assay interference.}

\parameterentry{76. Ferritin}{Estimates stored iron but also behaves as an acute-phase protein.}{Male 30--400; female 13--150 ng/mL, assay dependent.}{Low ferritin strongly supports iron deficiency.}{High ferritin may reflect iron overload, inflammation, liver disease, infection, malignancy, or metabolic disease.}
\parameterentry{77. Serum iron}{Measures the amount of circulating iron, much of it bound to transferrin, at the time of collection. It is highly variable and should not be interpreted alone.}{Illustrative interval: male 59--158; female 37--145 $\mu$g/dL. The performing laboratory's interval takes precedence.}{Low serum iron may occur with iron deficiency, blood loss, reduced absorption, pregnancy, or inflammation. A low value does not by itself prove iron deficiency.}{High serum iron may follow recent iron ingestion and may occur with iron overload, hemolysis, liver injury, iron poisoning, ineffective erythropoiesis, or repeated transfusion.}
\parameterentry{78. Total iron-binding capacity (TIBC)}{Estimates how much additional iron can bind to transferrin and other binding proteins in plasma. TIBC generally changes in the opposite direction to transferrin availability.}{Approximately 250--450 $\mu$g/dL, with method- and laboratory-dependent variation.}{Low TIBC may occur with inflammation, malnutrition, liver disease, protein loss, or reduced transferrin production; it can obscure iron deficiency.}{High TIBC commonly supports iron deficiency because the liver produces more transferrin, but it must be interpreted with ferritin and transferrin saturation.}
\parameterentry{79. Transferrin saturation (TSAT)}{Calculates the percentage of transferrin-binding capacity occupied by iron: serum iron divided by TIBC, multiplied by 100.}{Illustrative interval: male 20--50 percent; female 15--45 percent.}{TSAT below about 20 percent supports insufficient circulating iron for erythropoiesis, especially when ferritin is low; inflammation can also lower TSAT.}{TSAT above about 45--50 percent may suggest iron overload, hereditary hemochromatosis, repeated transfusion, iron poisoning, or ineffective erythropoiesis. Confirmation usually includes ferritin, repeat testing, liver assessment, and clinical review.}
\parameterentry{80. Vitamin B12}{Measures circulating cobalamin needed for DNA synthesis and neurologic function.}{200--900 pg/mL.}{Low B12 may cause macrocytic anemia, neuropathy, cognitive symptoms, and glossitis.}{High B12 may reflect supplementation, liver disease, kidney disease, inflammation, or malignancy.}
\parameterentry{81. Folate}{Measures folate available for DNA synthesis and red-cell production.}{Usually above 4 ng/mL.}{Low folate may cause megaloblastic anemia, glossitis, and fetal neural-tube risk in pregnancy.}{High folate usually reflects supplementation and is rarely harmful by itself.}
\parameterentry{82. 25-hydroxyvitamin D3 [25(OH)D]}{Measures the main circulating vitamin D storage form, also called calcidiol. Total 25(OH)D usually combines vitamin D2 and D3 and is the preferred blood marker of vitamin D status; the active 1,25-dihydroxyvitamin D test is different.}{NIH guidance considers below 12 ng/mL (30 nmol/L) deficient, 12 to below 20 ng/mL (30 to below 50 nmol/L) generally inadequate, and 20 ng/mL (50 nmol/L) or more adequate for most people. Values above 50 ng/mL (125 nmol/L) may be associated with adverse effects. Assay and clinical context matter.}{Low 25(OH)D may result from limited sun exposure, low intake, malabsorption, obesity, liver or kidney disease, or medicines. It may contribute to impaired bone mineralization, osteomalacia, muscle weakness, or secondary hyperparathyroidism.}{High 25(OH)D is usually caused by excessive supplementation and can lead to hypercalcemia, nausea, confusion, kidney stones, or kidney injury. Do not treat a high result by stopping or changing medicines without medical advice.}
\parameterentry{83. C-reactive protein (CRP)}{Measures an acute-phase protein that rises with systemic inflammation.}{Below 5 mg/L.}{Low CRP generally indicates little measurable acute inflammation.}{High CRP supports inflammation or infection but does not identify the cause or location.}
\parameterentry{84. Procalcitonin}{Measures a peptide that may rise with significant bacterial infection.}{Below 0.05 ng/mL in healthy adults.}{A low result lowers the probability of some bacterial infections but does not exclude localized or early disease.}{High procalcitonin supports systemic bacterial infection in the right context but can rise after trauma, surgery, or severe shock.}

\parameterentry{85. High-sensitivity troponin I or T}{Measures cardiac muscle protein released with myocardial injury.}{Below the assay-specific 99th percentile.}{A low serial result makes acute myocardial infarction less likely when timing and symptoms are appropriate.}{High troponin indicates myocardial injury; infarction requires a rise or fall plus clinical evidence of ischemia.}
\parameterentry{86. Creatine kinase (CK)}{Measures an enzyme released mainly from skeletal muscle, with smaller cardiac and brain contributions.}{Male 39--308; female 26--192 U/L.}{Low CK is usually not clinically important and may reflect low muscle mass.}{High CK suggests muscle injury, vigorous exercise, myositis, seizures, or rhabdomyolysis.}
\parameterentry{87. CK-MB}{Measures a creatine-kinase isoenzyme associated with cardiac muscle.}{Usually below 5 ng/mL.}{Low CK-MB is generally not significant.}{High CK-MB may support myocardial injury but can also occur with skeletal muscle injury and is less specific than troponin.}
\parameterentry{88. B-type natriuretic peptide (BNP)}{Measures a cardiac hormone released with ventricular wall stress.}{Below 100 pg/mL, age and context dependent.}{A low BNP makes acute heart failure less likely in many settings.}{High BNP supports cardiac wall stress but may also occur with kidney disease, pulmonary disease, age, or atrial fibrillation.}
\parameterentry{89. N-terminal pro-BNP (NT-proBNP)}{Measures an inactive fragment released with cardiac wall stress.}{Often below 125 pg/mL in outpatients; age dependent.}{A low result makes significant heart failure less likely in appropriate settings.}{High values support heart failure or cardiac strain but are affected by age, kidney disease, rhythm, and obesity.}
\parameterentry{90. Myoglobin}{Measures a muscle oxygen-binding protein released early after muscle injury.}{Male 28--72; female 25--58 ng/mL.}{Low myoglobin is not clinically significant.}{High myoglobin may indicate skeletal or cardiac muscle injury and can contribute to kidney injury in rhabdomyolysis.}

\parameterentry{91. Immunoglobulin G (IgG)}{Measures the major circulating antibody class responsible for long-term humoral immunity.}{700--1600 mg/dL.}{Low IgG may cause recurrent infections and occur with immune deficiency, protein loss, or medicines.}{High IgG may reflect chronic infection, autoimmune disease, liver disease, or monoclonal gammopathy.}
\parameterentry{92. Immunoglobulin A (IgA)}{Measures an antibody important in mucosal defense.}{70--400 mg/dL.}{Low IgA may cause recurrent mucosal infections and complicate celiac and antibody testing.}{High IgA may occur with chronic inflammation, liver disease, infection, or gammopathy.}
\parameterentry{93. Immunoglobulin M (IgM)}{Measures the antibody class often produced early in an immune response.}{40--230 mg/dL.}{Low IgM may occur with immune deficiency or protein loss.}{High IgM may reflect recent infection, chronic inflammation, or an IgM monoclonal gammopathy.}
\parameterentry{94. Complement C3}{Measures a central complement protein consumed in some immune-complex diseases.}{90--180 mg/dL.}{Low C3 may indicate complement consumption in lupus, infection, glomerulonephritis, or inherited deficiency.}{High C3 is an acute-phase response and may occur with inflammation or infection.}
\parameterentry{95. Complement C4}{Measures a complement protein useful in immune-complex disease assessment.}{10--40 mg/dL.}{Low C4 may occur in lupus, cryoglobulinemia, hereditary angioedema, or complement consumption.}{High C4 may accompany acute inflammation and is usually nonspecific.}
\parameterentry{96. Antinuclear antibody (ANA)}{Screens for antibodies directed against nuclear components.}{Negative; titers and patterns are assay dependent.}{A negative ANA reduces the likelihood of some connective-tissue diseases but does not exclude all autoimmune disease.}{A positive ANA may occur in autoimmune disease, infection, medicines, or healthy people; titer and symptoms determine significance.}
\parameterentry{97. Rheumatoid factor (RF)}{Measures an autoantibody commonly associated with rheumatoid arthritis.}{Below 14 IU/mL, assay dependent.}{A negative RF does not exclude rheumatoid arthritis.}{A positive RF supports but does not diagnose rheumatoid arthritis and may occur in infection, other autoimmune disease, age, or healthy people.}
\parameterentry{98. Anti-cyclic citrullinated peptide antibody (anti-CCP)}{Measures an antibody with relatively high specificity for rheumatoid arthritis.}{Negative, commonly below 20 U/mL.}{A negative result does not exclude seronegative rheumatoid arthritis.}{A positive result strongly supports rheumatoid arthritis in the right clinical setting and may predict erosive disease.}
\parameterentry{99. HIV-1/2 antigen/antibody screen}{Detects HIV p24 antigen and/or antibodies as an initial screening test.}{Nonreactive.}{A nonreactive result usually indicates no laboratory evidence of HIV, but very early infection may require repeat or nucleic-acid testing.}{A reactive result is preliminary and requires an approved supplemental confirmatory algorithm.}
\parameterentry{100. Hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg)}{Detects a surface protein of hepatitis B virus and screens for current infection.}{Nonreactive.}{A nonreactive result usually indicates no detectable current infection but does not prove immunity or exclude very early infection.}{A reactive result suggests current hepatitis B infection and requires confirmatory and additional serologic testing.}

\section{Pre-analytical considerations}
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item Confirm the specimen type, collection tube, patient identity, and collection time before testing.
  \item Fasting is commonly required or preferred for fasting glucose, triglycerides, and some metabolic investigations; follow the laboratory's instructions.
  \item Hemolysis can falsely increase potassium, LDH, AST, and other analytes. Lipemia, icterus, delayed separation, and contamination from intravenous fluids can also affect results.
  \item Medication timing is important for therapeutic drug monitoring and for hormones with diurnal or cycle-related variation.
  \item A critical or unexpected result should be verified according to local laboratory and clinical protocols rather than interpreted from this table alone.
\end{itemize}

\section{Scope and next revision}
This is an initial draft. A subsequent revision should add assay-specific sources,
age- and pregnancy-specific intervals, critical values, specimen stability,
conversion units, and references to current laboratory medicine guidelines.

\section{How Medical Authorities and Standards Should Be Used}

The organizations named in laboratory reports do not all have the same role.
This distinction is important when reviewing a multi-test package or describing
whether a blood test is ``approved,'' ``recommended,'' or ``accredited.'' A
test is a measurement performed by a laboratory; an in-vitro diagnostic (IVD)
device is the kit, reagent, analyzer, software, or system used to produce that
measurement. The regulatory status of a particular IVD does not by itself prove
that the test is appropriate for every patient.

\subsection*{India: regulation, accreditation, guidance, and professional governance}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item \textbf{CDSCO and the Central Licensing Authority:} The Central Drugs
  Standard Control Organisation regulates IVD medical devices in India under
  the Medical Devices Rules, 2017. Its responsibilities include risk
  classification, import, manufacture of higher-risk devices, clinical
  performance evaluation, permissions, and post-market regulatory activities.
  This is the appropriate Indian source for claims about authorization or
  regulatory requirements for a commercial IVD, not for deciding whether a
  screening panel is medically necessary.
  \item \textbf{State drug-control authorities:} State licensing authorities
  have defined responsibilities for manufacturing, sale, stocking, and
  distribution of IVDs, particularly for the relevant risk classes. A state
  drug licence is different from laboratory accreditation and from a doctor's
  clinical recommendation.
  \item \textbf{NABL and ISO 15189:} The National Accreditation Board for
  Testing and Calibration Laboratories provides voluntary accreditation of
  medical laboratories against ISO 15189 requirements for quality and
  competence. Accreditation should be checked for the specific laboratory and
  scope of tests; it is not evidence that every advertised package is useful,
  that every analyte is individually accredited, or that an abnormal result is
  a diagnosis.
  \item \textbf{ICMR and MoHFW:} The Indian Council of Medical Research can
  provide research evidence, technical guidance, and performance-evaluation
  work, while the Ministry of Health and Family Welfare develops national
  policy and programmes. Neither should be described as the body that
  individually approves every routine blood-test result or every laboratory
  package.
  \item \textbf{National Medical Commission and State Medical Councils:}
  These bodies primarily govern medical education, registration, professional
  standards, and ethical conduct. They do not function as the regulator that
  authorizes each assay or certifies the medical necessity of a commercial
  health package. Professional rules and local legal requirements still apply
  to how tests are ordered, reported, and explained.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{United States and international authorities}

\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item \textbf{FDA:} The U.S. Food and Drug Administration regulates IVD
  products as medical devices through routes such as 510(k) clearance, De Novo
  classification, premarket approval, emergency authorization, labeling, and
  post-market controls. The report should name the specific product and
  intended use when making an FDA claim; it should not say that ``FDA approved
  the test'' when it means only that the analyte is commonly measured.
  \item \textbf{CDC:} The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention provides
  public-health surveillance, laboratory science, technical resources,
  standardization and quality-assurance programmes, and clinical-laboratory
  guidance. CDC information is valuable evidence, but CDC is not a substitute
  for FDA device authorization, and CDC guidance is not automatically a
  patient-specific screening recommendation.
  \item \textbf{CMS and CLIA:} In the United States, the Centers for Medicare
  \& Medicaid Services administers the Clinical Laboratory Improvement
  Amendments programme, which sets quality standards for laboratories testing
  human specimens. CLIA concerns laboratory quality and competence; it is not
  the same as FDA clearance of a test kit.
  \item \textbf{WHO and professional guideline bodies:} WHO develops
  international technical guidance and laboratory-quality frameworks.
  Organizations such as the ADA, AHA, USPSTF, Endocrine Society, and specialty
  colleges may publish evidence-based recommendations. These recommendations
  help answer whether and when to order a test, but they are not product
  regulators.
  \item \textbf{MHRA and the EU IVDR:} For UK and European-market claims,
  distinguish MHRA requirements in Great Britain from the European Union's
  In-Vitro Diagnostic Medical Devices Regulation (IVDR). Market-access rules,
  conformity assessment, notified bodies, and post-market surveillance are
  separate from clinical guidelines and from the reference interval printed by
  an individual laboratory.
\end{itemize}

\subsection*{Editorial rules adopted for this report}

For each test, the report should use government and professional sources for
clinical meaning, laboratory standards for analytical quality, and regulatory
sources only for claims about devices, authorization, licensing, or market
access. It should identify the specimen, method when relevant, intended use,
laboratory reference interval, and date of the source. It should not claim that
NMC, CDC, NABL, WHO, FDA, or a medical council has endorsed a particular
100-test package unless a source explicitly says so. FDA clearance or CDSCO
authorization supports a product's regulated intended use; it does not mean the
test is indicated for everyone. NABL or ISO 15189 accreditation supports a
laboratory quality system; it does not make all results normal or clinically
actionable. These distinctions are retained in the interpretation and
disclaimer sections of this draft.

Key official references for this section are the
\href{https://www.cdsco.gov.in/opencms/opencms/en/Medical-Device-Diagnostics/InVitro-Diagnostics/}{CDSCO IVD page},
\href{https://nabl-india.org/}{NABL medical-laboratory accreditation information},
\href{https://www.nmc.org.in/about-nmc/functions-of-nmc/}{NMC functions},
\href{https://www.fda.gov/medical-devices/products-and-medical-procedures/in-vitro-diagnostics}{FDA IVD information},
\href{https://www.cdc.gov/clia/index.html}{CDC CLIA and laboratory-quality information},
\href{https://www.cms.gov/medicare/quality/clinical-laboratory-improvement-amendments}{CMS CLIA information},
\href{https://www.who.int/teams/regulation-prequalification/quality-assurance-of-in-vitro-diagnostics}{WHO laboratory-quality guidance},
\href{https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/in-vitro-diagnostic-medical-devices-guidance-on-legislation}{MHRA IVD guidance},
and \href{https://health.ec.europa.eu/medical-devices-vitro-diagnostics_en}{European Commission IVDR information}.

\section{Reliable Sources Used}

The clinical explanations in this draft are based on government, regulatory,
national-health, and peer-reviewed medical references. Commercial laboratory
pages are not used as evidence for medical claims, reference ranges, symptoms,
or treatment decisions.

\sloppy
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*]
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus Medical Tests:} Overview pages for blood testing, complete blood count, metabolic panels, liver tests, kidney tests, thyroid tests, cholesterol, electrolytes, enzymes, and related investigations. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, How to Understand Your Lab Results:} Explains why reference intervals differ by laboratory, method, age, and population. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/how-to-understand-your-lab-results/}
  \item \textbf{Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), A1C Test:} Provides HbA1c interpretation for normal results, prediabetes, diabetes, estimated average glucose, preparation, goals, and factors that affect accuracy. \url{https://www.cdc.gov/diabetes/diabetes-testing/prediabetes-a1c-test.html}
  \item \textbf{National Institutes of Health Office of Dietary Supplements, Vitamin D Fact Sheet:} Describes 25-hydroxyvitamin D as the principal status marker, unit conversion, deficiency and adequacy ranges, assay variation, and potential toxicity at high concentrations. \url{https://ods.od.nih.gov/factsheets/VITAMIND/HealthProfessional/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Iron Tests:} Explains serum iron, transferrin, TIBC, ferritin, preparation, and why iron results should be interpreted together. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/iron-tests/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, FSH and LH Levels Tests:} Describes the roles of FSH and LH in reproduction, cycle- and age-dependent interpretation, preparation, and common causes of low or high results. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/follicle-stimulating-hormone-fsh-levels-test/}
  \item \textbf{National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases (NIDDK), Chronic Kidney Disease Tests and Diagnosis:} Describes eGFR, creatinine, urine albumin, UACR, and kidney-disease thresholds. \url{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/kidney-disease/chronic-kidney-disease-ckd/tests-diagnosis}
  \item \textbf{NIDDK, Identify and Evaluate Patients with Chronic Kidney Disease:} Describes the use of persistent eGFR reduction and albuminuria as key markers and identifies diabetes and hypertension as important risk factors. \url{https://www.niddk.nih.gov/health-information/professionals/clinical-tools-patient-management/kidney-disease/identify-manage-patients/evaluate-ckd}
  \item \textbf{National Heart, Lung, and Blood Institute (NHLBI), Blood Cholesterol Diagnosis:} Describes lipid profiles, LDL, HDL, triglycerides, non-HDL cholesterol, fasting considerations, and risk-based interpretation. \url{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/blood-cholesterol/diagnosis}
  \item \textbf{American Heart Association, ApoB: Another Look at Heart Disease Risk:} Explains ApoB as an estimate of the number of atherogenic particles and discusses its usefulness when triglycerides, diabetes, or metabolic risk make LDL-C less complete. \url{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/how-to-get-your-cholesterol-tested/apolipoprotein-b}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Apolipoprotein B100:} Describes the ApoB blood test, preparation considerations, laboratory variation, and its relationship to lipid disorders and cardiovascular risk. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/ency/article/003502.htm}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, C-Reactive Protein Test:} Distinguishes standard CRP from hs-CRP and explains that hs-CRP may estimate heart-disease risk but does not identify the cause of inflammation. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/c-reactive-protein-crp-test/}
  \item \textbf{American Heart Association, hsCRP Test:} Describes hs-CRP as one component of cardiovascular-risk assessment and recommends clinical context and repeat assessment when inflammation may be transient. \url{https://www.heart.org/en/health-topics/cholesterol/how-to-get-your-cholesterol-tested/high-sensitivity-c-reactive-protein}
  \item \textbf{Merck Manual Consumer Version, Basophilic Disorders:} Describes the role of basophils, common causes of high and low counts, symptoms, and the fact that basophilia is often found incidentally. \url{https://www.merckmanuals.com/home/blood-disorders/white-blood-cell-disorders/basophilic-disorders}
  \item \textbf{Merck Manual Professional Edition, Laboratory Reference Ranges:} Explains that intervals vary with the laboratory method, population, age, sex, and other patient factors. \url{https://www.merckmanuals.com/professional/resources/normal-laboratory-values/laboratory-reference-ranges}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Troponin Test:} Explains cardiac troponin, serial testing, myocardial injury, and the need to interpret results with symptoms and other tests. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/troponin-test/}
  \item \textbf{NHLBI, Heart Attack Diagnosis:} Describes ECG, cardiac biomarkers, imaging, and the clinical diagnosis of myocardial infarction. \url{https://www.nhlbi.nih.gov/health/heart-attack/diagnosis}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Creatine Kinase:} Describes CK testing, muscle injury, rhabdomyolysis, and factors that can change CK results. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/creatine-kinase/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Natriuretic Peptide Tests:} Describes BNP and NT-proBNP, their use in suspected heart failure, and factors that affect interpretation. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/natriuretic-peptide-tests-bnp-nt-probnp/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Immunoglobulins Blood Test:} Describes IgG, IgA, IgM, their roles, common causes of abnormal results, and the need for additional testing. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/immunoglobulins-blood-test/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Complement Blood Test:} Describes C3 and C4 testing, complement consumption, autoimmune disease, and infection-related evaluation. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/complement-blood-test/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, ANA Test:} Explains ANA screening, positive and negative results, symptoms that prompt testing, and why a positive result does not establish a diagnosis. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/ana-antinuclear-antibody-test/}
  \item \textbf{MedlinePlus, Rheumatoid Factor Test and Rheumatoid Arthritis:} Explains the limits of RF and the role of RF, anti-CCP, inflammation tests, and imaging in rheumatoid arthritis evaluation. \url{https://medlineplus.gov/lab-tests/rheumatoid-factor-rf-test/}
  \item \textbf{CDC, Getting Tested for HIV:} Describes laboratory antigen/antibody tests, nucleic-acid tests, window periods, and follow-up after a reactive or recent-exposure result. \url{https://www.cdc.gov/hiv/testing/index.html}
  \item \textbf{CDC, Hepatitis B Diagnosis and Testing:} Describes the three-test hepatitis B serologic panel and the interpretation of HBsAg, anti-HBc, and anti-HBs. \url{https://www.cdc.gov/hepatitis-b/hcp/diagnosis-testing/index.html}
\end{itemize}

These sources support general education, not individualized diagnosis. A result
should be interpreted using the reference interval printed by the performing
laboratory and reviewed with a qualified healthcare professional.
\fussy

\section{Disclaimer}
This educational report is not a substitute for professional medical advice,
diagnosis, or treatment. Reference intervals and decision limits must be taken
from the performing laboratory and interpreted by a qualified healthcare
professional.
